How To Watch The Social Reckoning At Home

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"The Social Reckoning" is finally hitting the home market. It will become available to buy or rent on digital starting November 24, 2026, prior to its physical media release on January 12, 2027.

In case you're unfamiliar with "The Social Reckoning" writ large, here's the gist. Almost two decades after he won an Academy Award for writing the screenplay for "The Social Network," a 2010 dramatization of the founding of Facebook directed by David Fincher, Aaron Sorkin decided to make a sequel that focuses on what Facebook has become in the 2020s. Jesse Eisenberg, who earned an Oscar nod for playing Facebook founder Mark Zuckerberg in "The Social Network," declined to return to the role and was replaced by "Succession" veteran, Emmy and Tony winner, and Sorkin's "The Trial of the Chicago 7" collaborator Jeremy Strong. With Mikey Madison, Jeremy Allen White, Bill Burr, Wunmi Mosaku, Betty Gilpin, and Billy Magnussen starring alongside Strong, the project seemed almost too big to fail before it premiered in theaters on October 9, 2026.

Fail it did, though. Though it reportedly only lost a couple million for Sony (per Deadline), the film absolutely underperformed at the box office, and its rating on Rotten Tomatoes is a middling-to-bleak 54%. So what happened here? Well, from my perspective, "The Social Reckoning" is just a bad movie ... and /Film's chief film critic Chris Evangelista agrees with me. What went wrong as Sorkin tried to make a legacy sequel to one of the best films of the 21st century? Quite a lot actually!

I'll circle back to the "Social Network" of it all shortly, but the bottom line here is that "The Social Reckoning" just ... stinks to high heaven. From the embarrassing prologue featuring Portia Doubleday as a caricature named Kayleigh (I'm not kidding) to the fact that Aaron Sorkin keeps forcing Oscar winner Mikey Madison into outfits that would make her title character from "Anora" blush (in the name of corporate espionage), I found "The Social Network" to be a uniformly dreadful watch from start to finish. Not only is the script clunky as all hell — unfortunately, an ongoing problem with many of Sorkin's recent screenplays — but the story is attempting to make some sort of larger point that doesn't just fall flat but thuds directly to the floor. We already know social media makes everyone angry and insane, and we also know that social media has contributed to right-wing radicalization. Sorkin making a movie that beats us over the head with these two ideas and also ends in a full recreation of the January 6, 2021 insurrection is just as bad as it sounds in theory.

It's also incredible to me that Sorkin managed to cast a lot of great actors and coach them into giving some of the worst performances of their careers. Madison is an enigmatic and compelling performer, and Sorkin transforms her into the robotic Facebook whistleblower Frances Haugen, a deeply unlikable person who doesn't seem to understand human behavior. Jeremy Allen White's journalist Jeff Horwitz is nothing but an exposition machine, and Jeremy Strong's Mark Zuckerberg has no interiority and sounds like Kermit the Frog. The only person who's good in this movie is Bill Burr. Think about that for a second.

"The Social Network" might be one of my very favorite movies of all time, but I can tell you, from a completely unbiased perspective, that "The Social Reckoning" is a wholly undeserving sequel to that 2010 masterpiece. It's clear now, with plenty of hindsight and movies like this, that "The Social Network" was good because of director David Fincher and despite Sorkin. To be fair to Sorkin, he basically admitted that to The Hollywood Reporter after "The Social Reckoning" got soundly panned.

"I would have rather David Fincher directed it — I would rather David Fincher direct everything — but David has an exclusive deal at Netflix, and this was owned by Sony," Sorkin told the outlet on their "Awards Chatter" podcast. Not only did Sorkin also admit that he's "very worried that the parts of directing that [he's] not good at are those parts that you can't learn," he also said, "I was surprised by how little of the science of filmmaking I'd managed to absorb [from other directors] ... I have no visual sensibility, it turns out."

I have to agree with him. Despite the same cinematographer — Jeff Cronenweth — working on both "Network" and "Reckoning," the sequel is visually bland, unimaginatively shot, and even recycles a couple reaction and establishing shots throughout its run time. Basically, "The Social Reckoning" is a poorly made film that I cannot in good conscience recommend; in fact, I'd recommend you just watch "The Social Network" instead. But if you're still interested, you can check out "The Social Reckoning" at home once it's available.

Wales v Portugal LIVE: Visitors continue life without Cristiano Ronaldo in Nations League decider

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Portugal suffered a 4-1 defeat to Denmark in their first match since Ronaldo’s retirement at the age of 41 last month

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Portugal aim to secure their first victory since the retirement of captain Cristiano Ronaldo as they visit Wales in the final round of the Nations League.

Portugal suffered a humiliating 4-1 home defeat to Denmark as they paid tribute to their record goalscorer on Saturday, which also meant they missed the chance to secure top spot in their Nations League group.

Portugal will ensure top spot if they avoid defeat in Cardiff, with Denmark needing to beat Norway in the other game in Group A4 to have any chance of overtaking them. Wales will finish bottom after losing 3-1 to Norway last time out.

Craig Bellamy’s side are safe from direct relegation from League A and will instead enter the relegation/promotion play-offs in March regardless of tonight’s result, but will be playing for pride as they seek to end the Nations League on a winning note.

Follow updates from Wales v Portugal below

Portugal's first match since Cristiano Ronaldo's international retirement ended in a ​crushing 4-1 home defeat by Denmark in the Nations League, as a night of tributes in Braga turned sour for Jorge Jesus and his players.

In front of a packed stadium, Portugal's players wore shirts bearing Ronaldo's name and number seven - which has been subject to a redesign to create a lasting legacy for the former captain - during the national anthem.

Supporters unfurled a banner reading "Obrigado, capi7ão", translated to "Thank you, captain", alongside ​images ⁠of him lifting the European Championship and Nations League trophies.

But celebration turned into humiliation as Denmark scored four. “Practically everything went wrong for us... It’s actually hard to find an explanation," midfielder Joao Palhinha told Portuguese broadcaster Sport TV.

Wales will be without Neco Williams after the right-back picked up an injury in the defeat in Norway, while goalkeeper Karl Darlow is a doubt. Key midfielder Ethan Ampadu is suspended after picking up a second booking against Norway. Centre-back Ben Cabango and winger Sorba Thomas will be available after missing out in Oslo through suspension.

There is pride on the line for Wales in Cardiff this evening as they prepare to host Portugal to conclude their Nations League campaign.

Craig Bellamy’s side know they will be finishing bottom of Group A4, following Saturday’s 3-1 defeat to Norway in Oslo.

But they can begin to prepare for a Nations League relegation play-off in March as they host one of the world’s best sides in Portugal.

The visitors started life without Cristiano Ronaldo in the worst possible way on Saturday, losing 4-1 to Denmark.

The Garmin Instinct 3 Is 44% Off During Amazon’s Early Black Friday Sale

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

For adventurers and outdoor enthusiasts who want more than the average smartwatch, Garmin sets the standard with its industry-leading features and durability. But while the brand is on many wishlists, its high price tags stop many shoppers from making the splurge. Fortunately, with Amazon’s early Black Friday sale, you can get one of its bestsellers, the 44mm Garmin Instinct 3 AMOLED, for 44% off at $249.99 (originally $449.99).

The 1.2-inch color AMOLED display makes the screen more readable in bright daylight conditions, and battery life is best-in-class, lasting up to 18 days (even longer when you’re not using it regularly), which reduces the need for weekly charging, a must for those going off-grid. Durability is also a major perk, with features like a metal-reinforced bezel, chemically strengthened glass, and a 10 ATM water rating, which makes it suitable for underwater activities.

It comes with a built-in flashlight and multi-band GPS, supported by Garmin’s SatIQ technology to better balance location accuracy and battery use, while tracking features include heart rate, sleep, blood oxygen, and other wellness metrics. There’s no shortage of training tools for workouts and outdoor activities, and like regular smartwatches, it displays alerts like texts and emails when paired with an iPhone or Android. The Garmin Pay feature is another bonus for those who don’t want to have their wallet on them at all times.

If you want to log your activities, whether that’s skiing the slopes, heading out on a long trail run, or going on a camping trip in the woods, the 44mm Garmin Instinct 3 AMOLED is a rugged option with plenty of useful tools for those who love the outdoors. And you can’t forget its wildly impressive battery life. At $200 off, it’s a powerful entry into the Garmin world that can accomplish a lot and won’t set you back nearly as much as the brand’s more loaded watches.

The 31 Best Gifts for the Snobbiest People You Know

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

We found the stuff they don’t already own.

This is not meant to sound like a brag, but it inevitably will. Tons of luxurious products come across our desks; as such, I’ve developed especially discerning taste. I’ve tested more than my fair share of $500 colognes, $700 headphones, and $1,000 espresso machines over the years. Not that luxury is synonymous with price, but you know. While these occupational hazards might make me a challenge to shop for, they also give me a discerning eye for gifts that will actually impress my fellow snobs.

If they’re a coffee connoisseur who only drinks single-origin, always espresso consider upgrading their at-home setup with Meraki’s dual boiler espresso machine. For the audiophile whose vinyl collection rivals that of an actual record store, a Fluance turntable will do their records justice. And if their vanity would put a Saks to shame, an under-the-radar Creed fragrance and an award-winning red light mask are worthy additions.

The last thing they want is a bad interface or those ugly Alexa models. Thankfully, 2026 is the year Apple entered the home display market.

My brother has the most extensive at-home espresso setup I’ve ever seen, and he swears by this tamper. It’s spring-loaded to deliver consistent pressure, with an integrated lever for perfectly even pucks. If your espresso pulls aren’t flawless after this, time to look inward.

For a gift that will impress even the most particular of coffee connoisseurs, go with Meraki’s second-gen espresso machine. Featuring a built-in grinder, scales, and a dual boiler system, it’s one of the most premium machines money can buy.

The ultimate snob’s wine opener. A needle and a bit of fancy pump work allow you to pour a glass without ever uncorking the bottle. Great for by-the-glass or sparing yourself any cork emergencies with older vintages.

Take it from someone who actually cooks—the home chef in your life doesn’t want gimmicky gadgets. Instead, gift them practical, high-quality cookware they’ll actually use, like this editor favorite stainless steel frying pan from All-Clad’s D3 line.

For the foodie who worships at the altar of Bourdain, consider gifting them his exact chef’s knife of choice. The Japanese-made Global G-2 has sleek all-metal construction, with a textured handle for a nonslip grip. What’s more, it can be custom-engraved with their name or initials.

For the giftee who’s always complaining that their matcha isn’t the right color (guilty), consider this tea kit. It has everything they need for the perfect cup at home, including a sifter, measuring spoon, whisk, and ceremonial-grade matcha.

Dubbed our top pick for audiophiles, this turntable has it all: consistently great audio quality, a built-in speed control mechanism, and an attractive wood finish.

Aesthetics and audio quality don’t always go hand-in-hand for headphones, which is precisely why this Bang & Olufsen pair is a standout. Our editors named it the best pair of noise-canceling headphones for sound quality, and it’s easily the most attractive model on the list.

If they’ve ever slept at one of the world’s best hotels, they have slept on Frette sheets, worn a Frette robe. Give them that luxury at home.

Every chess player I’ve ever met takes the game extremely seriously, and this is a set they’d be proud to display on their coffee table. Bonus: You can monogram it for a personal touch.

The best home gifts not only spark conversation, but also serve a purpose in their space. Pair this Greek mythology-inspired incense holder with some great-smelling incense (I’m partial to Le Labo), and you have an infallible gift for the artsy types on your list.

All the interior design obsessives I know in Brooklyn (myself included) are obsessed with this lighting brand, which draws from Bauhaus and mid-century modern influences. This retro, saucer-shaped table lamp is a foolproof pick for the giftee who wouldn’t be caught dead turning on “the big light.”

I have to hand it to the cinephiles here: Dolby Atmos is truly superior for watching movies (sorry, films) at home. This Sonos soundbar offers unmatched 3D surround-sound in a sleek package.

A candle is only “impersonal” if it’s ugly or smells bad. Loewe’s incense candle is beautiful, and it smells divine. No snob will ever turn one down.

Sure, Aventus might be Creed’s most popular cologne, but the fragrance snob you know doesn’t want to smell like every other guy. Instead, opt for one of the brand’s under-the-radar scents, like Bois du Portugal. Its notes of lavender, sandalwood, and cedar offer an aromatic scent reminiscent of a vintage barbershop.

Ah, the status hand soap. A gift that works for virtually anyone, especially those with discerning taste. This pick from Nécessaire somehow feels less sceney than other options on the market, and it’s infused with skin-softening ingredients like vitamin B3 and linoleic acid.

As a fragrance expert who tests new scents for a living, few earn me as many compliments as Althair from Parfums de Marly. It’s a grown-up vanilla, with notes of orange blossom, cinnamon, and a dose of smoky guaiac wood to keep it from going saccharine. Although it’s technically from the men’s line, its not-too-sweet gourmand scent profile is truly genderless.

The iRestore red light face mask just won a 2026 Gadget Award. Enough said.

Their parched, damaged strands deserve nothing but the best; in this case, it’s Kérastase’s Oléo-Relax hair mask. It’s got a ton of hydrating ingredients—rose oil, coconut oil, and shorea oil—to replenish dry hair. Not to mention, it smells damn good.

La redazione di Tuttomercatoweb.com ha stilato una classifica dei 5 giocatori che militano in Serie A che hanno aumentato maggiormente il loro valore dall'estate a oggi. Al primo posto di questa speciale classifica c'è Alphadjo Cisse del Milan, seguito da Gustavo Varela del Monza, da Davide Frattesi della Lazio e da Antonio Raimondo e Giorgi Kvernadze, entrambi del Frosinone.

Scrive TMW sul trequartista rossonero Cisse: "La vera sorpresa in casa Milan è l'ex Catanzaro. Acquistato a inizio 2025 per 10 milioni di euro complessivi, è diventato l'uomo copertina della squadra di Ruben Amorim fin dall'esordio contro il Torino, affermandosi anche in Europa League: 8 gol in 11 gare con il Diavolo bastano e avanzano per attirare l'attenzione delle squadre di Premier League, tra cui il Brighton. Building the Agent Host Protocol for sessions that continue across devices

Discover how we built the open Agent Host Protocol to keep your agent sessions accessible across editors, apps, phones, and the cloud.

Models are now good enough to work for hours or even days on their own. You don’t have to watch over every thing they do, but you still need to be able to check in, no matter where you are. An agent can’t live only inside one application. The work should continue even If you close a window or switch devices.

In GitHub Copilot you can start an agent session in one place and pick it up in another. This session continuity runs on the Agent Host Protocol (AHP), an open standard built first in VS Code and adopted across GitHub. Here’s how it works, what we learned building it, and how you can use it to bring your agents into your own apps and workflows.

GitHub Copilot was first built in VS Code as an extension that bundled its own harness. This gave you access to your agents in VS Code, but it tied their lifetime to the editor. Extensions are attached to a single VS Code window. Close that window, and the agent is gone. Update VS Code, and every agent stops as the application restarts. Connect to a remote machine, and you get an entirely new extension host commit-locked against the version of the VS Code workbench you have.

At the same time, the Copilot app was being developed, alongside the existing GitHub mobile apps for iOS and Android. Each product was doing its own distinct integration with cloud-based agents to work towards delivering session continuity.

Each of these integrations solved the problem for one product, but developers also still want to be able to pull their agents into their own workflows–whether that’s an editor, a terminal, a phone, or a tool they built themselves–and they can’t do that if every client needs its own one-off integration. That’s why developers needed a protocol: a standard, independently versioned language across versions of VS Code, the GitHub Copilot app, mobile apps, and any other client that might host an agent. A shared protocol means every client sees the same agents, history, and state without bespoke integrations, and you aren’t tied to one app’s idea of what an agent session is.

The protocol needed to allow multiple clients to interact with the same set of agents seamlessly. Start a session in the cloud from your phone, for example, then continue it from your laptop. It should tolerate network interruptions, reconnections, and concurrent client mutations. And it needed to be full-featured enough for you to do real work from each of your clients.

At the same time, the protocol needed to stay out of agent behavior. Unlike the domains that produced the Language Server Protocol and Debug Adapter Protocol, the world of agents is evolving quickly. That’s why AHP avoids encoding how agents work, and instead focuses on how you want to interact with them.

You also need the protocol to be simple enough to implement on your own client or server. While the full feature set would necessarily be extensive, the core protocol needed to be minimal.

The result of these requirements is the Agent Host Protocol (AHP). It is oriented around URI-identified “channels”, which are the coarsest unit of functionality in the protocol. Every server must implement the root, session, and chat channels, but can also implement more channels that provide terminal support, MCP additions, or OTel reporting, for example.

Within each channel is a reducer-based state object, which drives most of the logic for clients. The server is the arbiter of the state, and each client sends a sequence of actions that either mutate the state or trigger side effects, like sending a chat message. This provides a few very nice properties. Reconnection and replay in poor network conditions are easy: a client sends all queued actions, presents the right ‘loading’ state, and waits for the server to reconcile and acknowledge the actions before confirming them. Clients and servers are straightforward to debug. And reducer-based state also plays nicely with popular UI frameworks like React.

Adopting the Agent Host Protocol in VS Code required building a new harness and agentic stack underneath it. The Copilot Chat extension, which used to provide all of VS Code’s AI and agentic capabilities, had been growing for over three years, reaching over 400,000 lines of TypeScript, not counting tests. But, as a VS Code extension, it was structured around the lifecycle of a VS Code window and depended entirely on the VS Code extension API. Neither that lifetime nor those APIs would be available in a standalone agent host process.

As we started building the Agent Host Protocol in early 2026, the Copilot SDK was also being developed, and it made sense to leverage it as the foundation for the new agentic stack. This was a big transition. It’s easy to build a harness implementation that says hello and can edit files—we were using the agent host to develop the agent host after the first couple weeks—but it’s another thing to build one that covers the long tail of features developers had come to expect from Copilot Chat.

GitHub has had Copilot cloud agents for a couple of years, allowing developers to run tasks in the cloud from any device. While VS Code was quietly developing the Agent Host Protocol, GitHub was looking to expand that capability to coordinate sessions running on your own devices via the Copilot app, or in cloud sandboxes. This was exactly the N:M problem that AHP was built to solve, so adopting the same technology made perfect sense.

From the outset, the GitHub Agent Host had different requirements than VS Code’s implementation. VS Code runs the host locally and can drop its host on other compute over SSH, containers, WSL, or Dev Tunnels, and it is actually included as part of the VS Code Server bundle. The GitHub Agent Host needed to run in the cloud and across a much wider variety of infrastructure, environments, and security constraints.

Those differing requirements are why they’re separate implementations written in different languages: VS Code’s in TypeScript and GitHub’s in Rust. For now, having two agent hosts has been a good acid-test for the protocol itself. How well does it work when two entirely different teams and sets of agents implement the same protocol independently?

Pretty well, it turns out. Like MCP, the Agent Host Protocol provides escape hatches via custom methods and _metadata on objects that consumers can use for out-of-band signaling. After several months heads-down on our respective implementations, our first task as we worked toward unification was inventorying the ways each implementation had extended the protocol. Many of these extensions reasonably stay GitHub-specific, such as the GitHub Agent Host using metadata to link to project boards. In other cases, variance was a sign of a gap in the protocol; early versions, for example, could not accurately represent shell-type tool output that wasn’t backed by a PTY. And some were bad assumptions, like VS Code’s client assuming a particular structure for the session URIs it received from the agent host. A protocol with only one implementation isn’t truly portable; as we taught our clients and servers to talk to each other, AHP became portable in practice, not just in theory.

Today, the Agent Host Protocol provides that same continuity across all VS Code and GitHub surfaces. Agent sessions running locally in the GitHub Copilot app, in cloud sandboxes, and in VS Code all share the same underlying protocol. To see how it’s used, read about how you can work from anywhere with the new connected Copilot cloud experience.

AHP was designed to be easy to use in clients and servers. Agent Host Protocol libraries are available in Rust, TypeScript, Kotlin, Swift, .NET, and Go, and agents are able to build clients and servers with little prompting. I have a macropad sitting on my desk that reflects my agent’s state in real time, and others have built everything from smart watch apps to Kanban-style task management tools against the protocol. Whether you want a companion app for your own agents, a way to bring agent sessions into your team’s tools, or AHP support in your own editor or agent, the protocol is open for you to build on.

You can connect to an agent host you implement in the Copilot app (enable Experimental > Direct Connections, then go to Environments > Add Agent Host Protocol Host) and in VS Code (via Add Remote Agent Host in the Agents window). You can spawn a standalone version of VS Code’s agent host by running code agent host in your terminal, or see the endpoints for the one in your editor by running code agent endpoints, and samples can help you and your agent get started.

The Agent Host Protocol is an open standard, and questions and suggestions are welcome on GitHub.

Explore the Agent Host Protocol on GitHub >

OpenAI releases GPT-6.2 Astra to regain performance lead from Claude Fable 5.2

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The frontier model races continues. A week after the launch of Anthropic’s Claude 5.2 and Google’s Gemini 4 Hydrogen, OpenAI is releasing GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol as a “new standard for professional work.”

Stepping back in time a few days, Anthropic last week launched Claude Fable and Mythos 5.2. The company’s latest models are “designed to tackle the most daunting technical challenges with “improvements in areas like coding, reasoning, and vision.” Anthropic says that Fable 5.2 beats Opus 5.5, GPT-6 Astra, and Gemini 4 Carbon virtually across the board in benchmarks including coding, cybersecurity, knowledge work, and more.

But, now, OpenAI is stepping up to the challenge.

OpenAI shows that GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol are more or less playing ball with Anthropic’s latest models, but often delivering similar performance at a drastically lower cost.

Of the two new models, OpenAI says that Sol is “smarter, more efficient, and lets you complete more tasks on the same paid plan” as a “go-to model for everyday work.”

Astra, meanwhile, is tuned for the highest-quality work in professional use cases such as documents, slides, coding, and more:

GPT‑6.2 Astra sets a new quality bar for AI in complex professional work. Its slides, spreadsheets, and documents read less like AI, and more closely follow the templates you provide and the conventions of your field. It writes cleaner code and builds frontends that look and work better, particularly for websites, gaming, and 3D design.

GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol are now available to all Plus, Pro, Enterprise, and Edu users in ChatGPT Work and Codex, with Astra being significantly more affordable compared to GPT-6 Astra. Pricing for Astra per million tokens lands at $8 for input, $0.40 for cached input, and $40 for output. Sol matches its predecessor at $2 for input, $0.10 for cached input, and $10 for output, again per million tokens.

EXCLUSIVE: Kylie Jenner’s $38 Million Concrete Mansion Is Back on the Market With a Glamorous New Look

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Kylie Jenner has relisted her concrete fortress in Los Angeles for $38 million after giving the property a complete overhaul.

The reality TV personality, 29, initially put the seven-bedroom Holmby Hills property on the market on Dec. 22, according to property records, just over five years after she snapped it up for $36.5 million in 2020.

The extraordinary dwelling then lingered on the market for several months until June 24, when Realtor.com® exclusively revealed that the property had vanished from the selling block.

At the time, a source close to Jenner suggested to Realtor.com® that the property would be returning to the market. Then, in June, the listing came back with a very steep—and enticing—price cut as well as a sparkling new set of images and a fresh property description. Then, just last month, the home was once again removed from the market.

Now, the property has reappeared on the selling block with a fresh set of listing images—some of which have been polished by AI to show potential buyers what the property could look like.

The home, which is listed with Joshua Altman of Douglas Elliman of California, Inc., is described in its listing as "one of Los Angeles' most prestigious enclaves."

"Set in prime Holmby Hills and spanning approximately 15,227 square feet, this predominantly single-story estate was designed for those who demand it all: remarkable scale, fortress-level privacy, and an A-list lifestyle without ever leaving home," the listing description adds.

Just before entering the home, guests are greeted by 12-foot-tall gates that emphasize the importance of safety and privacy.

The living area comes complete with a cozy fireplace and an expansive view of the backyard, which features a resort-style pool.

The chef's kitchen is equipped with a marble island and seating area, plenty of cabinet space, and state-of-the-art appliances.

The spacious dining area is surrounded by all-white walls and topped by an elegant chandelier.

One of the many entertaining areas features a second bar and a pool table, making it the perfect place to welcome guests.

"Featuring 7 bedrooms, 14 bathrooms, a dedicated guard house, 4-car garage, and 5 additional covered parking spaces, this is the quintessential Holmby Hills trophy estate. A rare offering where grand-scale living, world-class amenities, and absolute discretion converge at one of Los Angeles' most coveted addresses," the listing reads.

Jenner may be trying to replicate the luck she had with her Hidden Hills mansion, which recently sold for $15.3 million.

Jenner, who is currently in a very high-profile relationship with actor Timothee Chalamet, purchased the sprawling 13,200-square-foot Hidden Hills dwelling for $12.05 million in late 2016, when she was just 19. It served as the on-screen star's primary residence for many years.

After listing the home for $20.25 million, Kylie sold it for $15.3 million, Realtor.com® exclusively revealed.

Jenner will not be quitting the Hidden Hills neighborhood—where the majority of her family members also reside—altogether, however. She purchased a huge plot of land in the area for $15 million in 2020, where she spent more than five years building her dream estate.

According to planning applications, Jenner had earmarked the parcel to serve as the location of an enormous 18,000-square-foot property that would offer her additional privacy and more room for her family to enjoy.

Among the features of her newly designed dwelling were a 12-car garage, a guesthouse, a sport court, and a security office, as well as an enormous swimming pool.

As of June 2025, images revealed that work on the project was nearly completed, suggesting that Jenner may have been preparing to move into her new abode full time.

In January, Jenner took her fans on a tour of the nearly completed property, showing off the different spaces and gushing about some of the design details that had been added, including the stone exteriors and the reclaimed wood floors.

On this day in space! Nov. 17, 1967: NASA's Surveyor 6 moon probe makes 1st-ever successful launch and hop from the lunar surface

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Latest space missions, discoveries and exploration updates.

Stargazing tips, cosmic events and expert insights on the universe

Start exploring exclusive deals, expert advice and more

Space news, cosmic updates and expert insights straight to your inbox.

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Sign up to our monthly entertainment newsletter to keep up with all our coverage of the latest sci-fi and space movies, tv shows, games and books.

Discover this week's must-see night sky events, moon phases, and stunning astrophotos. Sign up for our skywatching newsletter and explore the universe with us!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Surveyor 6 showed that spacecraft could land on the moon, and lift off again. The lives of NASA's Apollo astronauts depended on it.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

On Nov. 17, 1967, NASA's Surveyor 6 spacecraft made the first liftoff from the lunar surface.

Surveyor 6 was one of several robotic lunar missions NASA sent to scout the moon before sending humans there. It took television pictures of the surface and learned about the composition of lunar soil. Then came the big liftoff.

First Surveyor 6 launched from the surface and hopped to another position about 8 feet (2 meters) away. Next, Surveyor 6 took pictures of its original landing site. Flight controllers on Earth could see the landing pad prints of Surveyor 6. They also saw an imprint from the rocket exhaust when Surveyor 6 lifted off.

Surveyor 6 showed that spacecraft could land on the moon, and lift off again. Less than two years later, humans did the same thing during Apollo 11.

NASA's Surveyor 6 spacecraft launched to the moon on Nov. 7, 1967 and made its first landing on the lunar surface on Nov. 10 of that year. It's primary mission was to make a soft landing on the moon in the Sinus Medii of the moon.

The probe took nearly 30,000 photos of the moon during its 16 days on the lunar surface, with the lander shutting down due to the onset of lunar night on Nov. 26. Surveyor 6 was the sixth and penultimate moon mission of NASA's Surveyor lunar program, so a lot of what it did (launching and landing on the moon) had been done before.

But a lunar liftoff? That was something new.

"On November 17, as an engineering test to see if its rockets could be restarted on the Moon, Surveyor’s engines fired for 2.5 seconds, lifting the spacecraft 12 feet high and moving it 8 feet west of its original landing site, in the first powered liftoff from another celestial body," NASA wrote in a mission overview.

The engineering test proved that a spacecraft could land on the moon, and then lift off and take flight once more. That capability was a core requirement for NASA's Apollo astronaut moon landings that followed, since astronauts needed a way to return home after visiting the moon.

But there was critical science Surveyor 6 was able to do with the lunar hop.

"This maneuver provided excellent views of the surface disturbances produced by the initial landing and the effects of firing rocket engines close to the lunar surface," scientists with the Lunar and Planetary Institute wrote in a summary. "Photography obtained after the hop contributed to the soil mechanics investigation."

Surveyor 6 also had a bar magnet on its footpad that allowed scientists to test how magnetic the lunar regolith was at the landing site.

Want more space history? Check out our full On This Day In Space Story archive and watch our On This Day In Space videos on YouTube.

On this day in space! Nov. 16, 1974: The giant Arecibo Observatory in Puerto Rico broadcasts humanity's 1st interstellar message

As an editor with over 10 years of experience in science journalism she has previously written for Scholastic Classroom Magazines, MedPage Today and The Joint Institute for Computational Sciences at Oak Ridge National Laboratory. After studying physics at the University of Tennessee in her hometown of Knoxville, she earned her graduate degree in Science, Health and Environmental Reporting (SHERP) from New York University. Hanneke joined the Space.com team in 2016 as a staff writer and producer, covering topics including spaceflight and astronomy. She currently lives in Seattle, home of the Space Needle, with her cat and two snakes. In her spare time, Hanneke enjoys exploring the Rocky Mountains, basking in nature and looking for dark skies to gaze at the cosmos.

‘Hershey’ Review: Finn Wittrock Plays the American Chocolate Titan in a Sickly Sweet Biopic

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Alexandra Daddario, Alan Ruck and Richard Kind co-star in this film about Milton Hershey's rise to prominence, directed by Mark Waters ('Freaky Friday,' 'Mean Girls').

The new film about the founder of the Hershey Company begins with a loving, beautifully lit close-up of one of the company’s signature products, a Hershey’s Kiss. That tells you pretty much everything you need to know about this effort produced in part by Hershey Entities and distributed by Angel Studios. Starring Finn Wittrock (American Horror Story) in the title role, Hershey is a blandly sanitized biopic that’s as sickly sweet as the candies the company sells. Being released for Thanksgiving, it essentially serves as a feature-length product placement.

The film, which an opening title card informs us is “based on the unlikely true story,” begins in 1870 with a young Milton (James Kelly) growing up in a Mennonite community in rural Pennsylvania. After the tragic death of his younger sister and the abandonment of the family by his dreamer father (Alan Ruck), he begins working for a kindly confectioner, Joseph Royer (Richard Kind, at his most twinkly), who teaches him candy-making methods. Related Stories TV AMC's 'Mayfair Witches: Salem Coven' Gets December Debut as Catherine Zeta-Jones, Carrie-Anne Moss Tease 'Kill Jackie' and 'Yaga' at NYCC TV Angel Studios' 'Homestead: The Series' Sets Season 2 Premiere Date and Cast (Exclusive) Hershey The Bottom Line Will give you cavities. Release date: Wednesday, November 25Cast: Finn Wittrock, Alexandra Daddario, Alan Ruck, Richard Kind, David Costabile, James Kelly, Michael Moreland Milligan, Daniel David Stewart, Joel Brady, Francesca Faridany, Dina Spybey-Waters, John Hans Tester, Heléne Yorke, Mark Ashworth, Isabel Van NattaDirector: Mark WatersScreenwriters: Sharon Paul, Timothy Michael Hayes Rated PG, 1 hour 50 minutes

Milton is soon inventing original candies and eventually opens his own shop specializing in (oh, the irony) caramels. The shop fails but the young entrepreneur is undeterred, and he tries again with a store in Lancaster, Pennsylvania, that takes off after a bulk order from a visiting Englishman.

The episodic screenplay by Sharon Paul and Timothy Michael Hayes chronicles Hershey’s rise to prominence in dutiful fashion, including his eventual transition to producing chocolates. This comes about after a visit from a salesman representing a German chocolate company, who, after being rejected by Hershey because the product tastes bitter, warns him, “I think you’re missing out on what’s next.” Sure enough, Hershey changes his mind, realizing that all the chocolate needs is some milk to make it taste better. The rest, as they say, is history.

“Caramels are a fad, but chocolate is permanent,” Hershey confidently tells his associates.

On his way to becoming a chocolate titan, Hershey meets the adorable Kitty (Alexandra Daddario), with their love story fueling much of the film’s second half. He turns the village where he grew up into the company town that still bears his name, treating his employees benevolently (the violent labor disputes that occurred in later years are ignored) and, at the urging of his wife, opening a school for impoverished youth that still exists today. (Left unmentioned is the fact that admission was restricted to whites; Black students weren’t admitted until 1968.)

Another major plot element is Kitty’s serious illness, which prevents her from having children and results in numerous scenes that find her nearly collapsing while Milton looks on concernedly.

Flavorlessly directed by Mark Waters (far past his Freaky Friday and Mean Girls glory days), Hershey never succeeds in coming to life, playing like the sort of candy-coated dramatized film that would find its most receptive audiences at the Hershey Story Museum in Pennsylvania. Every scene plays out exactly as you’d expect, with conflicts resolved serendipitously and Hershey benefiting from fortunate strokes of luck, including a malfunctioning machine producing what became the Hershey’s Kiss and the couple miraculously avoiding a planned voyage on the Titanic (they dance to their good fortune, which, considering what happened to the poor souls onboard, seems a bit tacky).

Wittrock, eventually outfitted with a period-perfect moustache, does a reasonably good job but is ultimately unable to overcome the script’s deficiencies. Daddario fares better, conveying a tender warmth and playfulness that infuse the film with much of its heart. (Strangely, Wittrock eventually receives old age makeup treatment while Daddario looks virtually the same throughout). The supporting players, including David Costabile (Billions) as a composite business competitor, do exactly what’s expected of them.

The film ends in predictable fashion, with vintage photographs of the real-life figures and present-day footage of the school and its grateful students. That’s followed by one of those annoying “Pay It Forward” pitches, gamely delivered by Wittrock and Daddario, urging us to pour more money into the studio’s coffers under the guise of philanthropy.

Jaafar Jackson Shares What Jackson Family Holiday Festivities Are Like

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Jaafar Jackson shared how he celebrates the holidays with his fellow family members during an exclusive interview with E! News at the CCA's 9th Annual Celebration of Black Cinema & Television.

Jaafar Jackson will always remember having a good time around the holidays.

In fact, as the Michael star—son to Jermaine Jackson and Alejandra Genevieve Oaziaza—explained, his family finds more than one way to rock with one another during the festivities.

“It’s a lot of great music playing, there’s a lot of good food,” he exclusively told E! News at the CCA's 9th Annual Celebration of Black Cinema & Television Nov. 16. “We usually love playing some board games. Pictionary is one of our favorites. It can be loud at times, I would say, but it’s great, you know, it’s wonderful.”

Also wonderful, as Jaafar admitted, would be what’s on the table, too. “There’s always some peach cobbler, apple pie,” the 30-year-old noted. “And I usually go it first—to the dessert spread.”

Sweet treats and family gatherings aside, Jafaar—who moonwalked across theater screens earlier this year with his portrayal of his uncle Michael Jackson—is also gearing up to star in the film’s sequel. However, if you’re wondering about all the details to come, you are not alone.

“I haven’t started prepping just yet, it’s so early on—I can’t really say anything,” Jafaar shared. “It’s in the early works so we’ll see as time comes. And I don’t want to give anything away but it’s going to be a very special, incredible journey, I will say.”

But for now, Jafaar is enjoying what the man in the mirror has accomplished thus far with his role.

“It’s so much to take in,” he explained of the response. “I’ve been just overwhelmed with just so much joy and gratitude because it’s been a wonderful experience for me to see how much this film has connected with people.”

“It’s brought back that nostalgia feeling to a lot of people,” Jaafar reflected, “who grew up knowing Michael and experiencing his music and artistry. So, for me to be a part of that and play a hand in bringing forth his music to a new generation, I couldn’t be more grateful for that.”

For a deeper dive into everything the Jackson family has said about the Michael movie so far, keep reading…

As a producer on the film, Michael Jackson's eldest child "was on set every day," lead producer Graham King told The Hollywood Reporter at the film's April 20 Los Angeles premiere.

Prince, who attended premieres in L.A. and Berlin, has also touted his cousin Jaafar Jackson's performance, writing April 11 on Instagram that watching Jermaine Jackson's son "step into the role of his uncle Michael is incredibly special for the family. With his talent, dedication and spirit, he was the only right choice to portray Michael and bring his story to the big screen."

When Colman Domingo, who plays her late grandfather Joe Jackson, said that Prince and Paris were "very much in support" of Michael, Paris begged to differ.

“Don’t be telling people I was ‘helpful’ on the set of a movie I had 0% involvement in lol,” Michael's daughter wrote on her Instagram Story in August 2025. “That is so weird.”

Rather, she continued, "I read one of the first drafts of the script and gave my notes about what was dishonest / didn’t sit right with me and when they didn’t address it I moved on with my life. Not my monkeys not my circus. God bless and god speed.”

She crystalized her take the following month, saying in a series of Instagram videos, "They’re gonna make whatever they’re gonna make. The big reason why I haven’t said anything up until this point is because I know a lot of you guys are gonna be happy with it" because it "panders to a very specific section of my dad’s fandom that still lives in a fantasy."

Hollywood biopics are "sold to you as real," Paris said, but they're "sugar-coated."

Meaning, she alleged, "The narrative is being controlled. There’s a lot of inaccuracy and there’s a lot of full-blown lies. At the end of the day, that doesn’t really fly with me. I don’t really like dishonesty."

Paris also pushed back against accusations that she "hates her dad…because I'm not, like, calling the shots on set being a big-shot producer of a movie that's filled with just inaccuracies. That's not my truth…I just prefer honesty over sales and monetary gain."

As the most private of Michael's kids, Bigi hasn't commented on the film, but he attended the April 10 premiere in Berlin.

“To have my cousins there and my other family members, that meant everything,” Jaafar told E! News April 20. “Because it is a family story, and it all started in Gary, Indiana, so it meant the world to me.”

Michael's sister, the youngest of Joe and Katherine Jackson's 10 kids, asked to be removed from this narrative.

“I wish everybody was in the movie,” sister LaToya Jackson told Variety at the L.A. premiere. But Janet "was asked and she kindly declined so you have to respect her wishes.”

In response to a March Page Six report that Janet had issues with almost every scene in the film and drew Jermaine's ire during a family screening, La Toya told Entertainment Tonight, "There was absolutely no problem whatsoever, none whatsoever. Please believe it."

Director Antoine Fuqua said he has "so much respect and love for Janet," telling Variety she was "supportive of Jaafar, and that's what matters."

Jaafar was "absolutely fantastic," La Toya told E! News at the L.A. premiere. "He just embodied my brother and I'm so proud of him."

The fifth-eldest Jackson sibling considered it "very important" that her family collaborated on the movie, telling THR, "A lot of times people think they know the story and they read about things, but when the family’s involved, the family can say yay or nay."

Jaafar left Marlon "smiling inside at how great he is," the sixth-eldest Jackson sibling told E!, adding that he assured his nephew that his performance was "unbelievable."

And Marlon said he didn't spot any inaccuracies in the film, sharing, "When I first saw him on stage, when I went to the set the first time, they were shooting the [Jacksons' 1984] Victory Tour…And I said, 'Wow.' He looked just like Michael."

Jaafar's father, suffice it to say, was "extremely proud," telling E!, "He deserved the part, he worked so hard."

But before Jaafar took on the role, Jermaine explained to Fab TV at the Berlin premiere, he wanted his son to understand "what he was going up against," because those "are a tough set of shoes to fill."

Michael's nephew Taj Jackson told critics to beat it.

"Sorry, media, you don’t get to control the narrative anymore of who Michael Jackson truly was," the eldest son of late Jackson 5 member Tito Jackson wrote on X April 21, per Variety. "The public gets to watch this movie… they will decide for themselves. And you can’t handle that. Can’t wait until some critics have to eat crow. And yes, I will be that petty."

Tito's youngest son wrote on X April 22 that he was headed out to rewatch Michael, because he "wouldn't miss this opportunity to be masked in the electrifying energy that will be in that theater for nothing. My uncle deserves this, my cousin earned this and his loyal supporters are owed this. The world will be reminded or learn who MJ truly was and I can’t wait!"

Vitoria vs Bragantino - prediction, team news, lineups

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

By Joshua Cole | 17 Nov 2026 18:17

Vitoria will be looking to end a five-match winless run when they welcome Bragantino to the Estadio Manoel Barradas on Thursday in a Campeonato Brasileiro Serie A encounter.

The hosts sit 15th in the table with 38 points from 34 matches, just one point above the relegation zone, while Braga occupy eighth place with 47 points and are hoping to keep their hopes of qualifying for the 2027 Copa Libertadores alive.

Vitoria head into Thursday's encounter under pressure after losing 3-0 at home to Fluminense in their most recent league outing, a result that extended their winless run to five matches.

The Rubro-Negro had previously suffered a 2-0 defeat away to Coritiba, following consecutive 1-1 draws against Athletico Paranaense and Corinthians, while their earlier meeting with Sao Paulo ended in a 2-1 loss.

Their last league victory came on October 8, when they thrashed Chapecoense 4-0 at home, leaving them with only two points from the last five matches, the fewest points by any team in the league in that period.

The defeat to Fluminense also marked a difficult end to Jair Ventura's time in charge, with the coach dismissed after Vitoria slipped to within one point of the relegation zone.

Rafael Lacerda has been appointed and will take charge of his first match on Thursday, having had the international break to work with the squad and prepare for the challenge ahead.

Vitoria's home record offers some encouragement, however, as they have won 10 of their 17 league matches at the Barradao, drawing twice and losing five times, though they are without a win in their last two games as hosts.

Bragantino, meanwhile, arrive in Salvador after recording consecutive victories, having beaten Chapecoense 3-0 before securing a 2-1 home win over Santos in their most recent match.

Massa Bruta took the lead against Santos via an own goal from Rodinei, before conceding an equaliser in the 37th minute, but Wallace Yan scored his third goal in two games to claim all three points.

Vagner Mancini's side have now won three of their last five league matches, drawing once and losing once, with their other victory coming in a 4-2 home success over Cruzeiro, while they recorded a 1-1 draw away to Remo during that period, with their only defeat coming against Palmeiras, who beat them 2-0 in Sao Paulo.

Braga’s away form has been less convincing than their home performances, having won only four of their 17 matches on the road, drawing five and losing eight, while they are without a win in their last eight league games and last 10 across all competitions.

However, the head-to-head record also gives the visitors reason for optimism, having won the last two meetings between the clubs, though the Massa Bruta have lost the last two on the road.

Vitoria will be without several players through injury, including Nathan Mendes, Fabiano Souza, Edu, Camutanga, Ramon and Riccieli, while Dudu is also listed as unavailable.

Luan Candido and Nacho Laquintana are expected to miss out because they are on loan from Bragantino and their agreements reportedly include clauses requiring Vitoria to pay a fee for each player to feature against their parent club.

Emmanuel Britez is available again after serving a suspension, and the defender is expected to partner Caca in central defence.

Lacerda is also likely to retain Lucas Arcanjo in goal, with Rene, Erick and Matheuzinho among the attacking options as he prepares for his first match in charge.

Bragantino will be without Lucas Barbosa, who is suspended, while Rodriguinho and Davi Gomes are sidelined through injury.

Jose Hurtado is also a doubt after picking up a thigh injury, leaving Mancini with some decisions to make in defence as he attempts to maintain his team's recent momentum.

Yan is expected to retain his place in the attacking midfield, while Eduardo Sasha should lead the line.

Not Ribeye: Bobby Flay Recommends This Steak Cut For The Best Tacos

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

When taco night rolls around, you'll want to head to the butcher and grab some good meat. If you're making carne asada, your instinct might be to pick the ribeye. While it might be the gold standard of steak, tacos and ribeye are an inherent mismatch. Tacos are one of the most democratic foods out there, while ribeye is made for deep pockets and expensive tastes. Even Food Network's Bobby Flay recommends skipping it. Instead, he swaps the pricey cut for the seriously underrated skirt steak.

Ribeye is America's favorite steak thanks to its rich marbling and tender texture, but you'll rarely find it in taquerias. Beyond being pricey, ribeye's assets are its downfall in tacos. In an interview with Food and Wine, Flay revealed the reason why he often chooses to skip ribeye altogether: "[Ribeye] can be inconsistent because the fat doesn't always melt even when cooked properly." This is a real problem in tacos. Encased in a taco, you don't have the option to trim around a rubbery pocket of fat or leave it on the plate. Even if you trim the fat before adding the steak to your tacos, it's so unevenly distributed that you risk ending up with wildly inconsistent slices.

Unlike ribeye, skirt is cut from the plate primal, a hard-working area near the diaphragm. Rather than being streaked with generous ribbons of fat, it's comprised of long, coarse muscle fibers, which give it a chewier, more fibrous bite along with a bold, beef-forward flavor. Its thin profile may not win it fans at white tablecloth restaurants, but it's exactly why taquerias can't get enough of it. All that surface area compared to its thickness means skirt steak cooks quickly, soaks up marinades fast, and slices evenly, which is everything you want from taco meat.

There's no cut of beef more well-suited to carne asada than skirt. Literally translating to "grilled meat," the beef is usually marinated in a citrus and garlic blend, with the acidic ingredients helping to tenderize the surface of the meat. After being left to soak up the flavor for a couple of hours, it's then seared or grilled over an open flame or charcoal to impart a smoky, charred flavor (an air fryer also works if you can't grill). Because skirt is such a thin cut, it doesn't enjoy spending too much time over high heat; it only needs around two to four minutes per side. Skirt can be prone to drying out, so it would be wise to avoid cooking past a medium doneness.

How you cut skirt is just as important as how you cook it. Compared to other cuts, skirt has particularly long, dense muscle fibers, and if you slice them with the grain, you'll leave them intact, resulting in chewy, rubbery bites. Slicing against the grain shortens these fibers, turning them into more satisfying, bite-sized strands. To identify the grain, look closely at the surface of the meat to spot the direction of the muscle fibers running through the beef.

Although skirt is beloved by taquerias, that doesn't make it suited to all beef tacos. One popular style where we would skip skirt is birria, the slow-cooked, tender beef marinated in a chili-and-spice adobo served with a flavorful broth for dipping. The whole concept of birria revolves around slow-braising, requiring tough, collagen-rich cuts like chuck or beef cheek, which break down into tender, shreddable meat over several hours. While skirt does have some connective tissue, it's simply too thin and lean to survive hours of prolonged heat.

Rayan to Liverpool or Manchester United? How the Brazilian’s style suits both clubs

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

By Alexis Pereira | 17 Nov 2026 18:16

Rayan is living a fast-moving career in European football. Signed by Bournemouth in January 2026 after standing out at Vasco da Gama, the Brazilian forward quickly won a place in the Premier League and began attracting the attention of bigger clubs. Liverpool are already monitoring his progress, while a statement has put Manchester United in the conversation: the player revealed that he has always supported the Red Devils because of Cristiano Ronaldo.

Andoni Iraola, who worked with Rayan at Bournemouth, took charge of Liverpool at the start of 2026-27. Manchester United have just appointed Mauricio Pochettino, a manager known for developing young attackers and for implementing a high-intensity style. But which of the two clubs would be the better destination for the Brazilian? The answer depends less on Rayan’s personal preference and more on the characteristics that have made him one of the most interesting prospects in Brazilian football.

Bournemouth paid Vasco €35m (about £30m) in January 2026, betting on a 19-year-old forward. Adaptation was quick: Rayan scored five goals and provided two assists in 15 games in the second half of last season.

His profile is different from a traditional winger who relies on short dribbles and quick changes of direction to beat a marker. Rayan is a player of strength and acceleration. When he receives with space, he can carry the ball at speed, protect possession with his body and attack the box with few touches.

In the 2025 Brazilian Serie A, Rayan was the player who finished the most carries with a shot, with 33 occasions. He also scored 14 goals in the league, a number that placed him among the competition’s top scorers. The forward is not just a winger who cuts inside, however. Under Fernando Diniz at Vasco, he was also used centrally, as a centre-forward, and showed an ability to win aerial balls and shield possession with his back to goal. His height and physical strength make it possible to imagine growth in that role, although he still has to improve his control and combination play under pressure.

It is also important to consider what is left to develop. Rayan can still improve the quality of his final pass, his choice of moments to shoot and his involvement in building play. Despite the volume of carries that ended in shots, his creative output was more limited. That helps explain why he still needs an environment that gives him freedom to attack without requiring him to be the main organiser of the attack.

This season, the Brazilian has played ten Premier League games, with one goal and no assists. The output does not match his arrival in England, but Rayan remains a frequent starter and is still adapting to a new manager and a model different from Iraola’s.

Liverpool’s interest strengthened after Iraola’s arrival. The Reds followed the Brazilian closely early in the season and could consider a move in 2027. Iraola knows Rayan’s virtues and limitations. It was under his management that the Brazilian began adapting to the intensity of the Premier League, playing mainly on the right and being given freedom to attack space.

The manager also played a part in the step up of other Bournemouth players, such as Antoine Semenyo, who stood out before moving to Manchester City. Tactically, the combination makes sense. Iraola’s teams usually press high, try to win the ball back in advanced areas and accelerate as soon as they find space. He does not demand that his wingers stay tied to the touchline: they can attack inside, combine with the full-backs and appear in different channels. Rayan could play on the right, cutting inside onto his left foot, but also attack depth and offer a physical threat in transitions.

At Liverpool, that characteristic could be useful. The team has changed in attack: Bradley Barcola, Cody Gakpo, Victor Munoz and Rio Ngumoha offer alternatives, but Rayan would add a different profile, a more powerful forward able to carry the ball at speed and attack the box.

There is one caveat. Liverpool already have quality attacking options. The manager also has to manage a more demanding calendar and a team with title ambitions. Rayan could find a way to play, but would hardly have the same freedom as a young player developing at Bournemouth.

Rayan’s link with Manchester United came to light in an interview with ESPN. The forward said he has always supported the club in England because of Cristiano Ronaldo, his great idol. He also recalled the emotion of playing at Old Trafford and beating the Red Devils 3-0 with Bournemouth in October.

The statement became relevant when United hired Pochettino to try to reorganise a team going through a difficult season. The Argentinian has a track record of developing young players and built his best work in England with intense teams, aggressive in the press and quick in transitions. His most usual model starts from a 4-2-3-1, with wingers who often attack inside spaces and full-backs who push on to give width.

That structure could favour Rayan. Starting from the right, the Brazilian would have freedom to carry towards the box, finish with his left foot and attack the spaces created by team-mates’ movement. High pressing also suits his physical ability, although defensive involvement is still an area he needs to improve.

The problem is competition. United already have Bryan Mbeumo, Amad Diallo and Matheus Cunha among their attacking options, plus Benjamin Sesko as a central reference. There is also a developmental question. Pochettino usually demands a lot from his players without the ball and works with coordinated attacking movements. For a forward who still needs to improve decision-making and creation, the environment can be positive but also brings high demands. The Brazilian would need to learn to alternate individual carrying with quick combination play and movement without the ball.

United, on the other hand, could offer room to grow if Pochettino sees Rayan as a medium-term project and not an immediate solution to attacking problems. The Argentinian’s ability to develop young forwards is a point in favour, but the squad already has players in similar roles.

Between the two scenarios, Liverpool appear to offer the clearer fit. Iraola already knows the Brazilian, knows how to use his strength in transitions and has a model that favours attackers able to carry the ball at speed. The departure of Mohamed Salah has also opened space for a reorganisation of the right side, making the position a concrete possibility for Rayan.

Manchester United have the emotional appeal and a manager whose philosophy could match the Brazilian’s characteristics. But the presence of Mbeumo, Amad and Cunha makes the battle for space more complex. To turn admiration for the club into a transfer, Rayan would need to see a clear sporting plan.

At any rate, the moment is still one of development. At 20, the forward does not need to choose immediately between two giants, especially as he has a contract with Bournemouth until 2031 and is still gaining experience in a demanding league. Rayan has the attributes to reach a top-level club, but his next step needs to be defined by the project that best uses his potential. Between his familiarity with Iraola and his identification with United, Liverpool appear to have a tactical advantage.

Why Certain Pop Songs Trigger Inexplicable Emotion

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 17, 2026 | Reviewed by Abigail Fagan

Recently, I was scrolling through the Billboard Year-End Hot 100 singles of 1980. I was 14 years old that year, making it the absolute peak of my "musical reminiscence bump," the developmental window when our brains permanently wire the music of our youth to our deepest emotions.

While perusing the list, I realized there's one song that always stops me dead in my tracks and hits surprisingly hard whenever it catches me off guard in a random place, like a supermarket checkout line. That song is Elton John's "Little Jeannie."

Lyrically, this song doesn't say anything profoundly moving until near the end. And the refrain "I want you to be my acrobat, I want you to be my lover" always seems like a throwaway line. On paper, the song could easily be categorized as a seriously schmaltzy, mid-tempo early '80s pop song.

Nevertheless, every time I hear the opening percussion and cascading drum intro, I get verklempt. During the 20-second, lyric-free introduction that might sound like "Muzak-y" elevator music to some, my emotions swell and I'm filled with a deep sense of, for lack of a better word, "wistfulness" that doesn't make sense to me logically.

Curious if I was alone in having such a poignant response to a somewhat cheesy pop song, I looked at the YouTube comments to see if other listeners echoed my sentiment.

Notably, for many of the "Little Jeannie" versions on YouTube, all of which are sung in English by Elton John, the comment sections are dominated by foreign-language responses (lots of Portuguese!). According to Google Translate, these comments convey that even among non-English speakers or ESL listeners, "Little Jeannie" hits hard in bittersweet ways that seem to catch people off guard.

While sifting through various comments, I found two that elucidate the inexplicable power of "Little Jeannie" to stir emotions:

Why does a song that isn't inherently sad make someone cry? And how can a listener feel nostalgia for an era they didn't live through? As it turns out, the answers lie in a fascinating intersection of behavioral psychology, imagination, and neuroscience.

A new study published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences (PNAS) on November 16, 2026, investigated the sociocultural dimensions of music-evoked memories. The researchers played 30-second clips of billboard-topping hits to 223 college students, utilizing songs from their own teenage years (the 2010s) and their parents' teenage years (the 1980s and 1990s).

Using natural language processing to analyze the memories these songs evoked, the researchers found that certain songs reliably generated strikingly similar memory scenarios among strangers from comparable backgrounds, driven by culturally learned "scripts."

To find out where that emotion was coming from, the researchers stripped away the music and gave a separate group of participants just a song's lyrics to read. Without music accompanying the words to a song, the shared sociocultural dimensions of people's autobiographical memories and associations diminished.

Based on this observation, the researchers concluded that the lyrics alone don't drive our intense reactions to pop music. Instead, the emotional scripts are embedded in the musical sound and genre itself.

When a YouTube commenter notes that "Little Jeannie" isn't a sad song but makes their eyes well up with tears, it isn't necessarily the lyrics driving that reaction. It's the specific acoustic architecture created by an old Fender Rhodes electric piano, alto saxophone, and flugelhorn, all wrapped in layered vocal harmonies. This unique musical alchemy tugs directly at the heartstrings, completely bypassing the analytical mind where words reside.

How does neuroscience explain the enigma of a young listener feeling "ridiculously melancholic" when hearing a song released decades before they were born?

One possible explanation: While we experience a primary surge of memory attachment for the music of our own adolescence, we also develop a secondary attachment to the music of our parents and caregivers from earlier generations. As the authors explain in their recent PNAS paper:

"Interestingly, however, music-evoked autobiographical memories (MEAMs) are characterized by a potentially unique phenomenon known as 'cascading reminiscence bumps,' in which they exhibit a secondary reminiscence bump associated with music that was popular before listeners were born."

As children, we're often captive audiences in the backseat of a car or the family living room. Long before music was siloed into individual earbuds via streaming apps, "hard copy" formats like vinyl records, CDs, and mixtapes filled these shared spaces. Played aloud on common speakers, we passively absorbed our parents' adolescent soundtracks by osmosis.

Yet, even as modern listening habits shift toward isolated headphones, this intergenerational phenomenon persists. This new (2026) study suggests that these cultural "scripts" are so deeply wired into us that even if a younger participant had never heard a specific older song before, their brain can use the song's genre to automatically retrieve a feeling that fits the cultural vibe.

Because these acoustic architectures activate shared neural representations, the emotional script transcends both the generation and the delivery method.

Another recent study by Princeton's Elizabeth Margulis and colleagues, published in Nature Communications on August 18, 2026, used fMRI to explore the neural representations of imagined narratives that participants conjured up while listening to unfamiliar music.

During the experiment, researchers scanned people's brains while they listened to completely instrumental tracks. The scans revealed that across different test subjects, the music produced highly organized, shared patterns in the brain's default mode network (DMN). As the authors explain:

"This work demonstrates that music elicits detectable, modality-general representations, a kind of higher-order 'meaning', integrating both narrative imagery and its associated emotional profile, specific to particular excerpts."

Interestingly, Margulis's team found that the DMN doesn't need semantic language to engage in profound meaning-making. This helps to explain why it's almost impossible to pinpoint why certain music evokes shared imagined narratives; these emotions bubble up outside the realm of words (i.e., lexical semantics).

According to the latest 2026 research, music has the power to tap into a cultural frequency that bypasses our critical minds, reminding us that oftentimes, profound meaning is found in things we feel deeply but can't articulate.

Cara Turnbull, Benjamin M. Kubit, Kelly Jakubowski, Elizabeth H. Margulis. Music Illuminates Sociocultural Dimensions of Autobiographical Memory. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences (First published: November 16, 2026) doi:10.1073/pnas.2536885123

Elizabeth Margulis, Uri Hasson, Itamar Jalon, Jamal Williams, Grace Simmons, Karen Electra Christianson, Cara Turnbull. Music Evokes Shared Neural Representations of Imagined Narratives Across Sensory Modalities. Nature Communications (First published: August 18, 2026) doi:10.1038/s41467-026-76452-0

Kelly Jakubowski, Tuomas Eerola, Barbara Tillmann, Fabien Perrin, Lizette Heine. A Cross-Sectional Study of Reminiscence Bumps for Music-Related Memories in Adulthood. Music & Science (First published: October 23, 2020) doi:10.1177/2059204320965058

OMCO Solar offers solar-powered tracker control system

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

OMCO Solar now offers a version of its OMCO Star Tracker Control System powered entirely by solar energy, allowing tracker controls to operate without AC power at the site.

The system, first launched last year, is designed for OMCO Origin Factory-Direct Trackers. Its tracker controllers, array controllers and sensor bridges use all-weather battery packs charged by solar modules.

According to OMCO, the technology eliminates the need for AC power to tracker control components and an uninterruptible power supply. It also allows tracker controls to operate and protects modules against wind, snow and hail before AC power is available.

“Our industry has solved so many problems as we have grown – we’re now the leading source for new electric energy capacity in the US. This addition to the OMCO Star product line solves one more – how to protect modules if there’s no AC power to the site even for a short time. We’re excited to offer this solution to our existing customers and to new partners,” said Matt Kesler, vice president of solar technology at OMCO Solar, in a statement.

The OMCO Star system uses LoRa, or long-range, wireless technology with a communication range greater than 1 mile. According to OMCO, this reduces the number of network controllers required and allows direct communication with every tracker without relying on a mesh network.

The company says LoRa is 100 times less susceptible to electromagnetic noise than Zigbee and better able to connect to distant trackers despite obstacles.

OMCO says its all-weather battery packs allow the solar-powered controls to operate reliably in cold climates. The system includes field-replaceable batteries, a phone app that eliminates the need for buttons on controllers and a remote monitoring and control portal.

The portal allows operators to move trackers for activities such as mowing, snow plowing and module cleaning. The system is developed, manufactured and supported by OMCO Solar in the United States.

“We are excited about bringing this innovation to the Utility Scale and DG tracker markets. Over the past few years, OMCO has expanded its capabilities beyond just being a US steel manufacturer to now being a true technology leader in our space. We look forward to supporting our customers/partners with the OMCO Star Tracker Control System, improving reliability, and reducing the labor and materials needed to build their projects,” said Eric Goodwin, vice president of business development at OMCO Solar.

OMCO Solar will display the all-DC tracker control system at RE+ in Las Vegas, November 17–19, in the North Hall, booth N516.

Pochettino's Transfer Solution: Man Utd Academy Star Harry Amass

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Travel  |  Read Source →

Mauricio Pochettino is the new manager of Manchester United, and he already knows that he has some major issues to solve within his squad.

The Argentine has signed a two-and-a-half-year deal to leave the USMNT after their Nations League quarter-final second-leg clash with Haiti and make the switch to Old Trafford, where his first game will come on Sunday afternoon when they travel to Anfield to face off against arch-rivals Liverpool.

The boss will only have a number of days to assess his squad before picking his first starting lineup, but there are areas of the squad that are already short of quality and options, and the club will be looking to address that in the January transfer window.

In the summer the club were looking to add a forward and a left-back to the squad, but after spending £155m on Youri Tielemans, Andrey Santos, and Carlos Baleba, the club opted against bringing in any further additions.

Benjamin Sesko has since been ruled out for several months with a recurrence of his shin injury, meaning the striker position is short on quality, while at left-back both Luke Shaw and Patrick Dorgu have been dealing with injury issues throughout the campaign.

But while the striker situation has got the depth across the attack to survive for a few more weeks before any deals can be done, the left-back position is not so lucky, and Pochettino must start to look at options to solve the problem between now and then.

The 54-year-old has never been shy of giving young players an opportunity in the first team. It was he who gave Luke Shaw his debut at Southampton as a 17-year-old, while he also gave players like Dele Alli, Philippe Coutinho, Harrison Reed, Kyle Walker-Peters, Xavi Simons, and Harry Kane their big breaks at the top level.

Now at Old Trafford, where he has the chance to revolutionise Man Utd, he will have the responsiblity of ensuring that the famous pathway from Carrington to the first-team doesn't disappear.

The academy at Man Utd has been legendary in the Premier League era, producing top stars like Ryan Giggs, David Beckham, Paul Scholes, Marcus Rashford, and Paul Pogba. The biggest talent now is JJ Gabriel, although the 16-year-old is set to depart the club for Real Madrid after a fallout between the club and his family over his involvement in first-team games.

But there is another youngster who is ready to make an impact in the first-team, and Pochettino could solve a major issue by giving him his chance.

Youngster Harry Amass has been one of the most impressive young players to come out of the academy system at Man Utd in recent years, but he has found opportunities in the first-team hard to come by so far.

The 19-year-old spent last season out on loan at Sheffield Wednesday before joining Norwich in January, where he suffered a season-ending injury after just a handful of appearances.

But he returned to Old Trafford in the summer and was part of the pre-season squad, even scoring in the win over Rosenborg. Despite the injuries suffered by Shaw and Dorgu, however, Carrick still handed him just 18 minutes of action before he was sacked, preferring to use Diogo Dalot, Noussair Mazraoui and Ayden Heaven out of position instead.

Pochettino will see a lot of what he saw in Shaw at Southampton in Amass. He is quick, powerful, excellent on the ball, and good defensively. In one-on-one situations, he can hold his own too, and while he looks small, he is almost 6ft tall.

Whenever he has been given a chance to shine he has taken it, too. So much so, that former teammate Charlie McNeill described him as 'ridiculous'.

“He’s a joke, honestly. He’s so good, on the ball he’s ridiculous and he’s not shy of putting a tackle in. He's good enough to have a future there [Man Utd."

Pochettino has a chance to give him his opportunity and figure out whether a big signing is really needed in January, or if the club have enough to get by until the summer when Shaw will likely leave, and a new first-choice arrival can join to compete with Amass and Dorgu.
