As decision model use skyrockets, is customization the next frontier?

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In October, more than one in four requests on Vercel’s AI model router went to decision models, not LLMs — an enormous share of work for a kind of computerized intelligence that was invented only a month earlier.

Decision models are LLMs that don’t produce output tokens; instead, they’re tuned to use their general intelligence to quickly and cheaply decide between preset options, while registering its level of confidence in the choice. They’re useful in production environments, where fast decisions can be more important than the reasoning provided by a slower and more expensive frontier model.

TypeSafe became a $7.5 billion company after releasing its Jev family of models, which continues to dominate usage (at least where we can see metrics from services like OpenRouter, which handled more than 1.5 billion queries to TypeSafe’s models last week).

However, it’s easy to make a decision model, and dozens were released in the wake of Jev. One Amazon engineer made one in a weekend, and soon every hyperscaler had its own option. On Tuesday, Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella said that his company is rolling out its decision model, Microsoft-Decision-1.1, for wide use on Azure immediately.

The action is happening on both ends of the ecosystem: Sqwish, a U.K.-based AI startup behind a decision model called kettei fat, announced a $3 million pre-seed round today. Co-founders Ushnish Sengupta and Federica Freddi, who met as Cambridge grad students, had been developing small LLMs when Jev arrived on the scene, and they realized their architectures could be repurposed as a powerful decision model.

Data appears to be the key to a successful model in this category — TypeSafe CEO Diogo Almeida told TechCrunch that the hardest part of building Jev was finely calibrating its judgement using statistically-adjusted synthetic data. Sqiwsh, too, has been working with synthetic data, but its unique offering is built-in post-training tools that use a customer’s own data to improve the model’s judgement.

“What makes us not a commodity, I think, is the fact that we are offering our customers the same power…they can keep uploading their production data, and just the same way that we trained our models, their models can also keep getting better at the tasks that they care about,” CEO Ushnish Sengupta told TechCrunch.

Attempting to rank decision models is difficult; Sqwish’s “kettei fat” model has performed well on some benchmarks, but most builders of the technology say that the real proof is in the pudding: It’s generally simple for developers to sub in one model for another and see how it does in a specific context.

“The benchmark is more of an invitation to try the model out, and then the continual tuning is the reason why they should stick to us,” Sengupta said.

The Microsoft-Decision-1.1 model is also the result of the company carefully balancing across cost, latency, intelligence and calibration. Achint Srivastava, the engineering VP who led the project, agreed that too much focus on benchmarks is a mistake.

“What we see is that ‘benchmaxed’ models fall flat under real enterprise usage very quickly,” Srivastava wrote in an email. “Our testing underscores just how challenging it is to deliver accuracy, reliability, speed and cost efficiency in a single model. Microsoft-Decision-1.1 and Jev are among the few that perform strongly across all four dimensions, even when evaluated on ‘held out’ or unseen benchmarks reflective of real use cases.”

For startups like Sqwish, competition with hyperscalers and frontier labs is an opportunity. Freddi predicts that future systems will see so-called System One intelligence, like decision models, working in concert with System Two intelligence, like frontier LLMs.

“Even in that future, System One is maybe a $10 billion, $50 billion [opportunity], and OpenAI is doing this IPO with you know $1 trillion, $2 trillion market value in mind,” she told TechCrunch. “This is all we care about; whereas for them, this is just a side project.”

Data center slowdown would raise risks for utility investments

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

An estimated 191 GW of planned data center capacity faces new risk after the midterm elections, with exposure concentrated in Texas, Ohio and Georgia, according to a report from Orennia.

Utilities could be left holding the bill for billions in infrastructure spending committed in anticipation of artificial intelligence data centers that are delayed, downsized or canceled, industry observers and experts told Utility Dive.

Opposition to data center construction has crossed traditional party lines, while Democratic gains in the midterms are adding pressure on earlier-stage investments across key markets. Together, these dynamics raise the stakes for project approvals and utility cost recovery, they say.

“From a financing perspective, prolonged uncertainty will increase the cost of capital, reduce available leverage and make investors more hesitant to commit significant development dollars before power is secured,” said Andrejka Bernatova, managing partner of investment firm Dynamix Capital Partners, which invests in energy infrastructure.

Even in regions that theoretically have enough capacity to accommodate new large loads, projects may struggle to secure a credible path to power on the timelines they need.

“Interconnection, permitting, and financing all matter, but they are part of the same execution question of whether the team can get reliable power to the site and actually deliver,” Bernatova said. “A customer commitment or a large announced capacity number does not answer that by itself.”

Ultimately, Bernatova expects investors to favor markets that offer certainty around power availability and timelines, potentially eroding the competitive advantage of markets like Texas, which until recently attracted substantial data center investment.

Over the summer, Gov. Greg Abbott, R, announced a pause on new data center interconnections pending an audit of the queue. In the midterms, he lost his bid for reelection to Democrat Gina Hinojosa, who has pledged to maintain pressure on the industry after taking office next year.

Nearly all of the 46 municipal ballot measures tracked by Bloomberg that addressed data center development resulted in restrictions, moratoriums or bans.

That unfavorability extends to a substantial share of the development pipeline. An estimated 191 GW of planned data center capacity faces new risk, with exposure concentrated in Texas, Ohio and Georgia, where incoming Democratic governors campaigned on pausing or restricting construction, according to a report from energy analytics firm Orennia that was shared with Utility Dive.

Texas accounts for nearly half of the pre-construction in development in the U.S., so a statewide approval freeze would have an outsized impact on the national buildout, but the “impact of delayed or unrealized data center build-out on power prices varies widely across technologies and markets,” Orennia said.

If 20% of projected data center demand in the Electric Reliability Council of Texas region fails to materialize, solar and wind generators could see their average wholesale revenue fall 38% and 37%, respectively, according to the report. Implied heat rates, a measure of the economics of gas-fired generation relative to fuel costs, could decline 39%. In the same scenario, two-hour battery storage arbitrage spreads could fall below new-build break-even requirements.

In PJM, delaying half of the planned data center buildout by two years could reduce four-hour battery storage arbitrage spreads by 16% and lower solar and wind capture prices and implied heat rates by about 12% through 2030, the modeling suggests.

The election results are unlikely to produce sweeping federal AI legislation but could lead to congressional hearings and greater transparency requirements, according to D.C.-based research and advisory firm Capstone. Its analysts expect much of the policy debate to take place at state utility commissions and local siting boards.

Despite a proliferation of large-load tariffs over the past year, safeguards may still leave customers exposed in the event of a slowdown, some say.

“Existing tariffs and regulatory frameworks likely won’t fully protect customers because they were designed for a different century,” Louisa Eberle, principal at the Regulatory Assistance Project, warned in a written statement.

Eberle also called for reforms to utility business practices “to turn this data center surge into updated infrastructure, cleaner energy and a better deal for average customers.”

The push for lower electricity bills is prompting policymakers to look more closely at how utilities use existing grid infrastructure.

Richard Caperton, vice president of policy at SPAN, a maker of smart electrical panels, anticipates greater emphasis on data center projects that can be served without major new grid investments.

“I’m really thrilled to see governors like Abigail Spanberger [in Virginia] campaign on affordability and then embrace utilization as the solution,” he said.

In April, Gov. Spanberger signed legislation requiring Dominion Energy and Appalachian Power to report how effectively they use existing grid infrastructure. The State Corporation Commission can use those metrics to establish improvement targets and evaluate utility spending.

“Winning candidates who emphasized lowering electricity prices are already looking for ways to deliver on their promises quickly,” Caperton said. “I expect that will lead to more policies and programs that boost grid utilization being ratified and implemented in early 2027. How those policies affect data center development and utility investments will vary by state.”

Stephen Rose, CEO of infrastructure software provider Render Networks, said he expects to see a “pause and necessary shakeout” in data center development.

“Fully permitted projects with real capital will keep moving,” Rose said. “The speculative end takes the hit: developers land-banking without firm power, and the contractors who staffed up for projects that won't break ground.”

Rose cited AEP Ohio's large-load tariff, approved in July 2025, which requires new data centers above 25 MW to pay for at least 85% of requested capacity for up to 12 years, with exit fees and financial guarantees. The utility’s data-center pipeline subsequently fell by more than half from more than 30 GW to about 13.

Rose expressed concern, however, that a broad slowdown wouldn’t affect hyperscalers as much as it would the businesses and people that build data centers. He recalled delays in Louisiana's Broadband Equity, Access and Deployment program in 2025, where service providers bore the costs of equipment and crews while waiting for approvals.

“When timelines stalled, the hit didn't land on big tech balance sheets. It landed on local drilling, civil and fiber contractors,” Rose said. “A data center slowdown hits the same people first, and Texas risks the same fallout.”

Permitting hurdles could make data center developers more selective about where they build and under what terms.

Andy Roehr, managing director in the tax advisory firm Baker Tilly’s energy and infrastructure practice, sees developers as more likely to redirect capital than abandon projects – though that could lead to a winnowing of queues in some jurisdictions.

“A developer is typically evaluating three, four or five sites at any given moment, so when permitting, regulation or local opposition makes one location difficult to read, the project is far more likely to be delayed or redirected than canceled,” Roehr said.

Roehr said states and municipalities competing for projects that can no longer move forward elsewhere could find themselves in a “bidding war.”

"The most interesting thing I am seeing is that these moratoria are creating a developer’s market since they have the assets, tax revenues and jobs that communities want or need,” he continued.

Some developers may shift to “less optimal sites where projects can get done at an effective price point and time-to-market, but the regulatory and legislative environment is less hostile,” he said.

Changes in expected demand could also lead to repricing across the supply chain, from gas turbines and processor racks to construction costs.

Another uncertainty is the possibility of federal permitting reform, which could make it easier to build certain kinds of energy projects.

The Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, which advanced in the Senate last week, faces a difficult path to passage following the midterm elections, analysts say.

Democrats are split over the proposal, with some expressing concerns over changes to environmental protections in the bill and urging party leaders to wait until next year when the Democrats will control both the House and Senate to pursue their priorities.

Roehr said in his view, the BAAJA takes an “all of the above” approach and would be as meaningful to solar as to nuclear or natural gas. But what the industry needs most, he said, is stability.

“Developers, investors and host communities need certainty to keep committing capital and local resources to these projects,” Roehr said. “These are 20-, 40-, even 60-year investments, and if the environment keeps whipsawing then jobs don’t appear, tax payments don’t appear and capital moves on — there will be no winners.”

Get the free daily newsletter read by industry experts

Michigan is among the states weighing reforms to rate case planning and utility profits. Experts and stakeholders told Utility Dive what works — and what doesn’t.

Major work by regional planners is focused on flexible access to existing transmission. Some say greater emphasis on advanced transmission technologies can quickly and cost-effectively create room for new large loads.

The donation, which advocates called “problematic” and “convenient” for the company, comes to light as federal regulators chosen by President Donald Trump consider whether to allow NextEra to merge with Dominion Energy.

In North America, data centers are boosting the long-term investment case for energy storage and renewables as gas turbine backlogs and rising fuel volatility drive up peaking costs, according to a note from Wood Mackenzie.

CERN physicists measure the universe at the smallest scale ever: enter the 'zeptoverse'

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Physicists at the Large Hadron Collider have detected a rare, 1-in-10-billion particle interaction that opens a view into the 'zeptoverse,' the smallest distance scale ever measured.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Using the Large Hadron Collider (LHC), the world's largest particle accelerator, scientists have gotten the first glimpse of the "zeptoverse" — the universe as it behaves within less than a hundred-trillionth of a wavelength of visible light, the smallest scale that researchers have explored to date.

The new findings may one day help to answer long-standing cosmic mysteries, such as why there is far more matter than antimatter seen in the universe, and what makes up the dark matter that constitutes about 85% of all matter in the universe, the researchers announced Nov. 3 at a seminar at CERN (the European Organization for Nuclear Research), the organization that operates the LHC. The study, available as a preprint, has not yet been peer reviewed.

At the LHC, located near Geneva at the border of France and Switzerland, researchers were investigating elusive particles called beauty quarks. These subatomic particles are cousins of the standard "up" and "down" quarks that make up the cores of all atoms in the universe.

When beauty quarks are created — in this case, when the LHC smashes atoms together at incredibly high speeds — they live for just one and a half trillionths of a second before they decay. There are a number of ways beauty quarks can break down; researchers hope that analyzing rare forms of these decays might reveal that they might not unfold as expected.

Such outcomes could then shed light on new kinds of physics found only at very tiny scales of zeptometers, or trillionths of a nanometer. (For comparison, a wavelength of visible light ranges from about 380 to 750 nanometers.)

An artist's visualization of a beauty particle decaying into two muons.

By exploring this zeptoverse, scientists hope to discover new particles that have escaped detection until now.

"We're confident there must be new particles to discover, and these could help explain mysteries like dark matter and the existence of matter in the universe," study co-author Harry Cliff, a particle physicist at the University of Cambridge, told Live Science.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

"Why haven't we seen these particles yet? One answer, and perhaps the most likely answer, is these new particles only become manifest at very, very short-distance scales. So by probing into the zepto scale, we give ourselves the best chance possible of finding these new particles that could explain these mysteries."

We're now recording in a single year what took 10 years to record in the past.

Now, after hunting for more than four decades, physicists have found the first evidence of the rarest kind of beauty-quark decay seen yet. "Only one in 10 billion beauty particles decay this way," Cliff said.

In this form of decay, technically known as the B0 to mu+ mu− decay, a beauty quark collides with an oppositely charged beauty antiquark. They annihilate each other, generating a pair of smaller particles ‪—‬ a muon and an antimuon. Previous research suggested that this requires a complicated set of interactions, making this kind of decay extraordinarily rare.

An experiment at the LHC called LHCb was specifically designed to study beauty quarks. In 2024, a major upgrade to LHCb made it possible to distinguish different types of particles. Using machine learning techniques, the researchers suppressed huge amounts of background noise, which helped them detect the first evidence of the B0 to mu+ mu− decay.

"We're now recording in a single year what took 10 years to record in the past," Cliff said. "And so we have this massive sample of beauty decays, and that is ultimately what allowed us to see this decay for the first time."

The new results suggest that the way this rare decay happens matches the predictions of the Standard Model of particle physics, the current best description of how the basic building blocks of the universe behave. As such, the researchers have not yet discovered signs of new particles or new physics beyond the Standard Model.

"For me, I think the most exciting thing about this result is the fact that we are probing these unimaginably short distance scales," Cliff said. "Seeing the evidence for this decay begins a new area of research where we can start to make precision measurements that will give us the best chance of finding new physics in the future."

The researchers would now like to upgrade LHCb to fully exploit the improvements to the LHC's luminosity that are currently planned for the 2030s.

"However, there is some doubt over that now because the U.K. recently withdrew funding from the project," Cliff said. "So we are really trying to figure out if we'll be able to continue this type of research."

In an ideal world, "we'll collect many hundreds of these decays in the future, make precise measurements and probe deep into the zeptoverse," Cliff said.

Kevin Durant Lists Arizona Home for $13 Million—1 Year After His Trade to the Houston Rockets

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Houston Rockets star Kevin Durant has listed his sprawling Arizona property for $13.9 million—jut three years after he picked up the palatial pad.

The NBA icon, 38, purchased the property in 2023 for $12.9 million from fellow athlete DeAndre Hopkins when he was traded from the Brooklyn Nets to the Phoenix Suns.

Now, following his Texas trade, the basketball star is looking to part ways with the property, listing it with Alexa Kort of The Beverly Hills Estates and Adam Bowman and Brian North of Compass, as first reported by the Wall Street Journal.

Durant initially put the property on the market in September 2025 for $14.4 million. He then trimmed the price to $13.9 million, before removing it from the selling block.

The home, which was designed by acclaimed architect Jack DeBartolo, offers up 10,865 square feet and is situated on a 1.72-acre lot.

It comes complete with five bedrooms, eight bathrooms, and several other lavish amenities.

Upon entering the home, guests are greeted with poured-in-place concrete walls, gallerylike corridors, floor-to-ceiling glass, and custom finishes.

The living room offers guests the perfect place to lounge thanks to its cozy fireplace.

Although minimalist, the home offers a moody aesthetic complete with dark tones.

The chef's kitchen boasts a black marble island with matching black cabinets and state-of-the-art appliances.

The primary suite offers enough space for a king-sized bed and opens directly to the outdoor area.

The primary bathroom is certainly the crown jewel of the property thanks to its Calacatta marble bath and dual vanities.

The home also features a sleek movie theater room, a walk-in closet with custom storage space, and an office space.

Elsewhere in the home, there is a sauna and steam shower.

The property also features a 2,000-square-foot garage designed to show off spectacular vehicles.

Moving outside, guests can enjoy an 82-foot lap and reflecting pool as well as panoramic views of the Camelback Mountain, the Praying Monk and Piestewa Peak, as per a press release shared with Realtor.com®.

This isn't the first time Durant's property holdings were at the center of headlines.

In 2025, his former luxury townhome in Oklahoma City, OK, which boasts a prime downtown location, four bedrooms, two kitchens, and a private elevator—hit the market for less than the cost of a bag of groceries.

"The owner said, 'Let's just put it out there for $35 and see if someone wants to own a piece of NBA history,'" listing agent Ieasha Larkpor, of Thunder Team Realty, told Realtor.com® at the time.

The jaw-droppingly low price tag is meant to spark interest, and so far the ploy has worked, with Larkpor fielding hundreds of offers—some even in the high six figures, close to the price the owner is hoping to get.

But that hasn't stopped the occasional joker from taking the abnormally low price tag literally.

"Yes, I've gotten offers for $35," said the agent. "And I have to present them to the owner. But luckily, they can be weeded out fast."

Durant bought two three-level townhomes at 422 NE 2nd Street for $1,769,000 in 2013 while playing with the Oklahoma City Thunder.

The NBA and Olympic star put his stamp all over the four-bedroom, four-bath townhouse, including an extra-tall shower and an extra-long bathtub to accommodate his 6-foot-11-inch frame.

"Everything in the unit was designed by Kevin Durant himself or his designers according to his taste at the time," said Larkpor. The furniture and artwork could be negotiable with the sale.

In 2017, the sports legend sold one of the 3,904-square-foot units for $1.15 million to a neighbor in the complex. The neighbor happened to be a fan who kept most of Durant's stylistic choices, including the paint colors, bold wallpaper, and light fixtures.

"The new owner wanted to leave [Durant's] stamp, as it is a very nice stamp," said Larkpor.

Kelsi Karruli is a senior celebrity reporter who joined Realtor.com in 2025. She writes about trends encompassing real estate and celebrity properties. She previously wrote for Daily Mail, True Urban Culture, and JMedia Corporation, where she specialized in covering celebrities, relationships, psychology, and gender issues. She has a bachelor's degree in journalism from Pace University, where she received the James Rose Award for excellence in journalism. When she isn’t updating readers on A-listers' moves, she is spending time with her cat, Mary, and cheering on the Albanian soccer team.

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Your weekly briefing on Arsenal, featuring team news and expert analysis from Matt Verri

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Newcastle United will be without talismanic midfielder Joelinton for Saturday’s clash with Arsenal, but will at least have summer signing Matias Fernandez-Pardo available for the weekend.

Joelinton was already considered a doubt for the fixture before the international break, having missed the 1-1 draw with Fulham earlier this month due to a thigh issue.

And it has now been revealed the Brazilian has yet to return to training. Joelinton did not feature in an open session on Tuesday, with The Chronicle reporting he is now set for an MRI scan to determine the exact problem with his thigh.

There is optimism he can return before Christmas, with Matthias Jaissle expected to issue a clearer update at his pre-match press conference this week.

Before the international break, the Magpies’ boss explained: "Joelinton went out with an injury again. We need to make the final treatment plan and see how long he will be out."

Other absentees from Newcastle training were Joe Willock and Nico Gonzalez, although the duo were only ill and are expected to be fit to face Arsenal.

The big boon for Newcastle is that attacker Fernandez-Pardo looks to have finally recovered from a virus.

Fernandez-Pardo turned down the chance to represent Belgium during the latest international break, with his national team boss stating that he was not fully fit.

Belgium head coach Mark van Bommel shared: “He was ill for a very long time.

“He has only trained twice since October 17. As a result, he isn’t at 100 per cent, which is why he isn’t part of the squad. He still doesn’t feel quite right, so it was his own choice.”

The 21-year-old came off the bench to feature against Fulham, but the £51million man could be in line for a start against the Gunners on Saturday.

Since swapping France for Tyneside, Fernandez-Pardo has made six Premier League appearances.

Aston Martin Reveals Fernando Alonso's Dakar Challenger Inspired By A 'Call Of Duty' Tie-In

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The 2027 Dakar Rally is already set to be an unusual affair with an unexpected return to Africa as the route shifts from Saudi Arabia to Portugal and Morocco. Now, three Aston Martins will be tackling the famed cross-country rally in January. The British manufacturer revealed the Dreadnought on Tuesday, its first rally-raid machine. If that nameplate sounds familiar, the Aston Martin Dreadnought was originally a digital-only vehicle exclusively designed for "Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4."

The two-time F1 world champion won't be heading out into the desert alone. He will reunite with his previous Dakar co-driver, Marc Coma. Few competitors are as accomplished and experienced as Coma. The 50-year-old won the Dakar five times with KTM and briefly served as the rally's race director between 2016 and 2018. As a pairing, Alonso and Coma finished 13th in the 2020 Dakar Rally with Toyota. Alonso said his return was driven by a desire to challenge himself. Honestly, F1 isn't much of a challenge at Aston Martin this season. The Spaniard is just trundling around, hoping to finish races. He said in a release:

"After deciding to continue racing in Formula One with the Aston Martin Aramco, I thought carefully about Dakar. In the end, the decision became simple. I wanted to embrace the challenge completely and take on both Formula One and the Dakar Rally in the same year."

The version of the Aston Martin Dreadnought won't feature a carbon-fiber chassis, military-grade armor plating or weapons storage like its Call of Duty counterpart. This Dreadnought is based on the Prodrive-designed Dacia Sandrider. The machine is fitted with the same three-liter, twin-turbocharged Nissan engine. The starkest difference is the bodywork, styled after the digital-only combat SUV, most evident in the square headlights and the louvered rear window.

The Prodrive-run effort will also include another usual suspect at the wheel of a Dreadnought: Nine-time World Rally Champion Sébastien Loeb. The living legend has never won the Dakar Rally, but has finished second on three occasions. The third Dreadnought will be driven by Lucas Moraes. The Brazilian joined the Dacia-Prodrive effort after winning the World Rally-Raid Championship with Toyota in 2025. The vehicle's origin may seem ridiculous, but Aston Martin has a legitimate chance of winning the Dakar in January. Prodrive is the returning winner after Nasser Al-Attiyah took the 2026 victory in a Dacia Sandrider.

Grado's GW200 headphones boast fresh drivers and a longer battery life

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

They run $329 and are up for preorder now.

Grado has been a big name among audiophiles for decades. The company resisted making a wireless model until 2018 when it launched the well-received GW100 open-back headphones. Now, Grado is back with a major refresh of those initial Bluetooth cans.

The GW200 brings a spate of upgraded features to the table. First of all, there's a newly-designed audio driver. The 44mm S is based on the larger drivers found with some of its wired headphones, like the HP100 SE. The company promises they delivery "clarity, detail and a full-bodied musical presentation while preserving the openness and natural character that define the Grado listening experience."

The battery life here has also been improved. The GW100 headphones topped out at just 15 hours. This was an issue even back in 2018. However, the GW200 can last around 60 hours per charge, which is more in line with competitors. They can also operate via a wired 3.5mm connection and this won't draw any power.

They use Bluetooth 5.4 and support for a bunch of common codecs, including Qualcomm aptX Lossless, aptX Adaptive and SBC, among others. The headphones also include multipoint connectivity that can maintain two simultaneous Bluetooth connections. That should make it easy to switch audio sources on the fly.

These are on the lighter side, especially when compared to the GW100 headphones. The GW200 cans weigh 235 grams and include a padded leather strap and an adjustable steel band. They even integrate with the company's complete range of standalone headphone cushions, for increased comfort.

Grado's new GW200 headphones are available to preorder right now and cost $329. Shipments should go out in December. Since launching the wireless GW100 headphones back in 2018, the company has also branched out into wireless earbuds.

Inde Navarrette Credits Actresses Who Gave Her ‘Permission to Be Free’

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

For ELLE Women in Hollywood honoree Inde Navarrette, playing Nikki in Obsession was about far more than landing her first leading role. In her acceptance speech at our annual event at the Four Seasons Hotel Los Angeles at Beverly Hills last night, the actress spoke about putting everything that she had into playing the character. “I needed to break through every glass ceiling either school, peers, society, or even I put on myself,” she said. “I needed to be connected with that rage that I used to find solace in. I needed to be reminded that my voice, whether soft or loud, holds weight and creates tension that I get to be in control of. I can play a girl who has an internal world—crazy!—whom is the lead of a film and whom is not the perfect victim. Nikki was my opportunity to show the world what I knew I could do and to that, I have to say thank you.”

She went on to thank Obsession’s writer and director Curry Barker, along with all actresses who inspired her early on (including Bryce Dallas Howard, who presented Navarrette with the award). “Thank you not only to Curry Barker for writing a role that allowed me that chance, but thank you to all of the women that took that chance before me,” she said. “Thank you to Jessica Lange for showing me and all of her work that there is beauty in madness and to just give it to the world. Thank you to Natalie Portman for Léon: The Professional. It was the first time I got to see a kid in a movie being more than a kid. She was me. Thank you to Sally Field and Steel Magnolias for daring to portray grief honestly and the hardships of motherhood gracefully. Thank you, Bryce Dallas Howard and The Help, for portraying how intersectional womanhood can divide us and that women who look different than ourselves are no different than ourselves. Thank you to Florence Pugh, Carrie Coon, Julianne Moore, Elizabeth Debicki, Samantha Morton, Rosalind Eleazar, Penélope Cruz. Thank you for daring to be wild. Thank you. Incredibly, you have all given me permission to be free. Let’s all shine a light in each other.”

Watch Navarrette’s full speech above and read her Women in Hollywood cover story here.

Exceeding Expectations Every Step of the Way

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Berkshire Hathaway HomeServices Starck Real Estate

Most important thing you learned this past year: The value of recognizing and drawing out the potential in others, sometimes before they even see it in themselves.

Who has most influenced your success? My father, Andy, who led the company for nearly 40 years. I consider it a privilege and a blessing to have been able to work and learn alongside him.

As a third-generation member of your family’s real estate firm, how do you honor the firm’s legacy while bringing your own perspective and ideas to the business?

We consider ourselves a family business made up of family businesses, so inherent in that is a sense of responsibility to the people and the families we serve. And so it’s a stewardship of what has been entrusted, not only to me, but to everyone in our company. The more we grow and find success, the more we’re able to invest in our agents and give in meaningful ways to charitable organizations and the communities we serve.

What is the most valuable lesson you’ve learned from the previous generations of your family who worked in the business?

The firm was founded by my grandparents on the values of service to others over ourselves and doing business with complete truth and integrity, which is part of the reputation we’ve worked hard to build over the last 66-plus years. While we firmly believe that good things happen when you treat others the way you would want to be treated, we take it above and beyond the Golden Rule to the Platinum Rule, which is treating others the way they want to be treated. Taking that one step further, our focus is on providing double platinum experiences—or exceeding expectations in order to treat others in a way they can’t even conceive.

What factor has the largest influence on the real estate industry today?

Distractions, particularly the technology in our pockets, which brings to mind the following Robin Sharma quote: “The enemy of mastery is not mediocrity. It is distractions. The addiction to distractions ruins many potentially awesome lives.” We’re privileged to work in this noble profession, which will always be a relationship-based business. Technology can be a wonderful tool when it’s used properly, but we believe that focusing on relationships and service is the best way to build a sustainable business that’s successful in any market.

How does your company make its agents’ jobs easier?

We operate on the belief that we only succeed as a company when our agents succeed, so we’re passionate about providing value and support so that their businesses and families can thrive. That means saving them time or making their jobs easier by taking things off their plate so that they can focus on doing the things only they can do. Another core thing we talk a lot about is the go-giver mindset, which is rooted in the idea that when you give and give and then give some more, good things happen.

As we approach the end of 2026 and head into a new year, what are you most looking forward to?

Our business is constantly changing, so it’s all about embracing that and looking forward to how we’re going to grow and get better while at the same time serving our agents better. It’s all about controlling what we can control. I’m optimistic about the future.

For more information, please visit https://www.BerkshireHathawayhs.com.

Paige Tepping is RISMedia’s senior managing editor.

The Ford Mustang GTD 1901 Takes Inspiration From a 125-Year-Old Race Car

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Automotive  |  Read Source →

Our car experts choose every product we feature. We may earn money from the links on this page.

The Blue Oval is celebrating a century and a quarter of racing history with a special-edition Mustang GTD.

Henry Ford was racing cars before the Mustang, GT40, or even the Ford Motor Company itself existed. Back in 1901, he won his first race behind the wheel of a two-cylinder race car nicknamed “Sweepstakes,” a car that produced just 26 horsepower. 125 years later, Ford has unveiled the 815-horsepower 2028 Mustang GTD 1901—a special edition inspired by the car that helped launch Henry Ford's automotive empire.

The Mustang GTD 1901 has a 789-horsepower advantage over the Sweepstakes car, but the design inspiration is clear. The Mustang GTD 1901 features a distinctive Royal Red exterior and a wide, off-center black racing stripe featuring a bright red centerline. The color combination reverses the original race car's black body and deep red chassis components. Additional diagonal graphics and exposed carbon-fiber elements, including the front splitter, rocker panels, rear diffuser, and wing, complete the exterior look.

Have a seat inside the Mustang GTD 1901, and you immediately realize this is not an ordinary GTD—that is, if there is even such a thing as an ordinary GTD. The first thing you notice are the Mahogany Red leather seats with Race Red stitching. Keep looking, and you’ll see there are also matching red accents on the seatbelts, while the steering wheel receives Mahogany Red faux suede.

The Performance Package is standard equipment on the Mustang GTD 1901. Options include the magnesium wheels, which reduce the unsprung weight by 50.3 pounds. If you want to spice up the interior, you can order the optional titanium trim package, which gets you redesigned titanium shifter paddles that have been lengthened for the 2028 model year. The titanium trim in the GTD, by the way? It comes from retired F-22 fighter jets.

Ford has not announced how many Mustang GTD 1901’s will be built, or how much they will cost. But we wouldn’t count on seeing too many at your local track day.

A lot has changed since Henry Ford won his first race; horsepower figures, aerodynamics, and suspension technology have come a long way. But the desire of the company that bears his name to prove itself on the racetrack apparently hasn't changed much at all, and that’s something we’re certainly thankful for.

Pedestal AI raises $4.7 million to agentify the supply chain

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Delivery supply chains are the lifeblood of contemporary businesses — and a new startup is betting that purpose-built AI agents can make them run a lot more smoothly.

On Tuesday, Pedestal AI emerged from stealth with $4.7 million in seed funding. The company’s goal is to use agentic AI to make supply chains faster and more reliable — a plan that’s already brought them together with Fortune 500 companies and some of the largest shipping firms in the world.

The seed round was led by Eclipse, with participation from RRE, Converge VC, Timescale Ventures, and VitalStage. Pedestal says the funding will be used to expand its product and engineering time, grow the marketing organization, and support additional deployments.

In practice, much of Pedestal’s work is about removing friction from the shipping process — with agentic technology used as a means to that end. In one case, the company reduced missed deliveries for a major furniture company by 20% by deploying agents to take on some of the work that drivers were supposed to do. The agentic system they built would call customers 30 minutes before a delivery and collect any special instructions; if the call didn’t go through, a text would be sent from a separate system. Along the way, the deployment collected a wealth of data about friction points in the process, which could be used to optimize the system even further.

CEO Raj Aggarwal says contemporary AI systems are particularly useful for dealing with that data. “The challenge is that the data is messy and it’s constantly changing,” Aggarwal told TechCrunch. “It’s disparate because it sits in so many legacy systems. That’s what makes it hard.”

Supply chain AI has become a particularly hot space in recent months as alumni from autonomous driving teams look to launch their own startups. The supply chain startup Atomic, launched by a team of Tesla alumni, came out of stealth in 2025 with an emphasis on streamlining inventory. Another venture called Loop was launched by Uber alums to focus on predictive analytics.

As Aggarwal sees it, deliveries are particularly well-suited to training reinforcement learning systems. Because of their high volumes and easily measured outcomes, it’s easy to fine-tune contemporary AI systems toward what’s working and away from what isn’t.

Perhaps most importantly, it’s the kind of problem that can have an immediate effect on a client’s bottom line, making it easy to justify the expense.

“If you can get it right,” he told TechCrunch, “you can have really big impact.”

The Nvidia Scandal Could Upend Chip Sales to China. Trump Wants to Keep Them Going

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After days of political fallout from former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s affair with a suspected Chinese intelligence asset, President Donald Trump is defending commerce secretary Howard Lutnick.

Last week, Lutnick came under fire from treasury secretary Scott Bessent for not aggressively imposing export restrictions on advanced AI chips to China.

But Trump’s defense of Lutnick, sources tell WIRED, reflects the president's own view that allowing the sale of some advanced AI chips to China can keep China reliant on US technology and simultaneously boost US tech stocks.

Lutnick “was doing that because if you didn’t delay it, if you didn’t do something, you could have a blowup with China. We’re having a very good relationship with China right now,” Trump said ahead of a multiday trip to Asia and a meeting on Thursday with Chinese leader Xi Jinping.

Trump convened an Oval Office meeting on November 5 to discuss the political fallout from Huang’s affair with Alice Huang, the former Megaspeed executive whom some in the US government believe was recruited to spy for China, according to two sources briefed on the discussion.

Megaspeed received advanced AI chips manufactured by Nvidia through several layers of companies that started with a subsidiary of a blacklisted Chinese server conglomerate called Inspur, The New York Times has reported.

In the meeting, the sources say, Bessent confronted Lutnick, asking why Megaspeed was not added to the entity list faster, given that secretary of state Marco Rubio and US trade representative Jamieson Greer, among others, had already agreed it was the correct move.

Lutnick responded that he would defer to Trump and would add Megaspeed to the entity list if that was what the president wanted, the sources say.

But Trump settled on a third approach: for Lutnick to work on introducing a new interim trade rule to stop Chinese AI labs from using advanced AI chips remotely by leasing their compute power from data centers in Southeast Asia.

In a statement to WIRED, a White House spokesperson said the new rule would “crack down on the smuggling of advanced US SI chips” and “help ensure that America’s most advanced chips go first to US SI innovators, not Chinese shell companies.” (Superintelligence, or SI, is the administration’s term for artificial intelligence.)

A senior White House official added that Trump has been adamant that Chinese firms cannot acquire the most advanced Nvidia chips, known as Blackwells and Rubins. Those are different from the H200-type chips, built on previous-generation Hopper architecture, that Megaspeed was acquiring.

Lutnick’s position appears to have been based on a series of considerations, the sources say. For one, Megaspeed has already had the chips for months, and blacklisting the company would be akin to playing whack-a-mole unless the administration dealt with the underlying issue of Chinese firms getting remote access to US chips.

Meanwhile, Lutnick has moved cautiously on any new export restrictions—essentially waiting to get explicit approval from Trump—after he faced heavy criticism in 2025 for expanding restrictions against firms with Chinese ties, the sources said.

In September 2025, the Commerce Department’s Bureau of Industry and Security started aggressively using the so-called affiliates rule, which automatically extends trade restrictions to any company that is 50 percent owned by a company already on the US entity list or the military end-user list.

But the move prompted immediate retaliation by China, which suspended exports of critical rare earth metals to the US and, the sources said, prompted a wave of internal criticism accusing the Commerce Department of having sparked a trade war.

After the Busan summit at the end of October 2025, where Trump and Chinese president Xi Jinping agreed to a trade truce, the White House instructed Commerce to halt the use of the affiliates rule and clear new export controls in advance, the sources said.

The suspension of the affiliates rule—which came almost as soon as it was implemented—may have had a knock-on effect on companies like Megaspeed, one person familiar with deliberations said.

Inspur, the Chinese server conglomerate connected to Megaspeed, operated a subsidiary called Aivres, which, as a California-based company, could legally buy restricted Nvidia chips without triggering direct export bans and ship them internationally to Southeast Asia.

In total, Aivres transferred billions of dollars’ worth of restricted Nvidia chips, including the powerful H200 chips, to Megaspeed’s subsidiaries.

Instead of shipping the physical chips into China, Megaspeed hosted servers at data centers in Malaysia and Indonesia—then rented out compute power to major Chinese companies. A spokesperson for Aivres said in a statement that the company complies with all US export controls.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/optum-rx-rolls-out-new-pharmacy-tools-aimed-transparency" hreflang="en">Optum Rx rolls out new pharmacy tools aimed at transparency</a>

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Optum Rx rolls out new pharmacy tools aimed at transparency" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Earlier this year, Optum Rx teased the rollout of new pharmacy tools designed to address medication cost and access.

Now, the company is offering a look at how those tools work.

The pharmacy benefit manager said Tuesday that the tools build on Optum's new and more transparent pharmacy model, which the company announced in May and shifts to a fee-based structure. The model moves away from more traditional approaches linked to manufacturer pricing and prescription volume and offers clients a clearly defined fee structure.

The first platform, called Shop MyScript, allows members to compare prices for their medications as well as their options for pharmacies or delivery before the prescription is dispensed. The tool will notify members of potential cost savings shortly after a script is written.

In addition, Shop MyScript embeds in the prescribing workflow, Optum said, so that the member does not have to make comparisons after the fact. If they choose to keep the existing prescription, it will then be sent to the pharmacy.

Optum said that early adopters have averaged $28 in savings through the program.

"People should have clear information about their medication costs and options when they can actually use it, not after a decision has already been made," said Katte Carey, president of Optum Rx, in the announcement. "These tools give people more information earlier in their journey, so they can make choices that work for them and get the support they need along the way."

In addition, the pharmacy benefit manager announced Optum Assistant, a conversational AI guide that helps members complete routine tasks and track down key information. The tool can triage concerns on a range of topics, from questions about medication and benefits to pharmacy experience challenges.

If the AI guide can't address the individual's needs, they can choose to be connected to live support to find a solution. Optum said about 80% of conversations using the tool led to a resolution without needing a live advocate.

The final new launch aims to support cash-pay options, according to the announcement.

Optum Price Wise will offer members a complete cost breakdown, including the price of a drug and any fees. That total, all-in price allows members to fully understand what their options may be, and make the choice that works best for them at the pharmacy counters.

The PBM said new tools like these build on Optum's new and more transparent pharmacy model, which the company announced in May and shifts to a fee-based structure. The model moves away from more traditional approaches linked to manufacturer pricing and prescription volume and offers clients a clearly defined fee structure.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/health-tech/hlth-2026-teladoc-health-expands-weight-care-program-new-employer-offering" hreflang="en">HLTH 2026: Teladoc Health's weight care program lets employers partially contribute to GLP-1 costs</a>

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
HLTH 2026: Teladoc Health's weight care program lets employers partially contribute to GLP-1 costs" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Teladoc Health is expanding its Comprehensive Weight Care program with a new option that allows employers to contribute to GLP-1 costs outside of traditional pharmacy benefit designs.

Through the new offering, employers can give access to medications from Eli Lilly and Novo Nordisk—accompanied by support from licensed professionals, personalized lifestyle coaching, artificial intelligence-enabled food logging and more.

With each plan year, employers will set a fixed payment per employee per fill that applies consistently across covered medications, the company said. Employees then pay the remaining balance for each fill. Teladoc manages eligibility, program oversight and consolidated invoicing.

Pharmacy claims are administered on a 100% pass-through basis, including eligibility verification and audit-ready claims documentation.

Moreover, employers can opt in to tie contributions to program engagement, using metrics like food logging and regular weigh-ins, executives say.

The offering is available now, with initial contracted clients slated to launch programs for their employees beginning Jan. 1, 2027.

“Employers want to provide benefits that help their workforce achieve their health goals and need flexible strategies that work for their organizations,” said Kelly Bliss, Teladoc Health U.S. Group Health president, in a statement. “We are helping employers meet the needs of their employees while providing greater predictability and transparency around healthcare spending. Importantly, we give people a trusted path to high-quality care and affordable access to FDA-approved, authentic medication for meaningful, sustained health outcomes.”

Alongside the announcement, the company also unveiled new research examining two years of weight-loss outcomes among nearly 23,000 members enrolled in its programs.

Results indicated “clinically meaningful weight loss” increased over time, with 47.7% of members achieving at least 5% weight loss by 24 months. And among those who lost weight during their first year, 53% continued losing additional weight during the second.

FAA pauses installation of new routers after ground stop

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Federal Aviation Administration said Tuesday it would pause the installation of new routers after problems with the technology led the agency to issue a rare nationwide ground stop on Friday.

Tens of thousands of flights were affected by the ground stop, which halted departing flights for more than four hours. It was the longest halt to U.S. flight departures since 9/11.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said Sunday that an overloaded router installed in southern Arkansas as part of an upgrade to a system overseen by L3Harris Technologies appeared to overwhelm systems that feed radar and other data for air traffic control.

The Wall Street Journal first reported the news about the pause.

The FAA and Department of Transportation have said L3Harris was working to convert the communications network from copper wires to fiber optic.

Democratic members of the Senate Committee on Commerce, Science and Transportation this week asked the FAA and the Department of Transportation for clarity on what went wrong.

"Identifying the device that failed does not establish why it failed," the senators wrote in a statement. "Modernization cannot come at the expense of operational safety."

The agencies have said they are working to figure out the root cause of the issue and have reiterated that there is no indication of a cyberattack or outside threat.

The senators also asked for a briefing with the FAA, L3Harris and Juniper Networks, which is making the new devices, by Monday. They asked the companies to lay out why the operational systems lacked sufficient redundancy and answer other questions about what led to the outage.

CNBC has reached out to L3Harris and Juniper Networks for comment.

Duffy on Friday called for Congress to approve $50 billion in funding for upgrades to the aging U.S. air traffic control system, more than he has previously asked for. Democratic lawmakers pushed back on the request, citing unspent money from existing funding, which includes Congress' approval last year of $12.5 billion for airport and air traffic control improvements over five years.

Aging equipment and a shortage of air traffic controllers have for years angered airline executives, who have complained about the cost of resulting disruptions.

— CNBC's Phil LeBeau and Leslie Josephs contributed to this report.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/payer-mix-non-labor-expenses-dragged-hospital-margins-september" hreflang="en">Payer mix, non-labor expenses dragged hospital margins in September</a>

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Payer mix, non-labor expenses dragged hospital margins in September" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Hospitals’ operations took another dip in September amid a slew of ongoing headwinds including weakened payer mix and rising costs, healthcare advisory firm Kaufman Hall reported alongside warnings of a “historically large portion” of the nation’s hospitals running on negative or near-zero operating margins.

The firm’s latest monthly benchmark report, released Tuesday, showed a calendar year-to-date operating margin index of 1.4% and a single-month operating margin index of 2.4% in September, the latter of which was a decline from August’s 3.0% operating margin (all including health system allocations for shared services costs).

The performance decline wasn’t for want of patients. Daily discharges and adjusted discharges both rose 3% from August to September, and are generally above the numbers from last year. Operating room minutes per calendar day also rose 6% from August—an encouraging sign for hospitals whose surgical slowdowns had hamstrung acuity—but remain 1% below September of last year.

The increased foot traffic also grew revenues, with daily net operating revenue rising 4% from August and 6% year over year while gross operating revenue rose 4% month to month and 8% year over year. Much of the increase came on the outpatient side, where daily revenue increased 5% from August and 10% compared to September 2025. Net patient service revenue per adjusted discharge was more mixed, rising 2% from August but remaining 1% below September 2025.

However, hospitals’ margins are being challenged by persistent bad debt and charity care burdens, Kaufman Hall’s data show. Daily bad debt and charity care may have remained flat from August, but is 17% higher than September 2025 and 16% higher when comparing both years’ first nine months. As a percentage of gross operating revenue, they’re 6% higher than Sept. 2025 and 8% higher year to date.

On the spending side, daily total expense rose 3% from August and is 5% higher than September 2025, primarily reflecting the heightened volumes even as hospitals held their average length of stay flat month over month or 1% shorter year over year. The expenses were more pronounced on the non-labor side, where increases on a per adjusted discharge basis were higher than labor’s per adjusted discharge decline.

"Hospitals continue to operate in an environment marked by significant financial variability," Erik Swanson, managing director and Data and Analytics group leader at Kaufman Hall, said in a release. “Many organizations continue to face mounting pressure from uncompensated care and rising expenses, underscoring the importance of proactive operational and financial strategies that build long-term resilience."

Another amplifying market trend is the “bifurcation of hospital performance” often highlighted by the firm and others that monitor multi-organization financial trends, such as credit rating agencies.

"The margin gap between higher and lower-performing hospitals has widened to 19 percentage points, with a historically large portion of hospitals operating at negative or very thin margins,” the report reads. “Even slight revenue reductions threaten organizations, which face increasingly difficult decisions on future operations, care delivery, and expense control.”

The firm encouraged hospitals to take “deliberate, proactive steps” to strengthen their financial situations, particularly as macro reimbursement trends stiffen and the site of care delivery shifts from the hospital to outpatient settings.

Kaufman Hall’s hospital reports pull data from more than 1,300 nationwide hospitals as collected by parent company Strata Decision Technology.

Accompanying the monthly hospital benchmark was also Kaufman Hall’s quarterly numbers on more than 200,000 employed physicians and advanced practice providers, which painted a much rosier picture for health system and hospital employers.

It showed, for the first time in five years of data collection, that healthcare employers’ investment/subsidy per provider and per physician has declined year over year (by 1%), and “across every cohort, including primary care, medical specialty, surgical specialty and hospital-based specialty.”

Kaufman Hall attributed the rare turnaround to revenue growth and sustained productivity increases that together outpaced expenses.

"Medical groups made meaningful performance improvements this quarter, achieving gains in revenue, productivity and thoughtful workforce management," Matthew Bates, managing director and Physician Enterprise Service Line leader at Kaufman Hall, said in a release. "The growing utilization of [advanced practice providers] and ongoing focus on operational and team effectiveness are helping organizations manage financial pressures while increasing access to care.”

→ Baca artikel asli

U.S. officially loses measles elimination status amid ongoing outbreaks

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The U.S. on Tuesday lost its official designation as a measles-free nation, more than 25 years after it eliminated the highly contagious virus.

The Pan American Health Organization announced the decision, which came after an independent panel of eight scientific experts convened last week to consider the country's epidemiological evidence. The director of PAHO, the regional branch of the World Health Organization, made the final call that measles is endemic — or always present — in the U.S. following 12 months of continuous transmission.

In other words, measles is spreading regularly in the country again, with ongoing outbreaks sparking new ones.

"The commission concluded that endemic measles transmission has now been reestablished in Bolivia, Mexico and the United States, and recommended that their measles elimination status be withdrawn — a recommendation which I have accepted," Dr. Jarbas Barbosa, PAHO director, said during a news briefing on Tuesday.

It's a devastating step backward for American public health, as declining childhood vaccination rates and vaccine hesitancy fuel outbreaks across the country that have sickened thousands of people and led to a tragic string of preventable deaths. Among the deaths where vaccination status was reported, all were unvaccinated.

More than 5,400 measles cases had been reported nationwide in 2026 as of mid-November, marking the country's worst outbreak in more than three decades. States have confirmed 10 deaths associated with the disease so far in 2026: seven in Pennsylvania, one in New York and two in Wisconsin.

The commission found that out of 52 U.S. states and territories it tracked, 48 experienced measles outbreaks during the reporting period between January 2025 and January 2026, PAHO Assistant Director Dr. Rhonda Sealey-Thomas said during the briefing. But Barbosa said countries are already taking action to control outbreaks, such as strengthening vaccination, surveillance and outbreak response efforts.

The U.S. in particular has ramped up vaccination efforts to close immunity gaps, particularly in areas with low immunization coverage experiencing outbreaks, said Dr. Daniel Salas, executive manager of PAHO's Special Program for Comprehensive Immunization. He said PAHO expects the U.S. to present its corrective action plan in the coming weeks, outlining strategies to regain its designation.

In a Tuesday release, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention said it will continue to ensure all communities have the resources to prevent and respond to outbreaks and is committed to working with states and other local partners to restore the U.S.'s measles elimination status.

CDC Director Dr. Erica Schwartz said in the release that the measles elimination designation "is not what protects Americans. Vaccination does." She said the agency has invested $100 million in state and local health departments to reach communities where transmission is occurring.

Last week, Schwartz told Reuters that the agency is going to be "doing the same exact thing that we've been doing today" if the U.S. loses its measles-free status.

The decision also poses a new test for the Trump administration and its Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who has taken steps to change recommendations for the childhood measles, mumps and rubella, or MMR, vaccine and has a long history of questioning its safety without evidence.

CNBC previously reported on how the return of measles in the U.S. exposes weaknesses in the nation's public health system that raise concerns about the return of other eliminated diseases. The resurgence of measles exposes eroding trust in health institutions and growing challenges in persuading people to protect themselves, their children and their communities.

Public health experts previously told CNBC that the return of measles ultimately boils down to fewer children getting vaccinated.

The MMR vaccine is about 97% effective against measles after two doses, while a single dose is 93% effective, according to the CDC. Yet data aggregated by the CDC shows that vaccination coverage for that shot among U.S. kindergarteners fell to 92.4% in the 2025-2026 school year, which is below the 95% threshold public health officials generally recommend to prevent sustained transmission.

The American Medical Association urged the public to get vaccinated after PAHO's announcement.

"We must treat the current measles outbreaks as an urgent call to rebuild trust in science-based recommendations and restore strong, fully-funded public health infrastructure to protect families and communities and prevent the return of diseases once eliminated," AMA President Dr. Willie Underwood said in a statement.

In a separate statement, the pharmaceutical industry's largest lobbying group called on policymakers to provide "clear, accurate information about the seriousness of measles infection and the effectiveness of vaccines against it, restore and sustain high vaccination coverage in every community, strengthen vaccination efforts, and invest in disease surveillance and rapid outbreak response."

"We have the tools to eliminate measles again and what we need now is the commitment to use them," said Dr. Michael Ybarra, chief medical officer of the Pharmaceutical Research and Manufacturers of America.

Measles isn't just a public health threat — it's also a huge financial burden on the healthcare system, CNBC reported.

Based on this year's case count, a new tool developed by researchers at Johns Hopkins University estimates an economic cost of nearly $83 million for the outbreaks. That total includes $56 million in public health response costs, almost $25 million in lost productivity and more than $1.8 million in direct medical expenses.

The U.S. now joins Canada, Spain and the U.K., which all recently lost their measles-free status after a review by experts at the World Health Organization. While the U.S. dropped out of the WHO, it remains a member of PAHO, its Americas branch.

In his first public statement since resigning as Nvidia CEO last week, Jensen Huang struck a contrite tone, saying that he is committed to cooperating with the board's investigation into his relationship with an alleged Chinese intelligence contact.

"I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret," Huang said in the written statement obtained by CNBC's David Faber. "I own my mistakes completely, and I am committed to cooperating with the Board's independent investigation."

Huang said he had seen support from investors and employees and was willing to come back as CEO to help the company navigate "complex technology, market, competitive, and geopolitical dynamics."

"If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO," Huang said in the statement.

Sources told CNBC Monday that the board is not considering bringing Huang back as CEO.

Many in the tech world have called for Huang to return as Nvidia CEO, putting together an online petition with more than 180,000 signatures.

Last week, Huang resigned from Nvidia after 33 years as CEO. The resignation came days after Huang, Nvidia's founder, admitted to an affair, and news stories alleged he had a liason with Alice Huang, who worked for a customer and is alleged to have contact with Chinese intelligence. Jensen Huang also reportedly had romances with employees at Nvidia.

Nvidia has hired law firm Wachtell Lipton to lead a probe into Huang's conduct and whether it compromised national security or company secrets.

I love our country, and I love NVIDIA.

I did not live up to the standards expected of everyone at NVIDIA, and I have made serious mistakes in my personal life. I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret. I own my mistakes completely, and I am committed to cooperating with the Board's independent investigation.

Since my departure, I've seen an outpouring of calls from NVIDIA's employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO. Many continue to reach out to me directly as the company navigates complex technology, market, competitive, and geopolitical dynamics. NVIDIA is a once-in-a-generation company. If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.

Thank you again to everyone who has shared their good wishes. I am profoundly humbled by your support. To my family: I love you, and I will spend the rest of my life being worthy of yours.

Ecuadorean Fishermen Who Survived U.S. Boat Strike Sue Trump Administration

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

The first lawsuit by strike survivors targets a secretive U.S. military campaign that has killed more than 240 people.

By Genevieve Glatsky and José María León Cabrera

Genevieve Glatsky reported from Bogotá, Colombia, and José María León Cabrera reported from Quito, Ecuador.

Ecuadorean fishermen sued the United States in U.S. federal court this week, claiming American military drones bombed their vessel in the Pacific Ocean earlier this year. It is the first lawsuit by survivors of a boat strike who are seeking accountability for a Trump administration campaign that has been cloaked in secrecy.

Since September 2025, U.S. forces have carried out 72 strikes against alleged drug-trafficking boats, killing at least 244 people, a tally by The New York Times shows.

Filed Monday in Pittsburgh, the suit seeks financial damages for 17 fisherman from the port city of Manta, Ecuador, and an order halting U.S. strikes on small boats in the Caribbean and eastern Pacific.

Relatives of two Trinidadians killed in a strike filed a similar suit in January over a strike that President Trump announced last year.

But unlike dozens of strikes that Washington has publicly acknowledged, including posting videos on social media, the suit filed Monday is over one of three unacknowledged strikes in the Pacific. In August, The Times reported that the Pentagon and Coast Guard denied any involvement in the three episodes, leaving unanswered questions about whether U.S. forces, an ally or private military contractors carried them out.

The fishermen made up the crew of a boat that left Manta on March 2, 2026, fishing near the Galápagos Islands, according to the complaint. While returning to shore on March 17, the Ecuadorean Navy intercepted and boarded the boat for a two-hour inspection.

His argument was that the solutions to some of the most pressing environmental challenges, such as clean air, could come from scaling up technology that already exists.

That this year the heir to the British throne has brought his six-year-old Earthshot Prize, which aims to promote creative solutions to the climate crisis, to India seems fitting. This country, with its 1.4 billion people, is a frontier for both the devastating impact of an increasingly erratic climate (more than 80 percent of the region around Mumbai is in drought), and the search for ways to mitigate that impact.

The prize’s five winners were announced at a ceremony hosted by the Indian filmmaker Karan Johar. Each will receive one million pounds, about $1.3 million.

The organizers also hope to offer wider benefits to the winners, in the form of recognition and a connection to business and political leaders.

“The people with the answers are already out there,” Prince William told a gathering of the finalists on Monday. “Our task is to find them, back them and help them scale.”

Democrats are preparing multiple probes of Trump's Venezuela oil deal

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

House and Senate committees are laying groundwork to examine the 35% U.S. stake in North American Blue Energy Partners

Congressional Democrats are lining up several investigations of the Trump administration’s agreement with Alejandro Betancourt’s North American Blue Energy Partners (NABEP), according to Bloomberg. Democrats on panels including the House Oversight Committee and the Senate Armed Services Committee have started preparing for those inquiries.

The agreement, announced in August, would hand NABEP a century-long concession covering 17 oil fields in Venezuela, while the U.S. would receive a 35% ownership share of the company, according to Bloomberg. The U.S. would be entitled to buy a fifth of what the fields pump, priced at cost.

Rep. Robert Garcia, a California Democrat expected to take the helm of the House Oversight Committee next year, said the aim is to establish the facts. “We want to go in there and find out exactly what happened,” Garcia told Bloomberg. He added that Democrats also want to pass legislation to prevent any abuse in the future.

Sen. Jack Reed, a Rhode Island Democrat and the top Democrat on the Senate Armed Services Committee, said his panel is among those planning investigations. “If we went in there with Maduro as an excuse, but the real goal is to take oil, that is to me wrong,” Reed told Bloomberg. Sen. Richard Blumenthal, a Connecticut Democrat set to chair the Permanent Subcommittee on Investigations, said he is concerned about “the potential for corruption.” A spokesperson for Sen. Martin Heinrich, a New Mexico Democrat expected to take over the Senate energy panel next year, said he plans to keep overseeing the deal, according to Bloomberg.

Following their midterm election victories, Democrats in both chambers will have the authority to summon administration officials for testimony, demand records and, if needed, issue subpoenas, according to Bloomberg. Their questions center on the contract's provisions, the compensation the U.S. extended to NABEP for its equity stake and the mechanism for supervising oil revenue. Democrats have also faulted the administration for keeping the text under wraps and questioned Betancourt's role, citing money-laundering and corruption probes that have targeted him in the U.S., Spain and Switzerland. Betancourt denies any wrongdoing and has faced no charges.

House Natural Resources Committee Democrats intend to carry on an inquiry launched earlier in the year, a staffer on the committee told Bloomberg. The Senate Foreign Relations Committee is also expected to launch an investigation.

Four ranking Senate Democrats, including Reed and Heinrich, had challenged the legality of the Pentagon's plan to take a 35% stake in NABEP in a letter to top administration officials. They demanded the full text of the deal before it was finalized. House Democrats sent a similar letter.

White House spokesperson Taylor Rogers defended the deal. "This deal is a major win for both the United States and Venezuela," Rogers said, according to Bloomberg. "If Democrats choose to pursue sham investigations over commonsense victories, they are choosing to work against the American people."

The administration has argued the deal would cut gasoline prices for Americans and give the government a route to restocking the strategic oil reserve, according to Bloomberg. A NABEP spokesperson told Bloomberg taxpayers paid nothing for the U.S. stake and that U.S. citizens must hold most of the seats on NABEP's board.
