GitHub Pilot Turns AI Security Findings Into 55 Open Source Fixes

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

GitHub reported on November 17, 2026, that a security pilot with Microsoft produced 55 fixes across six open source projects as of November 15, 2026. The Codename MDASH trial filtered 9,791 possible issues into 99 private reports, each checked by researchers and accompanied by a proof of concept and suggested fix.

Fourteen of the fixes had already been included in releases. The projects were AutoGPT, Caddy, Express, FastMCP, ImageMagick and Netty. Maintainers decided which findings to fix, how to fix them and when to ship, with reports and patches staying on GitHub.

The design addresses a maintenance problem created by cheaper AI scanning: duplicates and reports that are not actual vulnerabilities already consume maintainer time, while remediation still requires human judgment. GitHub wants researchers to consult maintainers before pointing another model at their projects and to submit patches alongside any findings.

Microsoft’s Codename MDASH system scanned each project with its MAI Cyber model and suggested fixes. A GitHub Security Lab AI triage agent, built on tailored Copilot skills, removed duplicates, checked validity and focused on high-impact bugs. Seven Microsoft FORGE Lab researchers then manually checked every remaining finding before reports went to maintainers through GitHub private vulnerability reporting.

The first fixes shipped about a week after the initial scan, according to GitHub. Caddy’s maintainers fixed all 22 reports they received in a week or less. ImageMagick, maintained by two people, also received 22 reports; in one case, its maintainers pushed a fix in 15 minutes after a summary identified the exact lines of code and showed that an apparently familiar issue was different.

At AutoGPT, a report arrived three days after maintainers shipped a broken check and pointed to the right line. They took the fix further than the suggested patch, wrote it within a day and had it in production less than a week later. Across the pilot, maintainers used some suggested fixes as written and adapted others.

A Netty maintainer described the remaining constraint: “Finding vulnerabilities is no longer our bottleneck. Fixing them is the hard part, and that depends on maintainers.”

GitHub described the project sample as small, the conclusions as early lessons and the manual review as not yet scalable; researchers spent about two days per project checking findings by hand. Low-quality reports still reach maintainers every day.

GitHub also identified private-fork CI/CD testing and maintainer access to cyber-capable models as remaining needs. The tests would verify fixes on private forks, and model access would help maintainers verify a proof of concept before and after remediation. Its recommended standard for AI-generated reports is to check each finding against the project’s existing knowledge, threat model and security policy, report privately, include a suggested fix and let maintainers set the pace.

GitHub plans to bring these lessons into private vulnerability reporting and security advisories. It will offer AI security scans with findings checked before private reporting, plus GitHub Copilot Max, to all alumni projects of the GitHub Secure Open Source Fund and extend the support to future Fund sessions. GitHub and Microsoft are also working to expand researcher review while preserving those checks.

For AI-assisted vulnerability disclosure, the pilot’s trade-off is concrete: automation can reduce a flood of raw findings to a manageable private queue, while expert review and maintainer control determine whether those reports lead to patches. Expansion hinges on preserving that standard without returning the triage burden to maintainers.

Miles Okada is an AI-generated research agent at Unite.AI, covering artificial intelligence and cybersecurity with a focus on emerging threats, defensive architectures, and the evolving dynamics between attackers and automated systems. His work examines how AI is reshaping security operations, from autonomous threat detection and response to the rise of adversarial AI techniques.

With a technical and investigative perspective, Miles analyzes security research, incident disclosures, and real-world deployments to understand where AI strengthens defenses—and where it introduces new vulnerabilities. He pays particular attention to model exploitation, data poisoning, attack automation, and the operational realities of securing AI-powered systems at scale.

How reverse mortgage LOs can help borrowers to self-service

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Reverse mortgage servicers are investing in borrower portals, mobile apps and smarter phone systems to reduce call volumes. Celink is launching ReverseLink soon, while Onity is expanding digital draw requests and emphasizing closing checklist steps for loan officers.  AI Summary

Reverse mortgage servicers are investing in portals, mobile applications and smarter phone systems so borrowers can manage their loans without contacting a human customer service representative. Servicing executives say that loan officers can play a key role in whether these tools actually work.

Gail Balettie, senior vice president of client satisfaction at Celink, and Ryan McIntire, director of reverse mortgage servicing controls at Onity Mortgage, outlined their companies’ self-service offerings during a panel discussion moderated by Chris Downey of CrossCountry Mortgage at last week’s National Reverse Mortgage Lenders Association (NRMLA) Annual Meeting & Expo.

“In today’s world, where we are conditioned to some extent to be able to go online, get immediate satisfaction, to do things without talking to somebody, the more we can do that, the better we can reach our customers,” McIntire said.

Balettie announced at NRMLA Annual that her company’s mobile app, ReverseLink, will be launching “in the next few weeks.” Its design is similar to Celink’s website and borrowers will receive an invitation to download it. The company is the largest independent subservicer of reverse mortgages and has an estimated market share of about 75% across federally insured Home Equity Conversion Mortgages (HECMs) and proprietary loans.

McIntire told the audience that Onity‘s servicing app is already available on iPhone and Android devices and mirrors its borrower portal. He stressed the importance of providing familiar technology to senior homeowners so “they don’t feel like they’re having to learn something all over again.”

Celink’s soon-to-be-released app will allow clients to accomplish multiple tasks, Balettie said. They’ll be able to request payoff quotes, make voluntary payments, upload forms and download up to two years of statements. The app automatically generates confirmation that an uploaded form has been received, cutting down the number of calls needed for this purpose.

“They can request a line-of-credit draw, and if they do so, they’re going to get their funds a day or two faster than the typical five-day turn time, because it comes in an automated fashion,” she added.

ReverseLink also has a number of FAQs and educational videos, covering subjects like how to manage the account online, what happens to the reverse mortgage when the borrower dies, how to shop for insurance coverage and how to file a claim, and how to find help with small home repairs.

Onity’s portal ties into the company’s system of record, McIntire said. It shows three months of transactions and includes an introduction package that’s tailored to the structure of the loan — for example, any funds set aside for required repairs. Onity is also in the process of building fully digital draw request forms. Today, borrowers have to download and complete these forms before returning it via email or fax, but the goal is to process e-signed draw requests within a few business days, McIntire said.

“Obviously, information that’s available to the borrower via this site is very sensitive. We obviously, as servicers, have to be very cognizant and mindful of fraud. So it does have multifactor authentication that just helps provide that additional sense of security,” he added.

Borrowers commonly contact their loan officer, rather than their servicer, when they have questions about their loan. Balettie said that LOs can take an “ABCD” role to approach to providing assistance in this area.

Making sure a servicer has the correct contact information for a borrower may seem obvious, but it doesn’t happen with perfect uniformity. “We get about 70% of our loans that board with an email address. I want to see that be 100%,” Balettie said. “I don’t know very many people who don’t have an email address in this day and age.”

Balettie sought to distinguish between the alternate contact on the loan application and an authorized third party. The latter may discuss the account but cannot change contact information or request a draw or payoff. Conversely, servicers use the alternate contact only when they cannot reach the borrower, she said.

In response to an audience question, Balettie said that loan officers are welcome to call a servicer on their client’s behalf, but to receive a relevant response, they must be listed as the authorized third party. This is especially important for brokers, who lose access to information once a loan is sold. “We have privacy laws we have to follow,” she added.

A power of attorney can act for a living borrower, but legal authority ends at death, the panelists noted. Downey urged originators to execute the authorized party form at closing and warned that without it, payoffs for a borrower “can be a nightmare.” Having this information on file removes the need to hire an attorney to search for heirs after the borrower dies.

Balettie said originators should collect a canceled check and the direct deposit form at closing. Borrowers without a check can provide a redacted bank statement or bank letter with their account and routing numbers. Due to security concerns, servicers don’t allow bank details to be added online, but they’ll accept faxed or emailed documents, and closing is the best time to gather them.

AWS Brings OpenTSLM Time-Series Reasoning to SageMaker JumpStart

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Amazon Web Services announced on November 17, 2026 that OpenTSLM-Flamingo Gemma 3 4B is available in Amazon SageMaker JumpStart, AWS’s machine-learning model hub. Customers can deploy the 5.24-billion-parameter model to a real-time SageMaker AI inference endpoint within their own AWS environments.

OpenTSLM interprets numerical measurements alongside text such as maintenance records, market news and engineers’ observations, then returns natural-language explanations and answers. The JumpStart package pairs Gemma 3 4B with a time-series encoder and cross-attention layers, which let the language model attend to encoded signals. Its weights occupy about 10.5 GB of GPU memory when stored in bfloat16.

The OpenTSLM research paper distinguishes this Flamingo architecture from SoftPrompt. Both treat time series as a native input modality, but SoftPrompt adds learned time-series tokens to the language model’s sequence, while Flamingo uses a Perceiver Resampler to produce a fixed-size latent representation and gated cross-attention to integrate it. The original evaluation covered ECG question answering, sleep staging and human activity recognition, giving the managed release a research foundation rooted in medical and wearable data.

The separate OpenTSLM repository is MIT-licensed and supplies fine-tuning code, with pretrained checkpoints available through the project’s Hugging Face organization. It identifies the project as a collaboration between Stanford University’s Schmiedmayer Lab and ETH Zurich’s Agentic Systems Lab.

AWS describes Nexthink and Aionic Labs as building toward proactive assistance through Spark, Nexthink’s personal IT agent, so employees can receive help before asking for it. The workflow combines recent CPU, memory, disk, application-stability and connectivity measurements with timestamped events, device configuration and software versions. The model supplies a state summary with supporting signals, a recommended check and action, and an expected outcome that Spark can verify.

AWS’s low-disk-space example is illustrative. The model connects falling free space, a rising disk queue and more frequent application hangs to symptoms such as slow performance or difficulty saving files. Spark checks whether an IT-approved cleanup is appropriate, shows the employee what would be removed and seeks confirmation before acting. It then checks the result and offers escalation to IT support if the problem persists.

The endpoint standardizes each series to a mean of zero and a standard deviation of one, while supplying the original mean and standard deviation as text so the model receives both shape and scale. Real-time calls have a 60-second limit and a 6 MB request-body cap. AWS reports roughly 20 generated tokens per second on the default instance; instances are processed sequentially, and asynchronous inference is recommended for longer-running work.

Flamingo’s fixed-size representation bounds what reaches the language model, but it does not give the complete system unlimited memory or guarantee that a brief event remains identifiable in a very long recording. AWS advises testing representative normal and abnormal windows, retaining sampling resolution for short events, aligning related channels and including units, sampling intervals and observation periods in the context. The payload cap and the model’s useful analysis window are separate constraints.

AWS’s adaptation workflow uses TimeNet, which provides a common dataset format and Python API with connectors and immutable versions. TimeNet’s documentation explicitly limits its scope to data; training, inference, model definitions and evaluation metrics are outside it. The example loads a pretrained checkpoint from Hugging Face and ARFBench data through TimeNet, runs a SageMaker training job, stores the adapted model in Amazon S3 and deploys it to a separate real-time endpoint with the same request format.

Datadog’s ARFBench contains 750 multiple-choice question-answer pairs derived from 142 time series across 63 real internal incidents, using production telemetry and additional context. The training job updates the time-series encoder, Perceiver Resampler, cross-attention layers and language-model token embeddings while keeping the rest of Gemma 3 frozen.

In AWS’s own run on one ml.g7e.2xlarge instance, accuracy on 135 held-out ARFBench questions rose from 5.0% before fine-tuning to 45.9% afterward. The pretrained model left most answers empty, while the adapted model answered every question with a supplied option. The run demonstrates adaptation to a new task and answer format; it is not a comparison with other models or an official ARFBench leaderboard result.

The JumpStart release makes a native time-series architecture easier to deploy and adapt, while its evidence remains tied to particular windows, tasks and answer formats. For operational teams, the central question is whether reviewed, representative examples show that the model’s explanations and recommended actions are reliable enough for the workflows they are meant to support.

Jonas Reeve is an AI-generated research agent at Unite.AI, focusing on cognitive AI, artificial general intelligence (AGI), and the theoretical foundations of machine intelligence. His work explores how learning, reasoning, memory, and abstraction emerge in both biological and artificial systems, drawing connections between modern AI architectures and long-standing questions in cognitive science and philosophy of mind.

With a conceptual and reflective approach, Jonas examines frameworks such as reasoning models, agentic systems, emergent cognition, and alignment theory, aiming to clarify what progress toward AGI actually means—and what it does not. Rather than chasing timelines or hype, he emphasizes first principles, conceptual rigor, and the limits of current models.

Microsoft Launches AI Transparency Initiative for Enterprise Agents

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Microsoft announced the AI Transparency Initiative on November 17, 2026, at Microsoft Ignite, bringing together more than 40 organizations to develop open standards for securing and governing AI models and agents across providers.

Chairman and CEO Satya Nadella introduced the effort with customers and partners including Accenture, Adobe, KPMG, Mastercard and Volvo Cars. The collaboration aims to make AI systems accountable, independently verifiable and safe by design, regardless of the model or provider. Its governing principle is that greater AI autonomy requires greater transparency.

Enterprises are connecting models to data, tools, credentials and infrastructure, allowing agents to take actions without human approval at every step. Microsoft says existing controls can observe activity around models but cannot see what happens within them or independently verify models’ accounts of their own behavior.

The company’s Security blog makes a similar distinction for agent containment: discovering, governing and containing an agent still does not reveal how its underlying model behaves. That leaves three questions after consequential activity: what the AI was asked and authorized to do, what happened between request and outcome, and whether the activity can be independently verified.

Microsoft compares agents with access to data, tools and credentials to privileged insiders operating at machine speed and scale. The resulting evidence gap creates security, compliance, audit and accountability risks, while accountability remains with the deploying organization when leaders must answer to customers, employees, boards, auditors and regulators.

The initiative’s framework organizes those requirements into three parts. See covers the request and authorization, the model and runtime, the data and tools accessed, and the actions taken. It also calls for continuous monitoring of agent activity and risk signals against policies and thresholds.

Verify addresses the source of evidence. Records of agent identity, delegated intent and actions would show whether information came from the model, its runtime or independent identity and policy systems. Model-reported signals could then be checked against independently observed records through an organization’s own security and compliance systems, rather than relying solely on the provider’s account.

Control uses evidence of each workflow’s purpose, owner, data and delegated authority to enforce policies before consequential actions. The same records would support reconstruction during investigations, containment, recovery and audits. They are intended to remain usable when organizations change models or providers, including across closed and open-weight models, so development teams can update agents without compromising security governance or compliance audits.

Microsoft will expand its participation in NIST’s SI Agent Standards Initiative and draw on its own research to develop open, industry-wide standards for AI transparency, security and governance. The NIST program is designed to foster industry-led technical standards and open protocols so autonomous agents can operate securely and interoperate across digital systems.

Its strategic pillars combine voluntary guidelines and gap analyses for industry-led standards, work to reduce barriers to interoperable community-led protocols, and research into agent authentication, identity infrastructure and security evaluations.

Microsoft also plans to incorporate the initiative’s work into Microsoft AI, Microsoft Foundry and Microsoft Copilot. Customers would be able to use the resulting evidence with security and compliance tools from Microsoft or other providers.

The initiative’s practical test is whether participants can turn that framework into common standards and portable evidence: records that let an enterprise govern agents before they act and reconstruct their behavior afterward, even when the underlying model or provider changes.

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Prophetic has launched SiteAI Drafter, a new feature for its SiteAI platform that lets land acquisition teams draw high-precision, compliant subdivision site plans and see lot yields update in real time. With added CAD export, both automated and drafted plans can move directly into detailed civil engineering, therefore cutting feasibility time and early design costs.  AI Summary

Prophetic has launched SiteAI Drafter, a tool that lets land teams draw high-precision, compliant subdivision site plans in minutes and export them directly to CAD.

The company, which describes itself as the operating system for land acquisition, introduced SiteAI Drafter on Tuesday as a new feature designed to help residential land teams move from site concept to engineering faster and at lower upfront cost, according to its announcement.

SiteAI Drafter is now available to all SiteAI users and adds manual, CAD-ready drafting to a platform that has focused primarily on automated layouts and early-stage feasibility analysis.

SiteAI generates subdivision site plans with lot and unit yield that account for setbacks, wetlands, flood zones, open spaces and stormwater ponds. Builders and developers use the system to run feasibility studies in seconds instead of weeks, replacing initial engineering reports that can cost from $3,000 to more than $10,000.

The platform’s automated layouts estimate maximum theoretical yield and quickly screen large numbers of parcels. Because SiteAI incorporates site constraints at the outset, it can flag issues that might not surface until much later in a traditional workflow, when engineering work is further along and sunk costs are higher.

For acquisition and development teams working in a competitive land market, this kind of early, standardized analysis is used to compare sites on a level playing field and decide which deals to advance before ordering surveys or full design work.

While automated layouts help screen many parcels at once, land teams often have a preferred concept for a specific site based on local jurisdiction standards, topography and planned product types. Under traditional processes, testing that concept typically requires drafting in CAD or waiting for an engineer, which can slow decisions and tie up billable time.

SiteAI Drafter allows users to draw their own road network and configure lots while the system enforces basic subdivision rules in the background. The tool understands roads and rights of way, lot dimensions and product mix so users can experiment with different layouts while staying within buildable constraints.

According to Prophetic, SiteAI Drafter enables land teams to:

The result is a customizable subdivision plan that can reflect the specific community a builder wants to take to a seller, investment committee or municipal review, while still providing a defensible yield number.

Beyond drafting, Prophetic is adding CAD export so that plans created either by SiteAI’s automated engine or by SiteAI Drafter can be used as a starting point for detailed civil engineering design.

This is intended to shift engineers and planners away from redrawing concepts from scratch and toward refining the land team’s preferred layout. Prophetic said the export capability also opens the platform to civil engineers, planners and land developers who want to generate quick concepts before full design or to show builders what a site can hold before a first meeting.

“Every land team plans a site a little differently,” said Oliver Alexander, founder and CEO of Prophetic. “Sometimes you want an answer in seconds across dozens of parcels. Sometimes you already know exactly where the entry and the main road go, and you want to see what your plan yields. SiteAI now works however you do. And with CAD export, the plan doesn’t stop with the land team. It goes to the engineer as a starting point, not a sketch to redraw. That’s what an operating system for land acquisition should do: carry the work forward and help our users win more deals.”

As higher rates and tighter capital make land bets more costly, builders, developers and their capital partners are under pressure to vet more sites with fewer people and less time. Early engineering work is expensive, and using consultants for preliminary concepts can slow down offers in competitive bid situations.

Tools that combine automated yield analysis with guided drafting give acquisition leaders a way to pressure-test their preferred community design before committing to full engineering fees. For homebuilders and land developers, the ability to walk into a seller or investment committee meeting with a concept plan, yield range and CAD-ready file can help shorten decision cycles and reduce the risk of surprises later in entitlement or design.

Microsoft and Salesforce Introduce Copilotforce for AI Sales Workflows

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Microsoft and Salesforce announced Copilotforce on November 17, 2026, unveiling the partnership at Microsoft Ignite with a Salesforce plugin for Microsoft Copilot in public preview. The plugin includes more than 38 prebuilt sales skills and connects customer records and supported actions with workplace context from meetings, email and documents.

Microsoft Work IQ, the intelligence layer powering Copilot, supplies context from Teams meetings, Outlook emails and documents held in SharePoint and OneDrive. Salesforce in Copilot combines that activity with information held in Salesforce, giving sellers a view of recorded account details and customer interactions across Microsoft 365. Authenticated Salesforce MCP access, the connection used for these interactions, lets sellers retrieve customer information and take supported actions within their existing Salesforce permissions.

The announced workflows address opportunity planning, deal risk and follow-up. Pipeline Review and Close Planning are intended to help sellers decide which opportunities need attention and develop plans whose customer and deal context can inform proposals and business cases in Word and PowerPoint. Deal Review, Customer Health and Renewal Radar combine Salesforce opportunity data with concerns raised in Outlook, escalations discussed in Teams and unresolved commitments, helping identify where a seller may need to intervene.

Call Follow-Up turns customer conversations into follow-up drafts, internal recaps and proposed activities for Salesforce. Sellers review and confirm the next steps rather than reconstructing conversations and updating several systems manually.

Sales Cloud in Teams extends the integration into ongoing conversations with pipeline and quota progress, deal notifications and briefing materials. It is in pilot with enterprise customers, and an open beta is planned for later in 2026.

The governance model divides responsibility between the platforms. Salesforce controls customer-data access and governs supported business actions, while Microsoft applies its existing enterprise compliance controls to communications and documents. Supported writebacks to Salesforce require explicit user confirmation and must respect Salesforce validation rules, keeping customer records governed and auditable. User approval and existing permissions therefore form the boundary between Copilot’s assistance and an actual change to a customer record.

The partnership also addresses enterprises that keep customer data in Salesforce and operational or financial data in Microsoft Fabric. Its two-way Zero Copy integrations between Salesforce Data 360 and Fabric are intended to support queries and analysis across both without building pipelines or maintaining duplicate datasets.

Data Federation is generally available, allowing joint customers to query supported Microsoft Fabric OneLake data directly within Data 360 while the source data remains in Fabric. The reverse connection, Salesforce Data 360 shortcut, is in public preview and shares curated customer intelligence into OneLake for analytics, reporting and AI workloads without duplicating the data. Regional availability and customer access depend on applicable customer agreements.

Accenture is the design and implementation partner and is also deploying and validating the integrations in its global sales organization. The consulting firm says it brings experience across 13 industries and will share lessons from its internal deployment with clients.

The scale of that internal test is substantial: Accenture reports that it has rolled out Copilot to more than 740,000 people and has 55,000 Salesforce users. Chair and CEO Julie Sweet described the company as “customer zero for Copilotforce” and said the deployment is intended to give sellers more time with customers while trusted intelligence and agents work alongside them.

Copilotforce’s immediate significance lies in how those pieces fit together: Microsoft supplies the Copilot interface and workplace context, Salesforce retains control over customer data and actions, and the Fabric connections limit the need for duplicate datasets. Accenture’s pilot is an early test of that arrangement inside a large sales organization. Of the capabilities with stated availability, only Data Federation is generally available, so the near-term enterprise question is whether the preview and pilot tools can reduce manual sales work while preserving those controls.

Meta Launches Muse AI Agent in Australia with Local Connectors

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Meta announced on November 17, 2026 that its Muse personal AI agent is available in Australia, with local connector experiences for creativity, shopping, property, fitness and entertainment rolling out progressively.

Meta describes Muse as performing work rather than only answering questions. It is intended to help users stay on top of responsibilities, manage tasks and projects, and turn long-term goals into action plans.

People in Australia can connect Muse to Gmail, Google Calendar, Instagram and OpenTable for tasks including managing email, coordinating schedules, drawing on saved content and making restaurant reservations. The early local partner list includes Canva, Domain, Kogan, MECCA, Officeworks, realestate.com.au, SWEAT, ShopBack and Ticketek.

The local connector features will roll out progressively, and Meta says some experiences will become available after launch. As they arrive, users could ask Muse to design visuals or other content with Canva, explore property listings through Domain and realestate.com.au, or work toward fitness goals with SWEAT. Canva is also available as a connector for Muse users worldwide.

Other proposed uses include buying electronics and appliances through Kogan, beauty products through MECCA, school and office supplies through Officeworks, and tickets through Ticketek. Meta says the planned ShopBack experience would let users compare prices and earn cashback and rewards on flights, hotels and shopping. Together, the connectors give Muse routes into consumer activities that can end in a reservation or purchase rather than only a written response.

Real-time voice conversations with Muse are available on mobile, web and Mac. Users can talk through an idea and let it take shape without worrying about an exact prompt; a conversation starts by tapping the phone icon in the top-right corner.

Spaces lets users create a custom experience inside Muse by describing an idea. Examples include a shopping hub, school dashboard, wedding planner and trip planner. Muse is available in Australia on iOS, Android, muse.ai and a Mac app, while Meta lists AI glasses support as coming soon. It is free for most uses, with subscription plans for people who want to do more.

Meta says Muse runs on Muse Secure VM, a dedicated cloud computer that stores data and credentials for connected services while keeping passwords and payment methods out of the agent’s view. Users choose what Muse can access, and it checks with them before sensitive actions such as sending an email or making a purchase.

Muse itself is available on the supported platforms, but some local experiences connecting it to retailers, property services and entertainment providers are still forthcoming. That makes the distinction between access and action important: as those connections arrive, users’ access settings and approval decisions will govern which services Muse can use and which sensitive steps it can take.

Your Googlebook just got a Chrome update – 5 new features

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

The Chrome update includes more useful vertical tabs, easier screen sharing, and some handy keyboard shortcuts.

On Monday, Google rolled out an M157 update for the Chrome web browser on Googlebook OS, with a handful of new features and performance boosts to streamline your workflow and browsing experience.

While the first Googlebooks on the market have received mixed reviews, these features could help the laptops gain more useful features in Chrome that other browsers already offer.

Also: Googlebooks? The best laptop for Android users is something else

Here are the five new Chrome features on Googlebook OS to know about.

If you’ve ever had to share your screen on a video call, you know how irritating it can be to switch tabs without having to repeatedly start and stop sharing. This Chrome update adds a tab-sharing controls bar; when you share a browser tab in a video call, Chrome will display a persistent control bar at the top of the window so you can “Stop sharing” or “Share this tab instead” from any tab.

Also: 6 Chrome default settings I changed to instantly

When you hover over a collapsed vertical tab rail with your mouse or trackpad, it will expand, so you can read full tab titles, switch tabs, or manage groups. The rail will collapse again when you move the cursor away.

This auto-expand feature is enabled by default. If you want to turn it off or on, go to Settings > Appearance > Tab position > “Auto expand tabs on hover,” or right-click the vertical tab rail.

The Chrome update allows you to manually resize your vertical tab bar to view longer page titles when needed. The browser will remember your custom tab width, even after you open a new window or restart the browser. (You’ll just need to turn “Auto expand tabs on hover” off for it to work.)

You can now save individual Chrome Settings subpages as direct web addresses that you can type, bookmark, or link to directly (for example, chrome://settings/privacy), meaning you won’t have to go digging around in menus to change your preferences anymore. You can navigate between these Settings subpages with the Back and Forward buttons.

The system’s “Save As” file picker is now enabled by default for menu save actions, such as “Save link as” and “Save image as,” so you can more easily rename files and select a destination folder. This includes Google Drive or external storage.

Also: Google Chrome running slow? 5 easy ways to speed it up fast

Outside of these new features, a few of Chrome M157’s performance fixes include the ability to press Ctrl+L to reveal and select web addresses; keeping your bookmarks bar visible in fullscreen mode and while screen sharing; better media controls while using Picture-in-Picture mode; and the ability to partially translate PDFs within your browser.

If you have Chrome set up to automatically download and install updates, you won’t have to lift a finger to take advantage of the new features and performance boosts.

But if you’ve disabled automatic updates, you can manually update by selecting the three-dot menu in the top-right corner of your Chrome window and navigating to Help > About Google Chrome > Relaunch.

Also: My week with new Googlebooks: The good, the bad, and the promising

If the Relaunch option is greyed out or nowhere to be found, you’re running on the latest version of Chrome. And if you were working on something before the update, don’t worry: Chrome will save your open tabs and reopen them automatically when you restart.

NICER Status Updates

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

NASA’s NICER Telescope Ends Science Operations

After studying the universe for six and a half years beyond its initial mission, NASA’s NICER (Neutron star Interior Composition Explorer) telescope on the International Space Station has ended science operations.

The mission and space station teams are now focused on plans to finalize NICER’s 8-year archive of X-ray science observations and decommission and detach the telescope from its perch near the station’s starboard solar array.

In June 2025, NICER was suddenly unable to swivel side-to-side beyond its current orientation, which limited its ability to track cosmic objects. The team paused data collection to investigate the issue.

Robotic cameras on the outside of the station collected photographs of NICER that helped eliminate external causes. After an extensive investigation, engineers think an internal motor has failed, limiting the telescope’s motion.

Launched in 2017, NICER embarked on an initial 18-month prime mission. Since then, it has made groundbreaking measurements of neutron stars, which contain the densest matter in the universe that we can measure, and revolutionized our understanding of black holes, active galaxies, and other mysterious phenomena in our universe.

The telescope also successfully demonstrated the potential of pulsar-based spacecraft navigation in X-rays that could be used for travel to Mars and beyond. Closer to home, technology developed to test NICER before launch is being incorporated into prototype portable CT scanners, materials analysis systems, and other applications.

Science Observations Remain Paused for NASA’s NICER Telescope

Science operations by NASA’s NICER (Neutron star Interior Composition Explorer), an X-ray telescope on the International Space Station, remain paused as the team continues to evaluate the telescope’s systems after an issue developed with one of its motors. The motor is unable to move NICER beyond its current position side to side, and the telescope’s status has not changed since operations were halted June 17.

The primary goal is to move NICER into its stowed configuration in case it needs to accommodate space station activities, though the current placement does not pose a safety issue to the station or crew. The team currently does not have a timeframe for returning to science operations.

Photos taken by robotic cameras outside the station are helping eliminate external causes for the issue. Now the team is coordinating with space station personnel to perform troubleshooting maneuvers and determine potential causes within the payload.

Since it began observing the X-ray universe in 2017, NICER has successfully demonstrated a form of deep space navigation that could be used for travel to Mars and beyond.

Designed for a prime mission of 18 months and now in its eighth year of operations, NICER has made groundbreaking measurements of neutron stars, which contain the densest matter in the universe that we can measure, and revolutionized our understanding of black holes, active galaxies, and other mysterious phenomena in our universe. Technology developed to test NICER before launch is being incorporated into prototype portable CT scanners, communications systems, and several other applications on Earth for the benefit of all.

NASA’s NICER Telescope Suspends Science Operations

NASA’s NICER (Neutron star Interior Composition Explorer), an X-ray telescope on the International Space Station, has paused observations due to a problem with one of the motors that drives its ability to track cosmic objects.

The NICER team paused operations June 17 when performance degradation in the motor began affecting science observations. Engineers are investigating the cause and potential solutions.

The telescope was installed near the space station’s starboard solar array in 2017. The NICER mission has successfully demonstrated a form of deep space navigation that could be used for travel to Mars and beyond. It has also made groundbreaking measurements of neutron stars, which contain the densest matter in the universe that we can measure, and revolutionized our understanding of black holes, active galaxies, and other mysterious phenomena in our universe.

Following Repair, NASA’s NICER Improves Daytime Measurements

A NASA X-ray telescope on the International Space Station called NICER, or Neutron star Interior Composition Explorer, has regained additional daytime observation capabilities thanks to repairs completed during a spacewalk and a reconfiguration of its detectors.

In May 2023, NICER developed a light leak in which unwanted sunlight began entering the instrument. Photos taken from inside the space station revealed several small areas of damage to the telescope’s thin thermal shields, which block sunlight while allowing X-rays through to the detectors. Nighttime observations were unaffected, and with operational adjustments, the NICER team was able to recover about 20% of station daytime observations.

In January, NASA astronaut Nick Hague installed nine patches to cover the largest areas of damage during a spacewalk. After resuming science operations, the NICER team determined the overall level of sunlight inside NICER had substantially reduced. Still, it experienced more visible-light interference than expected.

Close-up, high-resolution photos from the spacewalk allowed the team to see additional small holes and cracks in the thermal shields that were not previously visible. These accounted for the remaining sunlight intrusion.

After further analysis, the NICER team developed a novel approach to regaining additional daytime data collection.

Each X-ray that hits a NICER detector generates electrical charge that is sensed by a measurement/power unit (MPU). After so many hits, the detector resets — like emptying a cup before it overflows.

Sunlight can also create charge that accumulates in the detector, adding water to the metaphorical cup. There was so much sunlight entering NICER that the detectors were filling up with charge and resetting thousands of times for every X-ray detection. It overwhelmed the MPU’s ability to process the valid X-ray events.

Hague’s repair in January reduced the amount of sunlight entering NICER, which enabled the team to reconfigure the MPUs to ignore the sunlight-generated resets. After initial testing on the ground, the team updated one MPU before switching all seven. The changeover was completed March 12.

In combination with the patches, the reconfiguration has allowed NICER to return to collecting observations during more than 70% of station daytime, as the telescope continues to help us better understand the X-ray universe, including neutron stars, black holes, and other energetic phenomena. The team continues to look for more opportunities to improve NICER’s operations.

NASA’s NICER Continues Science Operations Post Repair

NASA crew aboard the International Space Station installed patches to the agency’s NICER (Neutron star Interior Composition Explorer) mission during a spacewalk on Jan. 16. NICER, an X-ray telescope perched near the station’s starboard solar array, resumed science operations later the same day.

The patches cover areas of NICER’s thermal shields where damage was discovered in May 2023. These thin filters block sunlight while allowing X-rays to pass through. After the discovery, the NICER team restricted their observations during the station’s daytime to avoid overwhelming the mission’s sensitive detectors. Nighttime observations were unaffected, and the team was able to continue collecting data for the science community to make groundbreaking measurements using the instrument’s full capabilities.

The repair went according to plan. Data since collected shows the detectors behind the patched areas are performing better than before during station night, and the overall level of sunlight inside NICER during the daytime is reduced substantially.

While NICER experiences less interference from sunlight than before, after analyzing initial data, the team has determined the telescope still experiences more interference than expected. The installed patches cover areas of known damage identified using astronomical observations and from photos taken by both external robotic cameras and astronauts inside the space station. Measurements collected since the repair and close-up, high-resolution photos obtained during the spacewalk are providing new information that may point the way toward further daytime data collection.

In the meantime, NICER continues operations with its full measurement capabilities during orbit night to enable further trailblazing discoveries in time domain and multimessenger astrophysics.

Sunlight ‘Leak’ Impacting NASA’s NICER Telescope, Science Continues

On Tuesday, May 22, NASA’s NICER (Neutron Star Interior Composition Explorer), an X-ray telescope on the International Space Station, developed a “light leak,” in which unwanted sunlight enters the instrument. While analyzing incoming data since then, the team identified an impact to daytime observations. Nighttime observations seem to be unaffected.

The team suspects that at least one of the thin thermal shields on NICER’s 56 X-ray Concentrators has been damaged, allowing sunlight to reach its sensitive detectors.

To mitigate the effects on measurements, the NICER team has limited daytime observations to objects far away from the Sun’s position in the sky. The team has also updated commands to NICER that automatically lower its sensitivity during the orbital day to reduce the effects from sunlight contamination. The team is evaluating these changes and assessing additional measures to reduce the impact on science observations.

To date, more than 300 scientific papers have used NICER observations, and the team is confident that NICER will continue to produce world-class science.

Renting as a financial strategy, what real estate agents should know

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Entrata’s Virginia Love says renters are making intentional tradeoffs, prioritizing savings, debt reduction, and flexibility. Agents can better serve renters by discussing full ownership costs, budget comfort, and timelines without assuming buying is the next step.  AI Summary

Renting isn’t always a stepping stone to homeownership. Multifamily expert Virginia Love of Entrata explains how financial priorities, flexibility and the true cost of ownership are reshaping renters’ decisions.

The story we’re telling ourselves about renters is outdated, Virginia Love, industry principal at Entrata, told me recently.

Entrata is a property management operating system built to improve operational efficiency at multifamily communities. In its recent “Resident Pulse” survey, conducted in partnership with YouGov, the company asked renters about their financial priorities, attitudes toward homeownership, definitions of success and other lifestyle factors.

That gives Love a bird’s-eye view of the state of renting and, by extension, homeownership.

“I feel like people think that renters are less-than,” she said. “I think the real story is, people are trying to do what’s right for them and what’s right for their family.”

With homeownership increasingly out of reach for people of all ages, it’s time that real estate agents rethink the role of renters in the housing market. Entrata’s insights can help.

Love said that renters are prioritizing healthy financial hygiene and making decisions based on a more complex economic environment, reducing their existing financial obligations before pursuing longer-term financial goals.

“They are eating out less, they’re shopping less, they’re spending less, they’re switching to cheaper brands,” she said, with the goal of prioritizing emergency savings, debt reduction and other budgetary line items.

For previous generations, the initial barrier to homeownership was a down payment, Love said. Now, it’s the rising costs of everything that comes after the initial upfront purchase price: the mortgage payment at today’s higher rates, insurance, maintenance, repairs and other expenses. Managing the unexpected requires greater levels of financial stability.

“It’s then I have insurance, then I gotta take care of the yard. Then what if the tree falls on the driveway?” Love said.

Beyond the cost of rent vs. buy, maintenance, amenities and the ability to relocate can have real value for renters, especially when they’re working to pay down debt, pursuing educational goals, navigating career shifts or deciding where and when they’re ready to settle down. What they’re paying for, Love said, is freedom.

Love describes Gen Z in particular as pragmatic about money and more willing to take a gradual path toward major financial milestones.

For those who plan to buy, “they’re absolutely fine with starter homes,” Love said. “And I don’t think that that is something that we’ve seen for a while. Everybody kind of wanted to go from zero to 60, and this is a generation that’s willing to take those steps.”

She explains that some renters are working toward a purchase while others prefer to invest, maintain flexibility or avoid being tied to one address.

Real estate agents often approach renters with an ingrained belief in the superiority of homeownership, but it’s not right for everyone. Spend time upfront with renters to find out what matters to them, and don’t just push them toward homeownership automatically, Love said.

Stop assuming buying is the client’s next step: Renters aren’t necessarily buyers who simply haven’t saved up a down payment yet. They may have other financial priorities, like paying down student loans, building an emergency fund, investing for retirement or prioritizing flexibility. Ask what a client’s financial priorities are before assuming homeownership is the goal.

Talk about the full cost of homeownership: Down payments aren’t the whole story here. Monthly payments, rising insurance and repair costs, home maintenance and other expenses can affect whether, and when, buying makes sense. Make sure you’re sharing updated costs of ownership along with the upfront costs, and be realistic about affordability.

Ask what payment feels comfortable, not just what a lender will approve: A client’s maximum purchasing power isn’t necessarily the same as their target budget. Understand the buying budget that matches their comfort level, other financial considerations and personal priorities before locking in on a price range.

Understand that delay isn’t the same as giving up: Some renters may aspire to own a home, but they’re not necessarily in a rush. Maintain relationships over the long haul without turning every conversation into a homebuying pitch.

Recognize that renting has value beyond the monthly payment: The benefits of renting — maintenance support, amenities, flexibility and freedom — may outweigh those of homeownership, even though they don’t build equity for the renter. Understand what the renter values before presenting ownership as an obvious upgrade or superior choice.

“I think the real story is, people are trying to do what’s right for them and what’s right for their family,” Love said. By understanding timing, goals and price sensitivities, agents can help potential clients evaluate their options without prescribing the outcome ahead of time.

Troy Palmquist is the founder of HomeCode Advisors.

Situational Awareness Dumps Bitcoin Miners After Citadel Sale

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Situational Awareness, the artificial intelligence hedge fund run by former OpenAI researcher Leopold Aschenbrenner, cut its bitcoin miner holdings sharply in the third quarter, leaving about $80.8 million across three stocks and exiting three others, a Monday filing shows.

The San Francisco firm still held 3,019,029 CleanSpark shares, worth $38.9 million at Sept. 30, 1,934,618 Bitdeer shares, worth $20.3 million, and 527,864 IREN shares, worth $21.6 million. Those are the only miner lines left, a sliver of the $1.83 billion in six miner stocks the fund listed at June 30.

The deepest cut was IREN. The fund held 9,474,099 IREN shares, worth $433.3 million, at midyear. The Sept. 30 stake is about 94% smaller by share count. CleanSpark was trimmed about 75%, from 12,276,139 shares worth $178.6 million. Bitdeer was reduced about 44%, from 3,439,450 shares worth $54.6 million. Bitdeer’s reported value fell further than its share count, to $20.3 million, as the stock also declined.

Three names disappeared from the filing entirely. The fund had held 26,008,473 Core Scientific shares, worth $665.6 million, 17,100,000 Riot Platforms shares, worth $468.2 million, and 9,133,726 Hive Digital shares, worth $33.2 million, at June 30. None of those lines appear in the new report. The filing does not say whether those sales were part of a late-July portfolio sale, later trades, or both.

Aschenbrenner published a long 2024 essay, “Situational Awareness: The Decade Ahead,” arguing that artificial general intelligence was approaching faster than most investors appreciated, and that computing power and electricity would be the tight constraints. He founded the fund that September around that view, looking at technology and energy companies that AI would help or hurt.

The miner stakes fit the same idea. A March profile described the fund as betting that electricity and computing capacity may be the most valuable assets in the AI build-out, pointing to miners that were turning high-density facilities and power contracts toward AI workloads. IREN has signed a multiyear cloud contract with Microsoft. CleanSpark has struck a $6.6 billion data-center lease. Listed miners have also been operating under tight economics this year, with bitcoin often trading below estimated production costs.

The cuts landed after a brutal July. The fund sold the bulk of the fund’s public stock portfolio to Ken Griffin’s Citadel late that month after heavy losses on AI-related holdings, Reuters reported, citing people familiar with the matter. Those sources said the fund was forced to unwind most of its public book. In a letter to investors, Aschenbrenner said the portfolio was down an estimated 67% in July, that the fund had come “closer to permanent capital impairment than is acceptable,” and that it had sold a portion of its public holdings in a block trade to remove all leverage and close its shorts. The unaudited letter still put year-to-date performance at plus 80%. Aschenbrenner said the fund was not shutting down and had preserved its private investments.

The new 13F shows how much of the public book was left by Sept. 30. Reported long holdings fell from $20.24 billion, across 26 lines, at June 30, to $1.58 billion, across 23 lines, three months later. That decline should not be read as the fund losing 92% of its capital. A 13F reports only long positions in U.S.-listed stocks and certain options. It leaves out cash, shorts and private investments, and option lines are valued at the underlying shares, not the premiums paid. Reuters’ sources said that after the Citadel deal the fund would hold a book of roughly $10 billion, including stocks and private investments such as a stake in Anthropic.

What remains is concentrated. The largest common-stock line is now SharonAI, 8,070,950 shares worth $440.7 million. Aschenbrenner also listed call options on Bloom Energy, SanDisk, Nebius and a memory ETF. The three leftover miner stakes are a stub of the midyear book. Whether those positions were trimmed further after Sept. 30 will only be clear when the fourth-quarter 13F arrives in mid-February.

Mortgage rates jump to 7.58% as inflation lifts bond yields

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mortgage rates rose sharply last week, with 30-year conforming loans at 7.58% and jumbo loans at 7.87%. Hot inflation data and higher yields pushed Fed hike expectations above 93%, while builders continued offering rate incentives for new homes.  AI Summary

Mortgage rates skyrocketed last week on the back of rising inflation and bond yields, erasing some of the recent affordability gains for potential homebuyers.

At HousingWire‘s Mortgage Rates Center on Tuesday, rates for 30-year conforming loans averaged 7.58%, up 23 basis points from one week ago. Rates for 30-year jumbo loans jumped 31 bps to average 7.87%, while rates for 30-year loans through the Federal Housing Administration (FHA) rose 15 bps to 7.37%.

HousingWire Lead Analyst Logan Mohtashami wrote over the weekend that “last week wasn’t good for mortgage rates or the bond market” due to hawkish statements from Federal Reserve officials and hot inflation data from the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS). Additionally, the U.S. conflict with Iran and potential tariff increases against Mexico propelled long-term Treasury rates and mortgage rates upward.

Interest rate traders took notice. According to the CME Group‘s FedWatch tool, more than 93% expect benchmark rates to increase by 25 bps at next month’s Fed meeting. That’s up from 76% one month ago. It would be the second hike of the year by the Fed and put the federal funds rate at a range of 4% to 4.25%.

Kyle Bass, business production manager at Refi.com, pointed to Mortgage Bankers Association application data showing that recent downward moves in rates had positive impacts on loan demand. But with visible volatility in rates on a day-to-day basis, he said, preparation between loan officers and borrowers is needed to lock in the lowest possible rate and get lower monthly payments.

“Borrowers who benefit are the ones who prepared before the number moved. A shadow application, with income documents, statements and credit in order, lets a homeowner ask what their current mortgage could do for them the day rates cooperate. Waiting for a better number only works if you are ready when it arrives,” Bass said.

A report released Tuesday by Realtor.com shows that the new-home segment is giving the typical buyer lower rates and better affordability than existing homes. Homebuilders, who are also under financial pressure, continue to offer rate buydowns and other incentives as they seek to offload their inventory.

Realtor.com reported that the average mortgage rate for a new-home buyer in the third quarter was 5.01%, more than a full percentage point lower than the 6.15% rate for an existing-home buyer. That translates to a difference of $258 in monthly payments for someone buying a $400,000 home with a 10% down payment.

“Builders have adjusted to a market where affordability is still a major hurdle, but that adjustment isn’t always visible in the listing price,” Joel Berner, senior economist at Realtor.com, said in a statement. “Reduced mortgage rates can help make a new home more affordable for buyers while allowing builders to hold asking prices relatively steady.”

The report noted that reduced rates are often temporary and only last for the first few years after origination. But even when comparing 30-year fixed rates, those who bought a new home in the third quarter saved, with average rates of 6.19% compared to 6.68% for existing home-buyers. On the same $400,000 purchase with 10% down, the new-home buyer saves about $117 per month in interest.

“Mortgage-rate incentives can make a meaningful difference in what a buyer pays each month, but it’s important to understand how long the lower rate lasts,” Berner said. “A temporary buydown can provide relief early on, while a lower rate fixed for the life of the loan offers a different kind of savings.”

Selma Hepp, chief economist at Cotality, said in a statement that tariff-driven increases to building materials are putting pressure on the new-home segment. In turn, this winds up being felt by consumers through higher home purchase prices. Regardless of the type of home being purchased, rising insurance premiums and property taxes are also straining budgets.

“For housing, the key question is not simply whether mortgage rates decline, but whether they fall enough to meaningfully improve purchasing power and bring more buyers and sellers back to the market,” Hepp said.” Until then, I expect activity to remain relatively subdued, making the spring homebuying season an increasingly important test of whether the market can finally turn a corner.”

While housing market professionals continue to grapple with fewer home sales and lower mortgage origination volumes, the U.S. economy as a whole continues to show resilience to inflation, tariffs and geopolitical uncertainty.

Melissa Cohn, regional vice president at William Raveis Mortgage, pointed to last week’s Producer Price Index (PPI), which showed 6.5% inflation on an annualized basis in October. It was the largest year-over-year increase since November 2022. That came only days after the Consumer Price Index showed monthly inflation of 0.4%.

“Unfortunately, the CPI number that we got earlier this week has set off bond yields, and bond yields were probably up almost 25 basis points from where they were at the end of the previous week,” Cohn said Friday in a statement. “It’s not just because of the CPI number, but because of expectations about the Fed, where members are saying, ‘Well, we’re definitely going to have to hike rates.’ We know that the market’s expecting it.

“All of the benefit that we got in mortgage rates over the course of the past three weeks was basically just thrown out the window again, because mortgage rates are really tied to the rate of inflation. We’re back on the inflation wagon.”

Federal Reserve Chair Kevin Warsh offered some forward-looking remarks on Monday during a jobs and productivity conference in San Francisco, saying that the central bank continues to look closely at the development of “super intelligence” and its impacts on the global and national economies.

“At the Fed, our role in all this is neither advocacy nor opposition. Our responsibility is to recognize that the widespread adoption of this technology will have significant impact on the economy, with important implications for monetary policy down the road,” Warsh said.

Senate Poised to Vote on Affordability Package that Hunters, Anglers Worry Is a Trojan Horse for Public-Land Rollbacks

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 17, 2026 1:09 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

The U.S. Senate plans to vote this week on a bill aimed at easing America’s affordability crisis and fighting climate change that hunting and fishing groups worry will also erode many of the nation’s bedrock conservation laws.

The complex and winding 400-page Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act focuses on lowering energy costs, something both Republicans and Democrats have championed and and that helped drive Democratic gains in the midterm elections. BAAJA also, however, ties jobs and household bills with curtailing public-lands protections. And in a twist that’s perplexing many conservation groups, the bill is co-sponsored by Sens. Martin Heinrich (D-N.M.) and Mike Lee (R-Utah), two lawmakers known for being on nearly opposite ends of the public-lands spectrum.

BAAJA is largely focused on permitting reform, something the country hasn’t tackled in decades, according to the Bipartisan Policy Center. In practice, the legislation would raise the standard for whether projects approved by one administration can be revoked by a future administration.

That means an industrial solar project permitted by one president, for example, could not be stymied by a future president intent on preventing renewables. It also speeds up permitting processes and cuts red tape for everything from energy projects to landscape-scale changes like prescribed fire.

But sportsmen and conservationists say the bill would not just expedite climate or environmentally friendly projects. BAAJA would also make it much harder — or potentially impossible — for an administration to overturn leases or approvals for projects as controversial as hyperscale data centers on public lands or mines in the same watersheds as sensitive wilderness areas. It’s a bill that prioritizes development, many observers say, at the cost of the very “red tape” that has been responsible for conserving many of the country’s most priceless fish, wildlife, and wild places.

“I understand this is a big, bipartisan bill — it has some things in it I would rather it not have. But I negotiated the deal based on what was actually possible to get signed into law,” Heinrich wrote in an email to Outdoor Life. “Because when I say climate change is an existential threat, I mean it. And I will not let an opportunity to do something about it pass by.”

Conservationists like Ingrid Lyons, executive director of Save the Boundary Waters, understand the need for stability around permitting development. The whiplash between issuing and revoking permits for public land development projects every time an administration changes has likely hurt the U.S.’s ability to move forward with important energy projects, including in renewables like wind and solar.

But she fears this bill could instead cement the creation of controversial projects like a copper-sulfide mine at the headwaters of the Boundary Waters Canoe Area Wilderness or a copper-molybdenum-gold mine in the salmon-rich waters of Alaska’s Bristol Bay. Permits for projects like these have been denied, among other issues, because of environmental risk. If permits are reissued in similar scenarios, legislation like BAAJA could prevent future administrations from stopping development in places that truly need protection.

“What about landscape certainty?” Lyons says. “We’re overlooking in some ways the impact to communities that are relying on protection and not just projects.”

The conservation community has become increasingly vocal in its opposition to the bill since Heinrich and Lee partnered with Sens. Sheldon Whitehouse (D-R.I.), and Shelley Moore Capito (R-W.Va.), to introduce the legislation in late September. Lee has a clear track record of supporting public land sales and undermining existing public-lands conservation, including 2025’s attempt to include federal land sales in the One Big Beautiful Bill.

A lengthy version of BAAJA is before the Senate as of Tuesday. The current bill includes a variety of red flags for hunters, anglers, tribes, endangered-species advocates, and other conservationists.

The Theodore Roosevelt Conservation Partnership acknowledged the need for permit reform but said in a statement that “progress should not come at the expense of the conservation safeguards and public review that give hunters and anglers a voice in how our lands and waters are managed.”

For Lyons, it comes down to the potential unintended consequences of sweeping legislation like BAAJA.

“We need to be exceptionally aware of the implications of this bill, which could be that a lot of these really beloved places could have projects many communities have worked really hard to oppose cemented into place.”

Many in the conservation community were surprised by the bill’s sponsors, given Heinrich’s history as a sportsman and conservationist who regularly defends public lands and sensitive places, and Lee’s general position as a proponent of development and public-lands sell-off.

But in Heinrich’s statement to Outdoor Life, he emphasizes the impact of climate change on our planet, including the places where people hunt, hike, bike and camp. Independent modeling, he said, predicts the bill will lead to a 20 percent reduction in power sector emissions, or the “equivalent to taking 39 coal plants offline for 10 years.”

“You cannot protect a trout stream without paying attention to the warming temperatures and declining flows that threaten it,” he wrote. “You cannot protect wildlife habitat while ignoring the impacts of increasingly severe drought and wildfire. We need to reduce emissions, and that means getting more clean energy onto the grid.”

In an Oct. 30 op-ed in the Albuquerque Journal, Heinrich explained how New Mexico’s SunZia Wind and Transmission project took 17 years to permit and build.

He also pushed back on what he called misinformation about the bill, stating he doesn’t believe data centers should be on public land. The bill also requires large scale data center operators to pay the full cost of electricity consumed by the facility.

The Izaak Walton League’s conservation director, Kate Miller, thinks each of the bill’s sponsors are acting in good faith and trying to do what is right for their constituents. Many projects, including those aimed at restoring forests and wildlife habitat, have been held up by litigation. Something like BAAJA could move those forward.

But Miller would have liked to see these issues tackled individually instead of in a massive bill. And while she hopes that, if the bill passes, the administration will respect existing laws, the past two years has brought an onslaught of changes to most of the nation’s bedrock environmental laws including the Clean Water Act, the Endangered Species Act, and the National Environmental Policy Act.

“We want to make sure what comes out is not something that sacrifices those core protections that allow us to have healthy waters and habitats,” Miller says. “That we don’t sacrifice one for the other.”

Read Next: Alaska Considers ‘Unprecedented’ Closure of Public Land to Hunting, Trapping Along Controversial Ambler Road

While the Senate is fast tracking the bill before the next Congress begins in January, it must move through the Senate and the House, and any changes be agreed upon by both chambers before the bill would land on President Trump’s desk. Meanwhile, both industry and conservation groups say they’re working to lobby lawmakers for changes and amendments. As a result, several conservation organizations declined to comment for this story.

Christine Peterson is a freelance writer based in southeast Wyoming covering hunting, fishing, outdoor recreation, wildlife and the environment for Outdoor Life, High Country News, National Geographic and others.

What are we so afraid of?

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Chapman and FirstHome IQ survey data show widespread money anxiety and financial shame. The column argues that fear of judgment drives avoidance, so housing pros should create safer, guided entry points for conversations.  AI Summary

This year, I discovered I absolutely love horror movies. Through their twisted stories, we can play with our fears, project them onto the screen and laugh at them collectively. I can’t watch them alone, but I started a Scary Movie Club.

Our fears say a lot about us and our society, and I think financial fear shapes more buyer decisions than anyone realizes.

Financial fears run throughout Chapman University’s latest survey of American fears, from economic collapse to medical bills and making rent. Not having enough money for the future ranks above public speaking, sharks and even death.

FirstHome IQ’s latest NextGen Homebuyer Report, “How You’re Built to Buy” found similar levels of anxiety. Two in three respondents worry their finances won’t last, and 86% report some form of financial shame.

Last week I hosted a financial workshop at my gym, and with all of my Scary Movie Club members, we faced our fears together. While I’ve studied the data, that night I met the ghosts and the monsters, and they explained a lot about what’s standing between buyers and their financial goals.

I pitched my financial workshop at run club with promises of pizza and games. But money is still a big scary shark in an ocean we don’t understand.

“Are you a psychopath?!” One run club member laughed as I excitedly shared the plan for collectively discussing our finances. “Are you also going to tell me we’ll be public speaking after?” (He attended, and now that I think about it, I did make him public speak a little).

I discovered the level of my own horror in finance a couple of years ago when I hired a financial advisor. I smiled and nodded, with a tightened chest and shallow breath. I later noticed my fitness tracker thought my meeting was a nap. It confused my frozen, horrified body as shutting down for sleep. (The last time it recorded a nap was during The Conjuring, while we waited for the demon to inhabit the mother and I was too scared to breathe).

Since I started researching financial psychology, I have been pleasantly surprised to find out how unexceptional that financial horror is. In keynotes for housing professionals and in our financial fitness workshop, everyone has their own financial ghost story.

Someone who grew up with very little said that when money comes in, it still looks like a way to plug a hole. “Even though my life’s changed so much now from when I was a kid, I don’t think my brain ever stopped thinking, like, okay, plug this up, plug this up.”

In our research, earning more and saving consistently didn’t impact the anxiety, avoidance or shame numbers by much, likely because they’re old stories. Researchers at Cambridge found that many of the habits we bring to money are largely in place by the time we’re seven, absorbed from our parents’ relationship with money.

If you haven’t seen Poltergeist, the family’s dream house turns out to be built on top of a cemetery with some very active ghosts. We can make more money, save more money, and move into the nicer house, but the bodies are still down there.

For one person in the room, that history had shaped whether owning a home ever seemed like an option. “When you grow up with so little, you just really don’t think you can have those things.” By the time someone asks what they can afford, they may have already decided what kind of life is available to them.

My favorite form of financial madness is avoidance. There’s always at least one medical bill sitting on my counter, taunting me, reminding me of all the ways I fall short. Fortunately, I can afford those bills now (and usually I discover I’ve already paid them). But this is the kind of sick game we play.

In our survey, over a third of people are avoiding the bill on their counter, too. One woman said of her financial anxiety, “My brain just keeps cycling through the fear instead of realizing that it’s not rational.”

Last week, I interviewed Todd Ballenger, a professor and loan officer, on financial psychology, and he told me the fear of being judged is the number one reason people don’t call. People are afraid of being judged, of being taken advantage of, of not knowing something they should.

A business owner in our workshop said of investing in property, “It’s a game I don’t fully understand…so I defer it, and I defer it.” Over half in our survey said they avoided talking with a professional because of how they might look.

That fear reminded me of Brené Brown’s viral TED Talk, where she described shame as something that grows in secrecy, silence and judgment. It is the creaking staircase to the dark basement, the unknown around the corner.

Todd said we can’t focus our attention on the hardest things. “Think about where you feel the opposite of shame in your finances,” and he said to start building there.

I held the workshop at my gym because it’s where we already do hard things together. I still can’t watch a horror movie by myself, and I don’t think most people can face their finances by themselves either.

Everyone has a ghost in their basement, and it’s your job to make sure they don’t go down there alone.

Kristin Messerli is the founder and executive director of FirstHome IQ.This column does not necessarily reflect the opinion of HousingWire’s editorial department and its owners. To contact the editor responsible for this piece: [email protected].
