Pastore: “Esposito? Tra due anni titolare inamovibile dell’Inter. Non roviniamoci la festa”

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Pastore: “Esposito? Tra due anni titolare inamovibile dell’Inter. Non roviniamoci la festa”

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Scegli Fcinter1908.it come tuo sito preferito su Google: clicca qui

Durante Fontana di Trevi, il giornalista Giuseppe Pastore ha parlato del grande momento di Pio Esposito e delle potenzialità dell’attaccante dell’Inter:

I dati ci aiutano, intanto i dati di continuità realizzativa. Feyenoord, Como, Francia, Belgio: quattro squadre non banali, quattro gol in area piccola, quattro gol da cacciatore d'area di rigore, anche se poi in realtà uno dei cinque è arrivato su rigore. Ma ne ha fatti comunque quattro su azione, vincendo dei duelli fisici. Esposito è un giocatore che sembra davvero destinato a fare tanti gol in Nazionale. E questo sistema di gioco che Mancini sta portando avanti, con qualche variazione più o meno felice come, lo migliora, lo completa, perché passa dal 3-5-2 molto particolare, molto peculiare di Chivu, in cui l'abbiamo visto fare anche cose fuori dal seminato, fuori dall'ordinario, all'uomo che deve stare lì e attaccare i palloni oppure essere lanciato cercando lungo, come non sempre è riuscito ieri sera da difensori o dal portiere, ma comunque fare cose un po' diverse.

A me Esposito piace, al di là di quello che sa fare e che continuerà a fare ancora meglio, perché è evidente la serietà e la prontezza di questo ragazzo, la maturità che mostra ogni volta che lo vediamo. Piace perché ha una specie di mantello dell'invisibilità che lo protegge rispetto a questo mondo di pazzi che noi stessi alimentiamo, in cui si passa dall'esaltazione alla depressione.È un giocatore che ha una regolarità, ha anche un carattere che non mi pare uno che si esalta, che si esalterà, ed è la dote chiave per un centravanti in una Nazionale importante, che è chiamato a incidere anche sul lungo periodo.

Noi non conosciamo affatto i limiti di questo giocatore. Nessuno li conosce ed è inutile, è bello, è romantico, è anche un po' un bel gioco da pausa Nazionale dire: ma lui sa dove può arrivare? Giocatori molto meno dotati di lui hanno fatto 50, 100 gol in altre Nazionali. Però il consiglio che do non tanto a lui, che non ne ha bisogno, ma a noi, ai nostri colleghi, a chi parla di calcio tutti i giorni, è di non valutarlo, anche se nella vita del centravanti è questa, perché ne avrà due, tre, quattro gare di fila in cui non segnerà. Però è un giocatore che ci porterà lontano, secondo me. È un giocatore che mi piace.

Qui abbiamo superato uno step, siamo già al secondo. Siamo verso un attaccante che secondo me tra due anni sarà titolare inamovibile dell'Inter, perché al di là della concorrenza che avrà sarà un giocatore molto migliore. Già adesso Esposito è molto meglio di un mese fa. Fidiamoci. Aspettiamo e godiamoci Esposito, sperando naturalmente che non abbia intoppi di natura fisica e psichica sul suo percorso. Qui è un messaggio che dobbiamo mandare a lui, perché racconta molto di noi. Abbiamo secondo me sì, finalmente dopo vent'anni, un centravanti che ci farà compagnia per sette, otto anni con la maglia azzurra. Non roviniamoci la festa da soli e mettiamolo in condizione di rendere al meglio, cosa che mi pare Mancini stia facendo senza troppi fronzoli.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/health-tech/fierce-healthcare-fundraising-tracker-26" hreflang="en">Fierce Healthcare Fundraising Tracker '26: Aevius launches with $22M for clinical insights; Abby Care secures $82M for family-led care</a>

Fierce Healthcare Fundraising Tracker '26: Aevius launches with $22M for clinical insights; Abby Care secures $82M for family-led care" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

At Fierce Healthcare, we keep track of all the venture capital being funneled into the health tech and digital health industries.

Our fundraising tracker provides updated coverage of noteworthy digital health and health tech funding rounds, though we'll still profile exciting new companies and larger rounds that catch our eye in depth.

Oct. 17—AeviusClinical intelligence AI modelsSeries: N/AAmount: $22 millionInvestors: Led by Insight Partners

Aevius is a newly launched Mass General Brigham spinout founded by veterans of AstreaZeneca-acquired Modella AI. The company builds models that intake different forms of medical data to paint a multimodal picture of a patient's current state and trajectory. That information can then be used by healthcare, payer and life sciences organizations for potential application in care delivery, operational decision-making and clinical research.

“Our mission is to make medicine computable,” Rowland Pettit, M.D., Ph.D., co-founder and CEO of Aevius, said. “As a physician-scientist, I’ve worked across many types of clinical data, and there is far more knowledge in those records than we’re able to use today. We’re bringing general medical intelligence to that data, so organizations can build on a deeper understanding for their patients.”

The company said it plans to use the $22 million launch money to support continued model and product development. It also plans to expand its engineering, clinical and commercial teams, as well as to focus on commercial deployment.

Oct. 15—Abby CareFamily caregiver trainingSeries: CAmount: $82 millionInvestors: Led by Transformation Capital, along with CVS Health Ventures, Frist Cressey Ventures and Pivotal, with participation from existing investors Khosla Ventures, Thrive Capital and Sequoia Capital

Abby Care is an AI-native home care startup that equips family caregivers with professional training, certification and ongoing support from dedicated RN care managers.  The company works with health plans to arrange care at home for families that need it. Fortune reported the series C round.

Oct. 14—AllswellBehavioral health for LGBTQ+Series: Seed Amount: $7 millionInvestors: Rethink Impact, Magnify Ventures, Ulu Ventures and Hopelab Ventures, Techstars and Amboy Street Ventures

Allswell offers one-on-one virtual therapy, group programs and trauma-informed care to LGBTQ+ patients. It is in-network with commercial and Medicaid payers. The startup, launched in 2025, plans to use the capital to expand beyond its DMV-area footprint, build out its clinical training program for therapists and launch psychiatric medication management next year.

"Nobody realizes how big this market is. Only 5% of Gen X identifies as LGBTQ+, compared with 25% of Gen Z. That's a massive generational and cultural shift, and we want to do for LGBTQ+ health what Midi did for menopause," Heidi Patel, managing partner at Rethink Impact, said in a press release.

Allswell's therapeutic models were built for patients navigating depression and anxiety, identity-specific trauma, family dynamics and social transition. It is also developing its own curriculum on PTSD and trauma care.

The startup, though new, is already seeing positive outcomes. The company often keeps patients for over a year, rare for behavioral health. In its most recent cohorts, 77% showed improvements in anxiety and 71% showed improvements in depression after 60 days of treatment.

"Our patients aren't used to being served with care and respect, or to having something built for them. ... This is a sleeping-giant category, and it has been underestimated at every step. Our ambition is to continue developing new, thoughtful clinical programs specifically for the LGBTQ+ community, regardless of the political climate," Dawn Androphy, co-founder and CEO of Allswell, said in an announcement.

Outro aims to support safe deprescribing of medications, starting with antidepressants. The company combines specially-trained clinicians with evidence-informed tapering protocols, patient monitoring, psychosocial support and technology. Its program is available in 14 states.

"Deprescribing guidance has existed in countries like the U.K. for years," said Mark Horowitz, M.D., Ph.D., Outro's co-founder, psychiatry researcher and deprescribing expert, in an announcement. "Yet in America, where antidepressant use is widespread, tapering support is very hard to come by, so millions of patients are left without support when they are ready to come off."

The funding will support the growth of Outro’s deprescribing clinician network, further the application of AI to deprescribing-specific workflows, expand the company’s tapering model into additional drug classes and grow existing partnerships with leading universities and advocacy groups.

It recommended providers regularly review the risks and benefits of psychiatric medications and offer alternatives like therapy when clinically appropriate. The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services also released guidance for providers on the importance of deprescribing.

Oct. 6—VitalizeAI-powered operating system for staffing and schedulingSeries: AAmount: $31 millionInvestors: Oak HC/FT, Norwest Ventures, .406 Ventures, Constellation Ventures, Y Combinator and MemorialCare Innovation Fund.

Vitalize aims to supercharge health system capacity and operations with an artificial intelligence platform that unifies staffing, scheduling, flow and capacity.

At health systems, labor and capacity management are highly manual, inefficient processes that take up hours of administrative work each day.

Labor optimization is the key constraint behind patient flow, operational efficiency and financial performance across health systems, Veeraj Shah, co-founder and CEO of startup Vitalize, said. Capacity planning is the other half of the equation. Hospital revenue depends on matching patient demand to available beds and staff.

Vitalize founders view hospital staffing and capacity management as a complex logistics problem that directly affects finances, patient outcomes and clinician experience.

Vitalize positions its software as an "air traffic control" system for healthcare operations, helping health systems automate staffing, forecast demand, manage capacity and reduce reliance on overtime and agency labor, executives said. Vitalize’s AI automates the most manual scheduling tasks for clinical leaders, while forecasting demand and orchestrating staff across thousands of beds in real time. The company's platform uses machine learning, optimization models and AI agents to forecast staffing needs, fill schedule gaps, automate staffing workflows and recommend patient placement decisions, Shah noted. Sept. 24—Basalt Health AI layer for post-acute care admissionsSeries: AAmount: $20 millionInvestors: NEA led the round, with participation from Frist Cressey Ventures and 25m Health

Basalt Health uses AI to streamline post-acute care admissions. The company plans to use the capital to expand its platform and grow its team.

Basalt's technology automates the referral and admissions process for post-acute providers, analyzing incoming referrals from faxes, portals and EHR integrations and matching patients to appropriate facilities based on clinical and payer criteria. The company says its platform reduces administrative work and speeds placement decisions that are often delayed by manual processes.

Each year, roughly 14 million patients enter post-acute care—yet the placement process has failed to evolve. The archaic process typically involves case managers faxing lengthy clinical packets to 6.6 facilities per placement, then waiting for the right bed to respond. The resulting delays and mismatches contribute to an estimated $17 billion in avoidable readmission costs annually.

After nearly a decade at Accolade, where he saw administrative work increasingly pull clinicians away from patients, Ben Hackett founded Basalt in 2024 to build technology that fits existing workflows, reduces friction and gives care teams more time to focus on patients.

The startup reports strong early traction with major health systems. Basalt has been live with Lifepoint Health’s team since its seed round in 2025 and the company has reduced median chart processing time from 8.5 minutes to 1.2 minutes. Basalt is also being implemented across 49 additional Lifepoint markets by the end of 2026.

“Case managers are still sending 70-page packets and waiting for responses, while patients remain stranded in hospital beds,” Hackett, founder and CEO of Basalt Health, said. “Basalt turns that uncertainty into fast, defensible decisions. This funding gives us the power to transform one of healthcare’s most stubbornly inefficient systems.”

Basalt plans to expand beyond admissions into discharge planning and payer workflows, positioning its AI platform as infrastructure for managing patient transitions into post-acute care.

Jordan Feldman, Rightway's co-founder and CEO said, "We built our financial model to reward getting members onto high-value drugs, and we embedded clinicians in the workflow and AI on the backend to do it. If we can help employers get more from one of their largest and fastest-growing healthcare expenses, we make healthcare more affordable for companies and the people who work for them."

Rightway will use the Series E financing to expand its AI capabilities and the technology that powers its pharmacy benefits model. "Our technology and AI pull costs out of a bloated ecosystem that has failed to be efficient and transparent. Blair Health, a clinical infrastructure company and virtual provider, plans to expand across Canada and the U.S. It is currently in seven states, available DTC and through employers.

Blair enables generalist providers to deliver specialty care. It is built with medical specialists, encoding their assessment logic, protocols, escalation triggers and follow-up pathways into its software. This then enables Blair’s in-house NPs and family docs to deliver specialty care, with subspecialists providing oversight and support for complex cases.

A patient may come in with complex symptoms that typically take several visits and a waiting list for a specialist to address. But Blair can treat them right away, it says, with the patient completing a structured intake modeled on a specialty clinic assessment. Then, Blair's LLM provides the family doc a recommended treatment plan. The doc reviews, adjusts and prescribes as needed.

“The clinician always makes the final decision; Blair gives them specialist-level structure to do it,” Madge Rumman, Blair co-founder and CEO, told Fierce Healthcare over email.

When a patient needs prescriptions, labs or other in-person services, Blair coordinates with those providers accordingly. Blair currently offers care across menopause, urology, pelvic health, clinical nutrition and weight management.

August 13—Aligned MarketplaceConnecting self-insured employers to primary careSeries: AAmount: $20 millionInvestors: Led by Venrock,

Aligned Marketplace runs a marketplace that connects self-insured employers and third party administrators to independent advanced and direct primary care and specialty care providers. The funding will be used to expand access to independent primary care practices, and the company is expanding its marketplace to include value-based specialty care. With more than 3,000 in-person clinics across all 50 states, Aligned sits atop employers’ existing carrier or third-party administrator.

Aligned Marketplace says it has experienced tremendous growth and cost savings for its employer customers over the past 18 months, having achieved measurable outcomes with large Fortune 500 employers.

In a program launched on January 1, 2025, engaged members at a Fortune 500 company with a national footprint cost 12% less than a risk-matched national benchmark built by a third-party actuarial firm, a savings of $96 per member per month, according to the company. Members at the same company who did not engage with an Aligned doctor came in at benchmark. The company says 70% of engaged members were identified as high risk, and those members moved their care toward their Aligned doctor, averaging 4.7 visits with that doctor and 1.6 fewer visits elsewhere.  More time with a primary care doctor showed up in preventative care, with engaged members roughly doubling their mammogram rate and nearly tripling their colonoscopy rate after their first visit.

“Our mission is to increase America’s healthy years. Unfortunately, the healthcare system was built to treat illness and generate volume, not to keep people healthy, control costs for the employer who pays the bills, or let independent doctors practice the way they want. We’ve proven that a different model works — one where keeping people healthy actually saves money,” said Patrick Nelli, founder and CEO of Aligned Marketplace in a statement. Press release

June 2—NovelliaPatient-controlled data platformSeries: AAmount: $18 millionInvestors:  Spark Capital with participation from Khosla Ventures, Acrew Capital, Bling Capital, and TMV

Novellia is tackling one of pharma’s biggest R&D challenges. Researchers have long depended on real-world data to understand how treatments perform outside clinical trials. But data is still largely stitched together from third-party sources, taking data from insurance claims and hospital records. This leaves gaps in longitudinal records that limit its value in clinical settings.

Novellia works directly with patients to compile and share their records with scientists to advance medical discoveries. The company says it puts this de-identified, anonymized data in the hands of the world's largest pharmaceutical companies and its customers include several of the top 10 pharma and early-stage diagnostics companies.

Along with the new funding, the startup also launched its patient-facing mobile app. Through Novellia’s platform, patients with serious and complex conditions can find and unify up to 20 years of medical data from disparate hospital systems, doctors' offices and labs across the U.S. The company then analyzes those records using proprietary natural language processing models that extract signals from unstructured clinical text, including physician notes, lab narratives and diagnostic reports, turning raw patient data into anonymized, harmonized datasets ready for research. Through Novellia’s platform, patients gain a free, unified view of their health history, while researchers unlock complete, consented, de-identified data for clinical trials and medical breakthroughs, according to the company. Release

May 27—SONDEarbuds that promote better sleepSeries: unlabeledAmount: $7 millionInvestors: E14 Fund (an MIT-affiliated fund), Crosslink Capital, Ubiquity Ventures, Alumni Ventures, Meach Cove Capital, and Boston Scientific co-founder, John Abele

Boston-based startup SOND emerged from stealth with $7 million in funding and debuted its sleep earbuds called Dreambuds. The  in-ear devices can capture 12 physiological signals and uses a cloud-based AI coach to actively intervene and improve sleep in real time.

The starup was founded by a pair of MIT grads, Yadid Ayzenberg, Bose’s former Head of Global Sleep, and Amir Lazarovich, a former senior engineering manager at Google.

The device tracks signals like respiration, heart rate variability, cardiorespiratory coupling, sleep staging, body position, snoring, and seismocardiography (SCG, or the mechanical vibrations of the chest wall produced by the beating heart), as TechCrunch reports.

The sensor data streams in real time to a cloud-based AI sleep coach that then selects a sleep audio program, or generates one on demand, learning over time which ones work best for the individual user. TechCrunch story

Co-founded by Dan Walmsley, chief technology officer, and Scott Hoch, CEO, Gravity Rail says it brings a fundamentally different approach to deploying AI in healthcare that shifts control from outsourced vendors and point solutions to the teams responsible for patient engagement and care coordination. Rather than offering a single AI agent or outsourced service, Gravity Rail provides a model-agnostic operating system for AI-powered engagement, where healthcare teams can build, certify, and run workflows using their own protocols—across phone, SMS, email, web, and messaging channels—without writing code or relying on implementation vendors.

The platform is designed for any healthcare organization or companies working with them—including health plans, provider organizations, contact centers, business processing organizations, digital health companies, and clinical trial platforms—that are responsible for managing patient and member engagement at scale.

Most AI tools are still deployed as isolated solutions that sit outside operational teams, but as workflows change, those systems quickly fall out of sync.

"Payer rules change, protocols evolve, better models emerge, and updates require coordination with an external vendor. AI transformation has to happen inside the organization. We put control in the hands of the people doing the work, turning protocols into live infrastructure they can see, test, and evolve continuously. As models commoditize, the advantage shifts to the organizations that can adapt fastest," Hoch said in a press release.

Organizations are connecting with patients 2x to 3x more often while reducing handle times and improving patient satisfaction. April 20—Cala HealthWearable tremor therapySeries: UnlabeledAmount: $50 millionInvestors: Trinity Capital

Cala Health is a bioelectronic medicine company developing wearable neuromodulation therapies for tremors. It developed and commercialized the Cala kIQ System, an FDA-cleared wearable device designed to help patients manage hand tremors caused by essential tremor and Parkinson's disease.

The device is worn on the wrist and measures a patient's unique tremor pattern and delivers individualized nerve stimulation to counteract tremors through noninvasive electrical stimulation. The therapy is prescribed by physicians for use at home, enabling patients to manage their symptoms outside of a clinical setting and regain the ability to perform everyday activities such as reading, writing, and eating and drinking.

The company plans to accelerate commercial expansion and product innovation, advancing Cala TAPS therapy to impact the lives of essential tremor and Parkinson's disease patients, Cala CEO Deanna Harshbarger said.

Latent started with prior authorization with high provider burnout, high stakes where patients can abandon therapy and complex clinical decision-making. The company is now expanding its engine across every process where clinical knowledge must be translated into action.

With the Series A financing, the company will expand its health system footprint, deepen the platform connecting hospitals, payers, pharmacies, and patients, continue investing in the reliability and trust required to operate in healthcare, and scale the team. Latent is a home for exceptional talent who believe responsibility doesn’t end at diagnosis, it extends to making sure patients actually receive care. Release

March 19—LanternSpecialty care platform for surgery, infusions, and cancer careSeries: UndisclosedAmount: $30 millionInvestors: Morgan Health, a division of JPMorganChase, and Echo Health Ventures, a strategic investment platform investing on behalf of multiple Blues health plans, led the round.

Lantern, a specialty care platform currently serving 12 million people, saw strong growth in 2025, adding more than 100 new clients representing millions of new member lives. Lantern has also added Thermo Fisher Scientific, Amtrak, Hillsborough County Schools, Cleveland Bakers and Teamsters Health & Welfare Fund, and other national employers.

With the $30 million investment, Lantern plans to expand across public and private employers and health plans.

Lantern's approach centers on its "Network of Excellence" model. Rather than relying solely on traditional centers of excellence – which concentrate care within a limited number of facilities – Lantern negotiates directly with high-quality providers to create the most accessible, high-performing network in the country. Lantern's model works across the three largest cost centers in specialty care: surgery, cancer, and infusions, where site of care shifts combined with high-quality specialists demonstrate savings up to $20 per employee per month (PEPM) across Lantern's customers. Today, Lantern's clients include more than 1,000 of the largest public and private employers and unions in the U.S.

Lantern's platform is designed to lower total medical spend, improve surgical outcomes, reduce downstream costs and provide a better experience for members.

We are restoring basic market dynamics in specialty care to bend the healthcare cost curve," said John Zutter, CEO of Lantern. "We leverage robust cost analytics, clinical data on outcomes, appropriateness measures, and dynamic value-based contracting to get people to great care, close to home."

March 19—Health UniverseIntegrating AI into healthcare organizationsSeries: SeedAmount: $6 millionInvestors: Led by Kleiner Perkins, follows a pre-seed funding round with participation from Susa Ventures, Twelve Below and Oncology Ventures.

Health Universe, launched in 2023, is an enterprise artificial intelligence platform that automates healthcare workflows.

Its AI platform deploys inspectable, auditable agents to regulated healthcare settings, including for oncology and clinical trials. It is also building an agent-to-agent marketplace. The company's navigator tool, which manages agents that run patient records, has processed more than 170 million clinical documents since its launch less than a year ago.

"Healthcare doesn't need another chatbot," said Dan Caron, Health Universe founder and CEO, in the announcement. "It needs AI systems that are traceable, compliant, and built for real clinical workflows. Our infrastructure allows organizations to deploy agents that solve high-priority use cases quickly, usually in a matter of days or weeks, not months or years. And we provide the security, inspectability, and auditability that academic medical centers and healthcare stakeholders require. This new funding allows us to scale these capabilities and bring trusted AI agents and models to more institutions and health systems across the country."

March 17—Conduit HealthSimplifying DME procurementSeries: AAmount: $17 millionInvestors: Led by Drive Capital, with participation from XYZ Ventures, Twelve Below, Eniac Ventures & others

Conduit Health, launched in 2024, combines clinical evaluation, insurance authorization and medical supply fulfillment in a single coordinated system. For the equipment a patient needs, Conduit connects them to a licensed provider and manages prescribing, coverage verification and delivery. It specifically focuses on Medicare and Medicaid patients.

Its agentic AI platform is trained on over 50,000 patient interactions to predict approval likelihood before claims are filed and automate payer workflows to prevent bottlenecks. The startup works with over 100 payers and takes on denial risk.

It also works with referral partners that include managed long-term services and supports plans and provider organizations.

"The healthcare system makes it nearly impossible for individuals to obtain the home care equipment they need, covered by their insurance. [Cofounders Natan Wise and Rocky Sefte] identified this acute pain point, and the solution the team built feels like magic for the consumer,” Molly Bonakdarpour, general partner at Drive Capital, said in an announcement. “Conduit is easily engaging the hardest to reach patients in healthcare by simply making their lives easier, and it shows in how fast the company is growing.

The startup's AI gents are already deployed in hundreds of healthcare orgs across acute care, long-term care and home care settings. Its platform manages the entire HR process on behalf of recruiting teams, using intelligent decision-making to escalate sensitive or complex tasks to humans as needed.

As the healthcare industry faces a workforce crisis, HR teams are overwhelmed by high turnover, manual processes and the complexity of staffing 24/7 operations, Carefam executives said.

March 5—Sage AI-powered insights to support senior care Series: CAmount: $65 millionInvestors: Growth Equity at Goldman Sachs Alternatives, with participation from IVP and Goldcrest.

Sage makes an AI-powered platform for senior living and skilled nursing facilities with the goal of surfacing key insights sooner and moving the industry to a more proactive model of care. Sage has raised $124 million to date.

An estimated 72 million Americans will be of retirement age by 2030. But healthcare faces a shortage of providers and licensed caregivers. Labor alone will not fix this looming crisis, Sage argues. Real-time data insights are crucial to empowering caregivers and helping them intervene in care early. Sage-powered communities have identified a $275 increase in net operating income per resident per month, 50% reduction in falls and 50% faster response times compared to the industry, the company claims.

The latest capital will be used to build out a predictive engine that analyzes daily activity patterns and other fall-risk signals to identify high-risk residents before an adverse event happens. Sage is also working to centralize data from EHRs into a single real-time care view to surface important context at the point of care. Additionally, Sage will deepen its capabilities for skilled nursing facilities, which operate under demanding clinical and regulatory conditions.

“We are experiencing a structural shift in the senior care market, necessitating a complete modernization of its underlying technology,” Antoine Munfa, managing director at Growth Equity, said in an announcement.

“By shifting the industry from a reactive to a proactive model, Sage is creating the scalable infrastructure to help meet the historic demand of an aging population,” said Ryan Leary, Vice President within Growth Equity at Goldman Sachs Alternatives. Release

March 4—UnityAIBuilding an autonomous workforce for healthcare operationsSeries: AAmount: $8.5 millionInvestors: Third Prime, with participation from Nashville Capital Network, Whistler Capital Partners, Max Ventures, Company Ventures and other existing investors

Companies are quickly deploying AI agents to tackle isolated tasks in healthcare. Taking a different approach, startup UnityAI is building what it calls autonomous operations, with agentic AI that has broader context and capabilities to optimize and continuously manage day-to-day clinic operations from the patient, provider and administrative sides.

UnityAI works with outpatient and specialty care practices to deploy an autonomous AI workforce designed to run core healthcare operations like patient scheduling, follow-ups, referrals and staffing coordination. Its AI agents unify and coordinate fragmented workflows and processes into a single, continuously operating system. These agents work across patient operations – scheduling and rescheduling, confirmations, follow-ups, and referrals and staffing operations – capacity optimization, shift management, PTO and coverage.

“Crossing the ‘last mile’ – actually engaging a person through voice AI to complete a task – has shifted us from simply optimizing workflows to operating them autonomously,” UnityAI co-founder and CEO Edmund Jackson said in a press release.

The company already is operating at nationwide scale, working with large specialty care groups like Tennessee Oncology and Peregrine Health, as well as national dental service organizations, value-based care providers and community and academic health systems. The company supports more than 300,000 patient interactions per month across hundreds of sites of care.

UnityAI touts strong results and ROI from provider deployment of its AI platform. At one national outpatient provider with more than 300 sites, UnityAI’s agentic workforce helped drive a 26% improvement in scheduler productivity and a 30% reduction in no-show rates.

Other measured impacts delivered by UnityAI across all of its customers include 90% of scheduling tasks completed without staff involvement, less than 6% escalation to human teams, 2x higher patient reach rates compared with traditional workflows, 25% increase in converted referrals and 65% workforce savings in supported operational areas.

UnityAI is integrated with major EHR platforms and is continuing to expand its roadmap into additional areas of operational decision-making. This includes staffing coordination capabilities that are now in beta across approximately 75 sites, in addition to advanced scheduling optimization designed to further improve access and efficiency.

The company will use the funding to scale go-to-market execution and extend its AI-powered operational capabilities. - Release

March 2—Ease HealthRe-architecting the behavioral health technology stackSeries: AAmount: $41 millionInvestors: Andreessen Horowitz

Behavioral health providers operate on fragmented, legacy software technology stacks that were not designed for the complexity of today’s modern care delivery. Admissions teams juggle disconnected CRMs and intake tools, clinicians document in EHRs built off paper workflows, and billing teams rely on manual, error-prone workflows.

Ease was built from the ground up to replace this patchwork with one AI-native system purpose-built for behavioral health.

The company’s tech unifies customer relationship management, electronic health record and revenue cycle management into a single platform designed to materially improve access to care, clinician experience, and provider economics across the behavioral health ecosystem.

Ease designed its platform to span the full patient lifecycle, from referral and intake through clinical care, billing and collections. By consolidating what are traditionally six to 10 separate systems into one, Ease reduces administrative overhead, eliminates redundant workflows, and enables automation across the most cost-intensive areas of behavioral health operations, according to the company.

Ease already supports a wide range of behavioral health levels of care, including outpatient, intensive outpatient (IOP), partial hospitalization (PHP), residential treatment, detox, and inpatient psychiatry, as well as medication-assisted treatment (MAT) across applicable settings. Ease works with both independent providers and large, multi-location provider groups, serving organizations across the United States.

The company’s approach has already demonstrated meaningful impact for customers, including reductions in third-party software spend through consolidation, faster time-to-admission, improved clinical documentation efficiency and materially improved billing throughput and collections performance, executives said.

The new funding will be used to expand Ease’s product and engineering teams, accelerate development of AI-powered automation across the platform and support continued growth with enterprise behavioral health providers.- Release

The company provides comprehensive front office services, from scheduling to prior authorization, through a white-glove implementation model that transforms operations in as little as 12 weeks. Feb. 25—BabaMedicare-covered patient advocacy platformSeries: SeedAmount: $6.5 millionInvestors: General Catalyst, with participation from Genius Ventures, Soma Capital, Ground Up Ventures, Triedge Investments and others

Startup Baba emerged from stealth funded with $6.5 million in seed funding to address a growing gap help older adults navigate care.

The company developed a Medicare- and Medicare Advantage-covered patient advocacy platform that assigns dedicated human advocates to work longitudinally with patients and caregivers, positioning patient advocacy as reimbursable healthcare infrastructure rather than a private-pay add-on.

While healthcare coverage may exist, many older adults still struggle to navigate insurance appeals, coordinate across providers, and manage follow-through after discharge.

To date, Baba has supported more than 6,000 families navigating complex care needs. The company partners with nursing homes, home care agencies and home health organizations nationwide to connect patients with Baba’s dedicated advocates during care transitions. Baba is enrolling in an IRB-approved clinical study with Johns Hopkins to independently evaluate the impact of its advocacy model on health and claims outcomes.

The new capital will support expansion across Medicare and Medicare Advantage populations and deepen partnerships in provider and post-acute settings.

Baba's advocates, who are usually nurses or social workers, manage the operational breakdowns that most often derail care plans, including insurance challenges, scheduling issues and care coordination, while working alongside providers rather than outside the system.

Baba also offers a phone- and text-based AI companion that provides daily engagement, reminders, and early signals when barriers to care arise, allowing advocates to intervene quickly and manage follow-through. Release

Big Health’s latest funding follows the CMS DMHT policy, which created new G-codes in the 2025 Physician Fee Schedule, enabling the first national Medicare coverage for FDA-cleared DMHTs such as SleepioRx and DaylightRx. Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

L'ex ad di Milan e Monza, Adriano Galliani, ha parlato a margine della presentazione del trofeo Galbiati parlando anche della candidatura a presidente della FIGC in contrapposizione con Malagò.

Scegli Fcinter1908.it come tuo sito preferito su Google: clicca qui

Il dirigente ha parlato della decisione di non ricorrere contro l'appoggio del Genoa a Malagò. Il club rossoblù si era espresso a favore di Galliani salvo poi cambiare idea in corsa: "Ho pensato di non fare nessun ricorso perché il calcio ha già i suoi problemi e ha bisogno di serenità. Anche se continuo a essere convinto che sia un vulnus non si possa dare un voto due volte, perché così in qualunque momento un voto può essere tolto all’ultimo minuto e in qualunque momento può diventare una compravendita di voti. I miei cinquant'anni di calcio mi hanno imposto questa scelta. Ottimista? Non voglio essere né fiducioso e né non fiducioso: siamo due candidati e uno vincerà con serenità. Vedremo come andranno le coe».

Al dirigente è stato chiesto anche se confermerà Mancini da ct in caso diventasse lui il numero uno della Federcalcio: «Se vincessi le elezioni FIGC confermerei Mancini come Ct? Ma no, questa roba qui, siccome non sono presidente, ma assolutamente sì in ogni caso, certamente sì. Sono troppe le cose che io penso si debbano fare e le ho scritte nel mio programma elettorale, dovremmo parlare fino a mezzanotte e mezza».

�Varela? È capocannoniere della Serie A, complimenti a chi lo ha scovato perché mi sembra un buonissimo giocatore», ha concluso sull'attaccante del Monza.

Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

As highlighted in our announcement post, we said that each new model generation lets agents do more on their own. However, the more an agent can reach, the more one bad decision can cost. You've probably seen a few of these in the news lately:

Because these events produced no system crashes or logging errors, the teams behind these agents had no way to know until the damage occurred.

Agent Anomaly Detection on the Gemini Platform watches for exactly these issues. It reads the traces and logs your agents already produce and flags sessions where an agent goes outside the job it was built for.

Before you point it at your production agents, you'll probably want to see it work in a test environment. In this hands-on guide, we’ll walk you step-by-step through setting up Agent Anomaly Detection and using it to capture your very first agent behavior anomaly. By the end of this post, you'll have:

To get started, open the companion notebook Detect anomalous agent behavior with Agent Anomaly Detection in a separate tab (available on Google Colab or GitHub). As you read through this guide, it will prompt you when to execute notebook cells or interact directly with the Google Cloud console.

If a step can be done in the UI console, this post shows that path, with screenshots. The notebook does the same thing through the Agent Anomaly Detection API, so by the end you'll also have the code to automate enablement and pull findings into your own tooling.

2. A place to run the notebook: Google Colab, Colab Enterprise, or a local Jupyter environment. The notebook installs the latest ADK for Python itself. Agent Anomaly Detection is compatible with agents running ADK 1.2+, though ADK 2.0+ is preferred for best results.

3. A Cloud Logging bucket and an Observability dataset in either US or EU multi-region. (Both resources need to be in the same multi-region)

4. In the notebook: run the Get started cells. They install the packages, authenticate you, take your project ID, and turn on the four APIs that Agent Anomaly Detection needs.

First, let’s create a Customer Support Agent called “anomaly-detection-refund-demo”. Our example agent is responsible for helping customers check their order history and request refunds. However, it has a deliberate flaw: the agent has access to an overprivileged fetch_records tool which can read any table, including a `payroll` table containing sensitive employee data. All three parts of the lethal trifecta are here: the agent can read private data, it takes input from people it can't vet, and it sends what it finds back in its reply. Tool permissions and guardrails should stop most of this, but they only block what someone thought to write down. Agent Anomaly Detection flags the sessions that get past them, usually within a few minutes.

The notebook has the full agent. The part that matters is the tool, which never checks the table name against an allowlist:

All data is canned and in memory, so nothing real is read or refunded. This tool is intended to be over-permissive to illustrate AAD functionality. Don't copy this tool into production.

The Step 1 cells deploy the agent to Agent Runtime. Two details in the deploy cell are what make the agent visible to Agent Anomaly Detection. First, it sets three environment variables on the deployment, which turn on telemetry and write prompt and response content to Cloud Logging, where detection looks for it:

Second, it sends the agent one test message right after deploying. Agent Anomaly Detection discovers agents through their telemetry, so an agent that has never served a request does not appear under Monitored agents.

The deployment takes about 5 to 10 minutes and prints the agent's resource name and the reply to the test message:

For more information on how to create and deploy an agent, take a look at these guides: Creating an Agent and Deploying an Agent.

In the console: The sample code we provide already enables logging and telemetry when deploying the agent. Verify this by navigating to the Agent Deployments page (Gemini Platform > Agents > Deployments) and clicking “Configure” next to the recently created anomaly-detection-refund-demo agent to open the Observability Service Configuration.

Great! You now have a working agent that is successfully configured and ready to be monitored by Agent Anomaly Detection.

Now let’s set up Agent Anomaly Detection to monitor your agent’s traffic.

In the console: navigate to the Agent Platform > Agents > Audit page and click “Enable”.

Choose your agent’s Logging Bucket and Observability Dataset, making sure they are in the same region (us-multi-region or eu-multi-region) enable “Automatically monitor new agents”, then click “Agree and continue” and Agent Anomaly Detection will be set up in your project.

If you'd rather do this in code, the first Step 2 cell in the notebook creates the same configuration through the API, using the default _Default bucket and _Trace dataset with automatic monitoring on.

Setup takes around 30 minutes. Once it’s done loading you’re ready to proceed to the next step! Because you enabled automatic monitoring and the agent already served a request in Step 1, setup picks it up right away. Check that it shows as Active under Settings > Monitored agents before you continue; sessions that start earlier aren't analyzed.

Now that the agent is being monitored, let's see Agent Anomaly Detection in action! The Step 3 cells send one normal conversation and one anomalous conversation, each in its own session. The normal conversation is a customer listing their orders and getting a refund for a broken lamp. The anomalous conversation simulates a user who finds the fetch_records flaw and uses it to pull payroll data:

Notice that the user brings almost nothing: a customer ID and one first name. Asking for a table that doesn't exist makes the agent reveal the existing tables, which is how they learn there is a payroll table within reach.

The notebook prints both transcripts with the agent's tool calls, and the session IDs you'll look up next.

Anomaly Detection will scan the sessions within 5 minutes. In the landing page, you should see that a couple sessions have been processed and one anomalous session detected.

Let’s take a look at the anomalous session in more detail. Click the session id to open the detail view or paste the anomalous session id into the search bar.

In our run, the top finding was Tool misuse at High severity, with 95% probability. Your detectors and severities may differ, because the models behind the analysis are non-deterministic. The recommended solution is to restrict the `fetch_records` tool to read from specific tables.

Click an invocation in the Session pane to see its analysis in Details. Each invocation is Medium on its own, but together they make the session High.

To stay informed without watching the console, connect Agent Anomaly Detection to your existing alerting workflows.

In Cloud Monitoring: Agent Anomaly Detection publishes two metrics: Detected events, which counts anomalies by detector and severity, and Monitored agents, which tracks your detection coverage. To view these metrics, click the arrow next to "View in Cloud Monitoring" on the Anomaly detection page and select a metric to open it in Metrics Explorer.

To be notified of new anomalies, go to Monitoring > Alerting, click "Create policy", and select the Detected events metric. Leave the filters empty so the alert covers every anomaly, including the demo's, then set the rolling window to 5 minutes with the delta function, and set the threshold to above 0. Once you know what's normal for your agents, add a severity filter (for example High or Critical) so you're only alerted on the findings that matter. A metric-absence policy on Monitored agents will also alert you if monitoring stops.

In Security Command Center: Each anomaly is also published as an AGENT_SESSION_ANOMALY finding in the Security Command Center if it is enabled for your organization. Click "View in Security Command Center" to triage these findings alongside your other threats, use "Set security marks" to tag and track them, and set up notifications for high-severity findings.

You’ve now seen Agent Anomaly Detection in action: it successfully flagged the session where our agent went off-script to read payroll records, while evaluating the benign session, and correctly not flagging an anomaly. The finding told you why it was flagged and how to fix the underlying issue, and alerts in Cloud Monitoring and Security Command Center ensure you are notified of future anomalies.

When you're ready to secure your own production workloads, follow this rollout plan:

To review the complete list of prerequisites or to request Private Preview access for your project, head over to the Agent Anomaly Detection documentation.

TPU Model Design Considerations and Optimization Guide

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Google designed TPUs from the ground up to be exceptionally efficient for machine learning workloads, delivering top-tier performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). Because TPUs trade general-purpose hardware scheduling for wide systolic arrays, dedicated SparseCore engines, and custom interconnect fabrics, adhering to specific architectural best practices is essential to achieve maximum performance. To help developers get the most out of TPUs, we are launching the open-source AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign guide. This post highlights its key principles—spanning shape alignment, architectural choices like LatentMoE, and parallelism strategies—to accelerate LLMs, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models, and other hardware-intensive workloads on TPUs.

Performance tuning starts with model definition. Architectural choices made before the first training step set permanent ceilings on MFU (Model FLOP Utilization), cost, and serving latency. This guide gives concrete, hardware-grounded design rules for teams building or adapting models for TPUs, organized into three groups:

How to use this guide. The shape rules apply to anyone designing a new model. If you are adapting an existing model rather than designing one, the Expressing Parallelism section applies without touching the checkpoint. The general rules hold for any large-matmul architecture (dense LLMs, multimodal, and world models), and the companion AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign repository grows model- and workload-specific sections over time.

Eighth-generation TPUs. These principles carry forward to TPU 8t and TPU 8i and to the newly announced TPU 8tx and TPU 8i Max. The reasoning stays the same and the thresholds move with the hardware. TPU 8t and TPU 8i both add native FP4. For training, TPU 8t also brings more balanced Vector Processing Unit (VPU) scaling. For serving, TPU 8i adds the Boardfly topology, a Collectives Acceleration Engine (CAE), and 3x more on-chip SRAM. We note the effects inline in Principles 5–8, and follow-up posts will cover eighth-generation designs in depth.

Measured evidence. Alongside the published sources, notes marked Measured carry results from our own Ironwood profiling program: open 20–30B MoEs (a Qwen3-30B-derived LatentMoE variant; GPT-OSS-20B) plus dense controls, single slice (except for the 512-chip TPU v7x and 1,024-chip TPU v4 ablations in Principle 4), BF16, training shapes up to 32K sequence, all against tuned-systems baselines. Treat the trends and rankings as transferable, the exact percentages as config-specific.

TPUs are purpose-built custom ML ASICs designed from the ground up to deliver state-of-the-art performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). While their architectural choices—such as wide 256×256 systolic arrays, software-managed VMEM, dedicated SparseCore engines, and direct multi-dimensional ICI fabrics—enable exceptional efficiency, they inherently create specific structural constraints and programming patterns that must be carefully followed to unlock maximal performance. Reaching peak efficiency requires aligning model shapes, routing, and parallelism directly with these underlying hardware characteristics:

Make d_model, FFN and expert intermediate widths (d_ff), latent widths, attention projections, and padded vocabulary sizes (used as contracting or output dimensions) multiples of 256 to match the 256×256 MXU systolic array. Token/batch axes and counts such as heads, layers, and experts are exempt. The most frequently missed dimension is head_dim: at 64 or 128, flash-attention QK products leave the MXU ≥ 50% idle. Reshape to head_dim = 256 with proportionally fewer Q and KV heads, preserving total projection widths and logical KV cache bytes (iso-quality in our ablations).

Measured: Reshaping Qwen3-30B MoE attention from 32 × 128 to 16 × 256 (identical parameters and FLOPs) sped up end-to-end training, with gains widening at longer context:

These rules apply for non-quadratic attention as well, notably GDN ablations from Yang et al. 2025 also noted at par or better quality for head dimension 256 for their Gated DeltaNet Attention.

XLA compiles each program for exact tensor shapes, so every buffer has a fixed address and every loop a known trip count, and a new shape means a recompilation and a host stall. What about dynamic shapes? Dynamic workloads fit this model: the trick is to separate the shape of a buffer from the extent of the data inside it.

Instead of padding to the largest group or the longest sequence, we keep the buffers at fixed shapes (tokens sorted by expert and padded only at the tail, the KV cache as a fixed pool of pages) and describe the ragged structure with a few small integer arrays (group sizes and offsets; KV lengths, the page table, cumulative query lengths). Pallas copies these into the TensorCore's scalar memory before the grid starts (PrefetchScalarGridSpec), and the kernel reads them to choose the next tile and bound its loops, with a mask covering a tile that straddles a group boundary or a partly filled last page. The data dependence becomes an address and a trip count instead of a shape, so the compiled program is the same for every routing and every batch composition and the kernel runs at the unpadded FLOP count.

Standard EP dispatches every routed token at full d_model width, making EP + FSDP slower than plain FSDP in our Ironwood benchmarks. LatentMoE projects routed tokens from d_model down to latent width ℓ (d/ℓ ≈ 2–4×) before dispatch, executes experts in latent space, and projects back up after combine (introduced by Nemotron 3 and adopted by 962B MAI-Base-1 and 2.8T Kimi K3):

Measured: On a 30B / 128-expert MoE, an ℓ = 768 (2.67×) LatentMoE cut on-device permute time 2.8×, lifted end-to-end training throughput +14% tokens/s (322 vs 270 TFLOP/s/chip, or +19%, since the latent projections add ~5% FLOPs per token; tokens stay ℓ-wide past the down-projection, so on-device permute and combine shrink alongside EP all-to-all), and freed ~25 GB/device of activation HBM when remat checkpoints were anchored on the compressed latent. While current MoE recipes in tpu-recipes run FSDP (+ TP), LatentMoE + FP8 dispatch makes EP roofline-viable when per-device batch is too small to hide FSDP weight all-gathers. Under both FSDP and EP, avoid over-fragmenting experts: small tokens × K / N group sizes starve MXU tiles.

When candidate MoE layouts match on quality, pick the one with higher compute intensity at a similar active-parameter budget. A larger shared expert (wider d_shared) shifts work into dense, communication-free GEMMs that overlap EP dispatch/combine in training and amortize weight reads across the decode batch. Fewer, wider routed experts (halving N and K while doubling d_ff at fixed sparsity K/N) cut dispatch traffic in half and double per-expert GEMM fill. Optimal (N, K) grows with model size, and over-coarsening hurts routing diversity. Ablate expert granularity and the shared-to-routed split at your target scale rather than copying GPU co-designed models, since the TPU optimum is more shared-heavy.

Measured: On a 63B LatentMoE (ℓ = 768, K/N = 1/32, 8K context, 512 TPU v7x chips at EP = 128), halving (N, K) from (512, 16) to (256, 8) while doubling d_ff and the shared expert lifted training throughput +57% tok/s and held lower loss across 120.8B tokens:

Low-concurrency decode streams model weights every token (~300 concurrent tokens per weight shard are needed in dense FP8 to reach compute roofline). Combine two architectural levers with explicit concurrency sizing:

Norms, activations, masking, routing math and quantization scale applies run on the Vector Processing Unit (VPU), which is roughly two orders of magnitude below the MXU in throughput and executes in sequence with the matmuls unless XLA fuses it into an adjacent GEMM or we move it off the TensorCore. Therefore we drop reshapes and transposes that carry no quality, standardize on fusable RMSNorm and SwiGLU so the elementwise work folds into the neighbouring GEMM, and offload the MoE sort, gather and scatter to the SparseCore.

Note that fused VPU work is free only while its cycle count fits inside the MXU window it is fused into. A quick illustration of this concept is block-scaled fp8 GEMM.

With a scale per block of B elements along the contraction axis, the MXU emits one partial sum per output element per block and the VPU rescales each one (about three vector ops), so per output element the MXU does 2K flops and the VPU does 3·ceil(K/B) ops. The rescale hides when B ≥ 1.5 × r_mxu / r_vpu, which on v7x (fp8 peak 4,614 TFLOPS against a 19.2 TFLOPS vector peak, a ratio near 240) puts the threshold near 340. In simulation on a DeepSeek-V3 expert shape, block 512 runs at the per-tensor time, block 256 is VPU-bound at 1.2x and block 128 at 2.4x. Achieved MXU efficiency is lower than peak, which lowers the effective ratio and can make the smaller blocks workable; the way to decide is to measure the GEMM at each block instead of using peak ratio. This threshold is specific to Ironwood. TPU 8t is designed with specialized support for scaling so quantization for MXFP4 and MXFP8 formats has no overhead. Re-measure the block-size threshold there instead of reusing 340. The rule itself carries forward: keep vector work hidden inside a matmul window.

For workloads dominated by BF16-sensitive matmuls (most notably long-context quadratic attention where direct FP8 degrades accuracy), native single-pass BF16 leaves MXU capacity unused whenever hardware low-precision MXU throughput exceeds native BF16 throughput by more than the pass count. Use the multi-pass BF16 algorithm to execute BF16-accurate matmuls via multiple passes over the higher-throughput lower-precision MXU pipeline without losing numerical fidelity. This matters more as low-precision throughput pulls ahead of BF16. TPU 8t adds native FP4, which doubles MXU throughput. On Ironwood the FP8 to BF16 ratio is exactly 2:1, so two or more passes cannot beat native BF16.

Assign communication-heavy parallel axes to the fastest physical links and keep logical mesh factors aligned with torus dimensions:

Minimum-degree CP matched to kernel trade-offs: All-gather CP is simplest (though its all-gathers can sit exposed) when paired with zigzag load balancing (context_parallel_load_balance in MaxText) so causal masking does not idle tail ranks; Ring CP overlaps collectives but trades off SplashAttention chunk efficiency; Ulysses A2A CP avoids causal skew but exposes all-to-alls and caps at head count.

Start every project with the quality-safe foundation: 256-aligned dimensions (1), static shapes with dropless routing (2), one parallelism plan mapped to physical links (8), wall-clock ablations on a tuned stack, and published reference targets (both in Measuring what matters below). For MoEs, add LatentMoE (3) after an ablation to cut routing/dispatch traffic and decode weight reads. Then prioritize by bottleneck regime:

Google engineered TPUs from the ground up as custom ML ASICs to deliver top-tier performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). Unlocking that potential requires bridging the gap between hardware architecture and model design. By standardizing on 256-aligned shapes, leveraging LatentMoE, and mapping parallel axes directly to physical links, engineering teams can maximize TPU efficiency across both training and serving workloads.

A reference blueprint is only as good as the loop used to validate it. Always evaluate architectural changes in wall-clock time on a tuned software stack rather than paper FLOPs: in our Ironwood benchmarks, pure systems tuning (SparseCore offload, MXU-aligned tiles, and remat tuning) lifted an open 30B MoE from 12% to 23% MFU before any model change, and published Ironwood baselines show 256-aligned MoEs (DeepSeek-V3, Qwen3-235B at 608–630 BF16 TFLOP/s/chip) running at roughly 2× the throughput of unaligned GPT-OSS-120B (330 TFLOP/s/chip). For the 6-signal XProf (profiler=xplane) triage checklist, roofline and HBM budgeting rules, wall-clock ablation methodology, published Ironwood reference targets, and ongoing updates as new TPU generations and kernels land, explore the living diagnostics guide and companion patterns in the open-source AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign repository.
